MA0902 · TUM · Study guide

Analysis für InformatikA chapter-by-chapter guide to the lecture notes

One page per chapter of the lecture script by Silke Rolles. Each page explains the chapter as one connected argument, works the matching exercise sheets into the text, and ends with the exam questions that chapter has produced in past exams, plus a short self-test.

Source labels on every box: Skript 2.3 = the lecture script · Blatt 2 · 2.2 = exercise sheet · Klausur WiSe20 = old exam · Extra = my own addition (intuition, a missing proof, an extra example).

→How the course fits together

The course is one long chain. Each chapter uses the tools of the previous ones, so it pays to read them in order:

  • Foundations (1–3). ℝ has no holes (supremum axiom) → so monotone bounded sequences converge → so we can add up infinitely many numbers (series) and define \(e^x\).
  • Functions (4–5). Continuity is defined through sequences. It gives the intermediate value theorem and the existence of max/min. Then ln and general powers come from inverting exp, and sin/cos are defined through \(e^{ix}\).
  • Differential calculus (6–7). The derivative is the best linear approximation (with Landau symbols). The mean value theorem turns derivative information into statements about the function: monotonicity, extrema, l’Hospital.
  • Integral calculus (8–9). The integral is a limit of sums; the fundamental theorem links it to derivatives. Then improper integrals and the comparison between sums and integrals.
  • Power series (10). Taylor polynomials approximate functions to higher order; power series are "infinite polynomials".
  • Several variables and ODEs (11–12). Gradient and Hessian generalise f′ and f″; differential equations use everything from integration.

§Chapters

!What the exams ask

I sorted every task in the nine uploaded exams (WiSe18 to WiSe24) by topic. A dot means the topic appears in that exam. This shows what has been asked before; it does not predict the next exam.

Reading the table: series convergence, integration and computing limits appear in practically every exam. Gradient/Hessian extrema and ODEs appear in every recent Rolles/Gantert exam. Sup/inf of a set is a common warm-up task.

Based on the lecture notes Analysis für Informatik (MA0902), Silke Rolles, TUM (version of 3 Feb 2026), the Aufgabenblätter 1–14 with Lösungshinweise (WiSe 2025/26), and the exams WiSe18–WiSe24. Personal study guide, not official course material.
Analysis für Informatik · MA0902 · Before Chapter 1

Basics: sets, maps, fieldsGrundlagen: Mengen, Abbildungen, Körper

The script starts with the sentence "\((\mathbb R,+,\cdot)\) ist ein kommutativer Körper" and moves on. This page explains everything that sentence and the first chapters take for granted: the language of sets, the basic proof techniques, maps (injective, surjective, bijective), the algebraic structures group, ring and field, ordered fields, and the algebra of powers and roots with the domains each rule needs. Only a handful of exam items ask about this material directly (they are listed at the end of B.3); everything else in the course uses it silently. The last section is a German–English list for reading the exam paper itself.

Source labels: Skript §1.1 where the script uses the idea · Extra background the script assumes (everything on this page is background, so most boxes carry this label).

0Why this page, and how to use it

Analysis builds numbers up from a handful of rules. To follow that, you need to know what a "rule for numbers" is — that is what fields and ordered fields are — and how mathematicians argue with such rules — that is what proofs and quantifiers are. If you already know these topics from Diskrete Strukturen or Lineare Algebra, skim the summary at the end and do the self-test. If not, read the page top to bottom once; it takes about an hour.

Topic on this pageWhere the course needs it
Sets, \(\in,\subseteq,\cup,\cap,\times\), set-builder notationevery page — e.g. \(\{x\in\mathbb R: x^2>1\}\) in the sup/inf exam tasks
Implication, contrapositive, contradiction, inductionevery proof; contradiction for \(\sqrt2\notin\mathbb Q\), Cantor, Archimedes (Ch. 1)
Injective / surjective / bijective, inverse mapcountability (Ch. 1), inverse functions ln, arcsin (Ch. 5, 6)
Field axioms and what follows from them"ℝ is a field" (Ch. 1); every algebraic manipulation you ever do
Ordered field and the rules for inequalities"ℂ cannot be ordered" (Ch. 1); every ε-estimate from Ch. 2 on
Powers and roots: the five rules, \(\sqrt{x^2}=|x|\), fractional and negative exponentsroot limits and the conjugate trick (Ch. 2); \(x^a:=e^{a\ln x}\) (Ch. 5); differentiating \(x^x\) (Ch. 6, 7); every integral of \(x^a\) (Ch. 8)

B.1Sets and notation [Mengen]

Intuition. A set is a collection of objects, called its elements. Order and repetition do not matter: \(\{1,2,3\}=\{3,1,2\}=\{1,1,2,3\}\). A set is completely determined by which elements it contains.

Notation you must be able to readExtra
symbolread asexample
\(a\in A\), \(a\notin A\)\(a\) is (not) an element of \(A\)\(2\in\mathbb N\), \(-1\notin\mathbb N\)
\(A\subseteq B\)\(A\) is a subset of \(B\): every element of \(A\) is in \(B\)\(\mathbb N\subseteq\mathbb Z\)
\(A\subsetneq B\)proper subset: \(A\subseteq B\) and \(A\neq B\)\(\mathbb Q\subsetneq\mathbb R\)
\(\emptyset\)the empty set [leere Menge]\(\{x\in\mathbb R:x^2<0\}=\emptyset\)
\(A\cup B\)union [Vereinigung]: in \(A\) or in \(B\) (or both)\(\{1,2\}\cup\{2,3\}=\{1,2,3\}\)
\(A\cap B\)intersection [Schnitt]: in \(A\) and in \(B\)\(\{1,2\}\cap\{2,3\}=\{2\}\)
\(A\setminus B\)difference: in \(A\) but not in \(B\)\(\mathbb R\setminus\{0\}\) = all nonzero reals
\(A\times B\)Cartesian product: all pairs \((a,b)\) with \(a\in A,\ b\in B\)\(\mathbb R\times\mathbb R=\mathbb R^2\) (the plane)
\(\{x\in A:\ \text{condition}\}\)"all \(x\) in \(A\) such that …" (set-builder notation)\(\{x\in\mathbb R:|x-1|<1\}=(0,2)\)
\(\{f(n):n\in\mathbb N\}\)the set of all values \(f(1),f(2),\dots\)\(\{\frac1n:n\in\mathbb N\}=\{1,\frac12,\frac13,\dots\}\)

Four things in these examples get their proper definition in Chapter 1 and are used here only as familiar notation: the number sets \(\mathbb N=\{1,2,3,\dots\}\) — in this course without 0 — and \(\mathbb Z,\mathbb Q,\mathbb R\) (§1.1); intervals such as \((0,2)=\{x:0<x<2\}\) (§1.2); the absolute value \(|x|\), the distance from \(x\) to 0 (§1.3); and countability, which is what maps are used for there.

How to prove two sets are equalExtra

Show both inclusions: \(A\subseteq B\) (take any \(x\in A\), show \(x\in B\)) and \(B\subseteq A\). This "⊆ and ⊇" pattern appears in the script, e.g. in Lemma 12.6(c).

Example: \(\{x\in\mathbb R:|x-1|<1\}=(0,2)\). "⊆": if \(|x-1|<1\), then \(-1<x-1<1\), so \(0<x<2\). "⊇": if \(0<x<2\), then \(-1<x-1<1\), so \(|x-1|<1\). This exact set is an exam question (SoSe19 A1c, worked in Chapter 1).

Reading sums and products: \(\sum\) and \(\prod\) Extra

NotationExtra

\[\sum_{k=m}^{n}a_k=a_m+a_{m+1}+\dots+a_n,\qquad \prod_{k=m}^{n}a_k=a_m\cdot a_{m+1}\cdots a_n.\] \(k\) is a dummy index: its name carries no meaning, \(\sum_{k=1}^3a_k=\sum_{i=1}^3a_i\). Conventions: an empty sum (\(n<m\)) is \(0\), an empty product is \(1\) — this is why \(0!=\prod_{k=1}^{0}k=1\).

Examples: \(\sum_{k=1}^nk=\frac{n(n+1)}2\); \(n!=\prod_{k=1}^nk\); \(\binom nk=\frac{n!}{k!(n-k)!}\); the series of Chapter 3 are sums with \(n\to\infty\).

The four manipulations used all the timeExtra
  1. Linearity: \(\sum_k(\alpha a_k+\beta b_k)=\alpha\sum_ka_k+\beta\sum_kb_k\). Constants come out, sums split — but only term by term.
  2. Splitting off a term: \(\sum_{k=0}^{n}a_k=a_0+\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_k=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}a_k+a_n\). Used whenever a formula starts at a different index than your sum (Chapter 3 does this constantly).
  3. Index shift [Indexverschiebung]: substituting \(j=k-1\) shifts both limits, \(\sum_{k=1}^{n}a_k=\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}a_{j+1}\). It is the discrete version of a substitution in an integral (§3.1, and in every power-series computation in Chapter 10).
  4. Telescoping: if \(a_k=b_k-b_{k+1}\), almost everything cancels: \(\sum_{k=m}^{n}a_k=b_m-b_{n+1}\) (§3.1).

Warning: \(\sum_k a_kb_k\ne\left(\sum_ka_k\right)\left(\sum_kb_k\right)\). Multiplying two sums produces all pairs \(a_ib_j\); sorting them by \(i+j\) is exactly the Cauchy product of §3.3.

B.2Logic and proof techniques [Beweistechniken]

Implication and equivalenceExtra
  • \(A\Rightarrow B\) ("\(A\) implies \(B\)", "if \(A\) then \(B\)") is false only when \(A\) is true and \(B\) false. It says nothing when \(A\) is false.
  • \(A\Leftrightarrow B\) ("\(A\) if and only if \(B\)", "iff", [genau dann, wenn]): both \(A\Rightarrow B\) and \(B\Rightarrow A\).
  • \(B\) is necessary for \(A\) [notwendig] if \(A\Rightarrow B\); \(A\) is sufficient for \(B\) [hinreichend]. Example (Ch. 3): "\(a_k\to0\)" is necessary for \(\sum a_k\) to converge, but not sufficient.
The most common logic mistake

\(A\Rightarrow B\) does not give \(B\Rightarrow A\). "Differentiable ⇒ continuous" is true (Ch. 6); "continuous ⇒ differentiable" is false (\(|x|\) at 0). What is equivalent to \(A\Rightarrow B\) is the contrapositive [Kontraposition] \(\neg B\Rightarrow\neg A\): "not continuous ⇒ not differentiable".

Four proof techniques cover almost every proof in the course:

techniqueto prove \(A\Rightarrow B\) you …example in the course
directassume \(A\), derive \(B\) step by steptriangle inequality (Satz 1.12)
contrapositiveassume "not \(B\)", derive "not \(A\)""if \(a_k\not\to0\), the series diverges" (Lemma 3.4)
contradiction [Widerspruch]assume \(A\) and "not \(B\)", derive something impossible\(\sqrt2\notin\mathbb Q\), Cantor, Archimedes (Ch. 1)
induction [Induktion]for statements about all \(n\in\mathbb N\): base case + step \(n\to n+1\)Bernoulli's inequality (Satz 2.18) — recipe in Ch. 2
One small proof of each kindExtra
  • Direct. Claim: if \(a\) and \(b\) are even, so is \(a+b\). Proof: even means \(a=2m\), \(b=2k\) with \(m,k\in\mathbb Z\). Then \(a+b=2(m+k)\), and \(m+k\in\mathbb Z\), so \(a+b\) is even. ∎
  • Contrapositive. Claim: if \(n^2\) is even, then \(n\) is even. A direct attack would have to take a square root; the contrapositive is easy. Proof: assume \(n\) is odd, \(n=2m+1\). Then \(n^2=4m^2+4m+1=2(2m^2+2m)+1\) is odd. That is "not \(B\) ⇒ not \(A\)", which is the same statement. ∎ (This is the step the script needs inside the \(\sqrt2\notin\mathbb Q\) proof, §1.1.)
  • Contradiction. Claim: there is no smallest positive real number. Proof: suppose \(x>0\) were the smallest. Then \(\frac x2\) is again positive and \(\frac x2<x\) — a positive number smaller than the smallest one. Contradiction, so no such \(x\) exists. ∎

Notice the shape: a proof starts by writing down what the assumption means (a definition unpacked into a formula) and ends with what the claim means. Most exam proofs in this course are three to six such lines.

Quantifiers (\(\forall\), \(\exists\)) and how to negate them are explained at the start of Chapter 1 (§1.1 · Quantifiers); induction is explained with Bernoulli's inequality in Chapter 2. "o.B.d.A." [ohne Beschränkung der Allgemeinheit] = "without loss of generality": the other case works the same way.

B.3Maps [Abbildungen, Funktionen]

Intuition. A map \(f:A\to B\) is a rule that assigns to every element \(a\) of \(A\) exactly one element \(f(a)\) of \(B\). Picture arrows: every element of \(A\) sends out exactly one arrow into \(B\).

DefinitionsExtra · used in Skript 1.2, 5.1
  • \(A\) is the domain [Definitionsbereich], \(B\) the codomain [Zielmenge], and \(f(A)=\{f(a):a\in A\}\) the image [Bild].
  • \(f\) is injective [injektiv] if different inputs give different outputs: \(f(a_1)=f(a_2)\Rightarrow a_1=a_2\). No two arrows land on the same point.
  • \(f\) is surjective [surjektiv] if every \(b\in B\) is hit: \(\forall b\in B\ \exists a\in A: f(a)=b\). Every point of \(B\) receives at least one arrow.
  • \(f\) is bijective [bijektiv] if it is both. Every point of \(B\) receives exactly one arrow. Then the arrows can be reversed: the inverse map \(f^{-1}:B\to A\) sends \(b\) to the unique \(a\) with \(f(a)=b\).
  • The composition [Verkettung] of \(g:A\to B\) and \(f:B\to C\) is \(f\circ g:A\to C\), \(x\mapsto f(g(x))\) — "first \(g\), then \(f\)". The identity \(\operatorname{id}_A(x)=x\) satisfies \(f^{-1}\circ f=\operatorname{id}_A\) and \(f\circ f^{-1}=\operatorname{id}_B\) for bijective \(f\) — this is Satz 5.3 in the script.
  • The preimage [Urbild] of a set \(C\subseteq B\) is \(f^{-1}(C)=\{a\in A:f(a)\in C\}\). It exists for every map — \(f\) need not be bijective, and \(f^{-1}(C)\) may be empty or large. Do not confuse this set with the inverse map \(f^{-1}\), which only exists when \(f\) is bijective. Example: for \(f(x)=x^2\), \(f^{-1}(\{4\})=\{-2,2\}\).
  • The restriction [Einschränkung] \(f|_{A_0}\) is the same rule with the smaller domain \(A_0\subseteq A\). This is how Chapter 5 makes \(\sin\) invertible: \(\sin\) on all of \(\mathbb R\) is not injective, but \(\sin|_{[-\pi/2,\pi/2]}\) is, and its inverse is \(\arcsin\) (§5.3).
Try it · injective, surjective, bijective

Every element on the left must send exactly one arrow. Click a left element, then click a right element to (re)direct its arrow. Change the sizes of the two sets and watch what becomes possible.

Examples — the codomain mattersSkript Bsp. 5.2
  • \(f:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R,\ x\mapsto x^2\): not injective (\(f(-1)=f(1)\)), not surjective (\(-1\) is never hit).
  • \(f:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb R,\ x\mapsto x^2\): injective, still not surjective.
  • \(f:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty),\ x\mapsto x^2\): bijective, inverse \(y\mapsto\sqrt y\).

Same formula, three different maps: whether a map is injective/surjective depends on the chosen domain and codomain, not just on the formula. For finite sets you can read off: injective needs \(|A|\le|B|\), surjective needs \(|A|\ge|B|\), bijective needs \(|A|=|B|\) — try it in the widget.

Where this page is asked directlyWiSe23 6a · WiSe19 1i · SoSe19 A1c
  • WiSe23 6a: is \(f:[0,\infty)\to\mathbb N_0,\ x\mapsto\lfloor x\rfloor\) injective, surjective, bijective? Answer each with a reason: one counterexample kills injectivity (\(f(6.2)=f(6.3)=6\)), and one preimage per target proves surjectivity (\(f(n)=n\)). Worked in Chapter 5.
  • WiSe19 1i: draw the graph of the inverse function — mirror at the line \(y=x\) (§5.1).
  • SoSe19 A1c: describing a set given by an inequality, then reading off sup/inf (Chapter 1).
  • The field and order rules themselves are never asked on their own, with one exception: "ℂ cannot be ordered" (Lemma 1.4) is a standard short item — see Chapter 1.

B.4Groups, rings and fields [Gruppen, Ringe, Körper]

Why this matters. When you simplify \(\frac{2x+6}{2}=x+3\) or move a term to the other side of an equation, you use rules like "you may divide by any nonzero number". A field is exactly a set with \(+\) and \(\cdot\) in which all the familiar rules of school algebra hold. Saying "ℝ is a field" is the script's way of saying "all of that works in ℝ". Rings and groups are the same idea with fewer rules — useful to see which rule fails where.

All three are built from binary operations [Verknüpfungen]: rules that combine two elements of a set \(M\) into a third element of \(M\), like \(+\) on \(\mathbb Z\).

Group [Gruppe]

DefinitionExtra

A set \(M\) with one operation \(\ast\) is a group if:

  1. (G1) associative [assoziativ]: \((a\ast b)\ast c=a\ast(b\ast c)\) — brackets don't matter;
  2. (G2) neutral element [neutrales Element]: there is an \(e\) with \(e\ast a=a\ast e=a\) for all \(a\);
  3. (G3) inverses [inverses Element]: for every \(a\) there is an \(a'\) with \(a\ast a'=a'\ast a=e\).

If also (G4) commutative [kommutativ]: \(a\ast b=b\ast a\), the group is abelian [abelsch].

Examples: \((\mathbb Z,+)\) with \(e=0\), inverse \(-a\). \((\mathbb Q\setminus\{0\},\cdot)\) with \(e=1\), inverse \(\frac1a\). Not groups: \((\mathbb N,+)\) — no \(0\), no negatives; \((\mathbb Z,\cdot)\) — \(2\) has no inverse in \(\mathbb Z\) since \(\frac12\notin\mathbb Z\).

Ring [Ring]

DefinitionExtra

A set \(R\) with two operations \(+\) and \(\cdot\) is a ring if

  1. \((R,+)\) is an abelian group (neutral element called \(0\), inverse of \(a\) called \(-a\));
  2. \(\cdot\) is associative, and there is a \(1\) with \(1\cdot a=a\cdot1=a\);
  3. distributive laws [Distributivgesetze]: \(a\cdot(b+c)=a\cdot b+a\cdot c\) and \((a+b)\cdot c=a\cdot c+b\cdot c\).

If \(a\cdot b=b\cdot a\) always, it is a commutative ring. In a ring you can add, subtract and multiply — but not necessarily divide.

Examples: \(\mathbb Z\) (the model example); polynomials \(\mathbb R[x]\); \(\mathbb Z/n\) (arithmetic "mod \(n\)", see the widget); \(2\times2\) matrices — a ring that is not commutative, since in general \(AB\neq BA\).

Field [Körper]

DefinitionExtra · the notion used in Skript §1.1

A field is a commutative ring \(K\) with \(0\neq1\) in which every nonzero element has a multiplicative inverse: for every \(a\neq0\) there is \(a^{-1}\) with \(a\cdot a^{-1}=1\). Equivalently: \((K,+)\) and \((K\setminus\{0\},\cdot)\) are abelian groups, linked by the distributive law.

In words: a field is a set where you can add, subtract, multiply, and divide by anything except 0, with all the usual rules. Division is defined as \(\frac ab=a\cdot b^{-1}\).

\((+)\) group\(\cdot\) commutativeinverses for \(\cdot\)structure
\(\mathbb N\)✗ (no 0, no \(-a\))✓✗none of the three
\(\mathbb Z\)✓✓✗ (\(\frac12\notin\mathbb Z\))commutative ring
\(\mathbb Q,\ \mathbb R,\ \mathbb C\)✓✓✓fields
\(\mathbb Z/p\), \(p\) prime✓✓✓field (finite!)
\(\mathbb Z/n\), \(n\) not prime✓✓✗ (in \(\mathbb Z/6\): no \(2^{-1}\), because \(2\cdot3=0\))commutative ring
\(2\times2\) matrices✓✗✗non-commutative ring
polynomials \(\mathbb R[x]\)✓✓✗ (\(\frac1x\) is no polynomial)commutative ring
Try it · when is arithmetic mod n a field?

\(\mathbb Z/n=\{0,1,\dots,n-1\}\) with "add or multiply, then take the remainder mod \(n\)" — the arithmetic of a clock with \(n\) hours. It is always a commutative ring. Look at the multiplication table: a field needs a 1 in every row except row 0 (that 1 marks the inverse). Cells highlighted red are zero divisors: two nonzero numbers whose product is 0.

What follows from the field axioms

Everything you do in algebra follows from the axioms. Here are the rules the course uses silently, each with its two-line proof — they show how "obvious" rules are actually consequences of the axioms.

Rules in every fieldExtra
  1. \(a\cdot0=0\).
  2. \((-a)\cdot b=-(a\cdot b)\), and in particular \((-1)\cdot(-1)=1\).
  3. No zero divisors [nullteilerfrei]: \(a\cdot b=0\Rightarrow a=0\) or \(b=0\).
  4. \(a\cdot x=b\) with \(a\neq0\) has exactly one solution, \(x=a^{-1}b\).
Proofs
  1. \(a\cdot0=a\cdot(0+0)=a\cdot0+a\cdot0\) (neutral element, then distributivity). Add \(-(a\cdot0)\) to both sides: \(0=a\cdot0\).
  2. \(a\cdot b+(-a)\cdot b=(a+(-a))\cdot b=0\cdot b=0\) by rule 1. So \((-a)b\) is the additive inverse of \(ab\): \((-a)b=-(ab)\). With \(a=1,\ b=-1\): \((-1)(-1)=-(1\cdot(-1))=-(-1)=1\).
  3. Suppose \(ab=0\) and \(a\neq0\). Multiply by \(a^{-1}\): \(b=a^{-1}ab=a^{-1}\cdot0=0\).
  4. \(x=a^{-1}b\) works: \(a\cdot a^{-1}b=b\). If \(x\) and \(y\) both work, \(a(x-y)=0\), so \(x-y=0\) by rule 3.

Rule 3 is what you use every time you solve \((x-2)(x+3)=0\) by "one of the factors must be 0". In \(\mathbb Z/6\) it fails (\(2\cdot3=0\)), and so does that way of solving equations — see the widget.

B.5Ordered fields [angeordnete Körper]

Intuition. A field says how to calculate. An ordering adds the notion of "positive", and with it \(<\). Analysis lives on inequalities (\(|a_n-a|<\varepsilon\)), so this is the second half of what makes ℝ suitable.

DefinitionSkript §1.1 · Anordnung

A field \(K\) is ordered if there is a notion "\(a>0\)" (positive) with

  1. for every \(a\): exactly one of \(a=0\), \(a>0\), \(-a>0\);
  2. \(a>0,\ b>0\ \Rightarrow\ a+b>0\) and \(ab>0\).

Then \(a>b\) is defined as \(a-b>0\).

The inequality rules (all follow from (a) and (b))Extra
  1. Adding keeps the direction: \(a<b\Rightarrow a+c<b+c\).
  2. Multiplying by a positive keeps it: \(a<b,\ c>0\Rightarrow ac<bc\).
  3. Multiplying by a negative flips it: \(a<b,\ c<0\Rightarrow ac>bc\).
  4. Squares: \(a^2>0\) for \(a\neq0\); in particular \(1=1^2>0\).
  5. Reciprocals flip: \(0<a<b\Rightarrow\frac1b<\frac1a\).
  6. Transitivity: \(a<b,\ b<c\Rightarrow a<c\).
Proofs
  1. \((b+c)-(a+c)=b-a>0\).
  2. \(bc-ac=(b-a)c\), a product of two positives, so \(>0\) by (b).
  3. \(-c>0\), so by 2: \(a(-c)<b(-c)\), i.e. \(-ac<-bc\); add \(ac+bc\) to both sides (rule 1): \(bc<ac\).
  4. If \(a>0\), \(a\cdot a>0\) by (b). If \(-a>0\), then \(a^2=(-a)(-a)>0\) (using \((-1)(-1)=1\) from B.4).
  5. \(ab>0\), so \(\frac1{ab}>0\) (its product with \(ab\) is \(1>0\); a negative would give a negative). Multiply \(a<b\) by \(\frac1{ab}\) (rule 2): \(\frac1b<\frac1a\).
  6. \(c-a=(c-b)+(b-a)\), a sum of two positives.

Rule 3 is the one people get wrong: from \(-2x<6\) follows \(x>-3\), not \(x<-3\).

Which fields can be ordered?Extra · with Skript Lemma 1.4
  • \(\mathbb Q\) and \(\mathbb R\): yes, with the usual "positive".
  • \(\mathbb C\): no (Lemma 1.4): rule 4 would force \(i^2=-1>0\), but \(1>0\) gives \(-1<0\).
  • \(\mathbb Z/p\): no: \(1>0\) and rule (b) give \(1+1+\dots+1>0\) for any number of ones — but \(p\) ones add up to \(0\) in \(\mathbb Z/p\).
And what makes ℝ specialSkript §1.2

\(\mathbb Q\) and \(\mathbb R\) are both ordered fields. The difference is completeness: in ℝ every nonempty set that is bounded above has a least upper bound (the supremum axiom), in ℚ not. So ℝ is a complete ordered field — this is the precise answer to "what are the real numbers?", and it is the starting point of Chapter 1.

B.6Powers and roots [Potenzen und Wurzeln]

Intuition. \(a^n\) starts as a shorthand: "multiply \(a\) by itself \(n\) times". Everything else — \(a^0\), \(a^{-3}\), \(a^{1/2}\), \(a^{\sqrt2}\) — is a later extension, and each extension was chosen for one reason: so that the five rules in the second box below keep holding. That is worth knowing, because it explains every restriction you are about to see. The rules are easy; what costs points is the domains — which rule needs \(a\ge0\), which needs \(a>0\), and why \(\sqrt{x^2}\) is not \(x\).

The course makes all of this rigorous much later: the \(n\)-th root exists by Anwendung 4.13 (intermediate value theorem), and the general power \(x^a:=e^{a\ln x}\) is Definition 5.11. This section is the algebra you are assumed to have from school, stated with the domains the course silently relies on.

The ladder of definitionsExtra · with Skript 4.13, 5.11
expressionmeansneeds
\(a^n\), \(n\in\mathbb N\)\(\underbrace{a\cdot a\cdots a}_{n\text{ factors}}\)nothing — any \(a\) in any field
\(a^0\)\(1\)\(a\neq0\)
\(a^{-n}\), \(n\in\mathbb N\)\(\frac1{a^n}\)\(a\neq0\)
\(a^{1/n}=\sqrt[n]a\)the one \(y>0\) with \(y^n=a\)\(a>0\) (Skript 4.13; \(\sqrt[n]0=0\) is added by hand)
\(a^{m/n}\)\(\left(\sqrt[n]a\right)^m=\sqrt[n]{a^m}\)\(a>0\)
\(a^x\), \(x\in\mathbb R\)\(e^{x\ln a}\)\(a>0\) (Skript 5.11)

Why the middle rows look the way they do: rule 1 below forces them. From \(a^n\cdot a^0=a^{n+0}=a^n\) you get \(a^0=1\); from \(a^n\cdot a^{-n}=a^0=1\) you get \(a^{-n}=\frac1{a^n}\); from \(\left(a^{1/n}\right)^n=a^{\frac1n\cdot n}=a\) you get that \(a^{1/n}\) must be an \(n\)-th root. Nothing was invented — the rules were kept and the definitions followed.

\(0^0\) is left undefined. Both \(a^0=1\) and \(0^x=0\) would apply and they disagree. In the binomial theorem (Satz 2.19) and in power series (Ch. 10) the convention \(0^0=1\) is used silently, because there the exponent is a counting index, not a limit.

The five rules — what you may doSkript Lemma 5.12+ Extra

For \(a,b>0\) and all real \(x,y\):

  1. \(a^x\,a^y=a^{x+y}\) — multiplying powers adds exponents (this one is Lemma 5.12)
  2. \(\dfrac{a^x}{a^y}=a^{x-y}\)
  3. \(\left(a^x\right)^y=a^{xy}\) — a power of a power multiplies exponents
  4. \((ab)^x=a^x\,b^x\)
  5. \(\left(\dfrac ab\right)^x=\dfrac{a^x}{b^x}\)

Read with \(x=\frac1n\), the same five rules are the root rules:

\[\sqrt[n]{ab}=\sqrt[n]a\,\sqrt[n]b,\qquad \sqrt[n]{\frac ab}=\frac{\sqrt[n]a}{\sqrt[n]b},\] \[\sqrt[m]{\sqrt[n]a}=\sqrt[mn]a,\qquad \left(\sqrt[n]a\right)^m=\sqrt[n]{a^m}=a^{m/n}.\]

For integer exponents the five rules hold for every \(a,b\neq0\) — negatives included — because they are then just bookkeeping of factors. The restriction \(a>0\) is needed exactly when the exponent stops being an integer.

Proof of all five in one line each Extra

With \(a^x=e^{x\ln a}\) (Definition 5.11) everything reduces to two facts about \(\exp\) and \(\ln\): \(e^{u+v}=e^ue^v\) and \(\ln(ab)=\ln a+\ln b\).

  1. \(a^xa^y=e^{x\ln a}e^{y\ln a}=e^{(x+y)\ln a}=a^{x+y}\).
  2. Same with \(e^{-y\ln a}=\frac1{e^{y\ln a}}\).
  3. \(\left(a^x\right)^y=e^{y\ln(a^x)}=e^{yx\ln a}=a^{xy}\), using \(\ln(a^x)=\ln\!\left(e^{x\ln a}\right)=x\ln a\).
  4. \((ab)^x=e^{x\ln(ab)}=e^{x\ln a+x\ln b}=a^xb^x\).
  5. Rule 4 with \(b\) replaced by \(\frac1b\), since \(\ln\frac1b=-\ln b\).

The script proves only rule 1 (Lemma 5.12) and the case \(y=n\in\mathbb N\) of rule 3; the rest it uses without stating. This is why \(a>0\) appears everywhere: \(\ln a\) has to exist. ∎

What you may not doExtra
  • \((a+b)^n\neq a^n+b^n\). For \(n=2\) the truth is \(a^2+2ab+b^2\); in general the binomial theorem (Satz 2.19). There is no rule for a power of a sum — only for products and quotients.
  • \(\sqrt{a+b}\neq\sqrt a+\sqrt b\). Test \(a=b=1\): \(\sqrt2\approx1.41\), not \(2\). What is true is \(\sqrt{a+b}\le\sqrt a+\sqrt b\) (square both sides: \(a+b\le a+2\sqrt{ab}+b\)).
  • \(a^x+a^y\neq a^{x+y}\). Exponents add when powers are multiplied. Adding powers does nothing at all; \(2^3+2^4=24\), while \(2^7=128\).
  • \(\left(a^x\right)^y\) and \(a^{x^y}\) are different. \(\left(2^2\right)^3=4^3=64\), but \(2^{2^3}=2^8=256\). A tower without brackets is read top-down.
  • \(\frac1{a+b}\neq\frac1a+\frac1b\) — the same mistake as the first two, with exponent \(-1\).

All four wrong lines have the same shape: a rule for products being applied to a sum. If you remember only one thing from this box: powers and roots commute with \(\cdot\) and \(/\), never with \(+\) and \(-\).

Negative numbers — where the points are actually lostExtra
  • \(\sqrt{x^2}=|x|\), not \(x\). The symbol \(\sqrt{\ }\) returns the nonnegative root by definition, so for \(x=-3\): \(\sqrt{(-3)^2}=\sqrt9=3=|-3|\). Generally \(\sqrt[n]{x^n}=|x|\) for even \(n\) and \(=x\) for odd \(n\). This is the identity behind \(|x|\) (§1.3) and behind the Euclidean norm \(\|x\|=\sqrt{x_1^2+\dots+x_n^2}\) of Chapter 11.
  • Even powers destroy the sign, odd powers keep it. \((-2)^4=16\), \((-2)^3=-8\). So \(x^n=c\) with \(c>0\) has two real solutions \(x=\pm\sqrt[n]c\) when \(n\) is even and one when \(n\) is odd. The \(\pm\) comes from solving the equation, never from the root symbol.
  • \(\sqrt a\sqrt b=\sqrt{ab}\) needs \(a,b\ge0\). Ignore that and you get the classic fallacy \(-1=i\cdot i=\sqrt{-1}\sqrt{-1}=\sqrt{(-1)(-1)}=\sqrt1=1\). Rule 4 was applied outside its domain; nothing else went wrong.
  • Fractional powers of negative numbers are not defined here. \((-8)^{1/3}\) has no meaning in this course, even though \(y^3=-8\) does have the solution \(y=-2\). The reason is rule 3: if \((-8)^{1/3}\) were \(-2\), then \(-2=(-8)^{1/3}=\left((-8)^2\right)^{1/6}=64^{1/6}=2\). Either a rule or the definition has to go, and the course keeps the rules by demanding \(a>0\).
  • \(a^x=e^{x\ln a}\) needs \(a>0\) simply because \(\ln a\) must exist. That is why the exam functions \(x^x\), \(x^{1/x}\), \(x^a\) are always studied on \((0,\infty)\) (Ch. 6, Ch. 7) — not because someone chose a convenient domain, but because there is no other one.
Fractions in the exponent, read slowlyExtra
  • \(a^{1/n}\) is \(\sqrt[n]a\): the number \(y>0\) with \(y^n=a\). That such a \(y\) exists is not obvious — it needs completeness of ℝ (Anwendung 4.13 via the IVT). In ℚ it is false: \(\sqrt2\notin\mathbb Q\).
  • \(a^{m/n}=\left(\sqrt[n]a\right)^m=\sqrt[n]{a^m}\) — both orders give the same number. Take the root first if you want small numbers: \(8^{2/3}=\left(\sqrt[3]8\right)^2=2^2=4\) beats \(\sqrt[3]{64}\).
  • A minus sign in the exponent means "reciprocal", never "negative result": \(4^{-3/2}=\frac1{4^{3/2}}=\frac1{\left(\sqrt4\right)^3}=\frac18\), and \(\left(\frac14\right)^{-1/2}=4^{1/2}=2\).
  • \(\sqrt{\ }\) is a function, which is why it picks the nonnegative root: \(x^2=4\) has the solutions \(\pm2\), but \(\sqrt4=2\) alone.
  • Cancelling in the exponent is safe for \(a>0\) (\(a^{2/4}=a^{1/2}\)) but not in the root notation, where the radicand may be negative: \(\sqrt[4]{a^2}=\sqrt{|a|}\) is defined for every real \(a\), while \(\sqrt a\) is not.

Sanity checks (cover the right-hand sides): \(27^{-1/3}=\frac13\); \(\sqrt{9x^2}=3|x|\); \(\sqrt[4]{16x^8}=2x^2\) (no bars needed — \(x^2\ge0\) already); \(\sqrt{x^2-2x+1}=|x-1|\); \(\left(\frac{a^3b^{-1}}{a\,b^2}\right)^{-1}=\frac{b^3}{a^2}\).

The five moves the course actually usesExtra · signposts
  1. Pull the dominant power out of the root. \(\sqrt{n^2+n}=n\sqrt{1+\frac1n}\) for \(n>0\) (rule 4, backwards), and \(\sqrt{1+\frac1n}\to1\). This is how almost every root limit in Chapter 2 is done.
  2. Conjugate trick [Erweitern mit dem Konjugierten]. A difference of two roots that both grow is "\(\infty-\infty\)", which says nothing. Multiply by \(\frac{\sqrt{n+1}+\sqrt n}{\sqrt{n+1}+\sqrt n}\) and use \((u-v)(u+v)=u^2-v^2\): \[\sqrt{n+1}-\sqrt n=\frac{(n+1)-n}{\sqrt{n+1}+\sqrt n}=\frac1{\sqrt{n+1}+\sqrt n}\to0.\] Appears on Blatt 2.2 and in the exams (§2.2).
  3. Anything to the power of anything goes to base \(e\): \(f(x)^{g(x)}=e^{g(x)\ln f(x)}\), valid where \(f>0\). This is the only way the course differentiates \(x^x\) (Ch. 6) or evaluates \(\lim n^{1/n}\) — a power with the variable in the exponent is not a polynomial and has no other derivative rule.
  4. Swap base and exponent past a logarithm: \(a^{\log_bn}=n^{\log_ba}\), since both equal \(e^{\frac{\ln a\,\ln n}{\ln b}}\) (Lemma 5.13). This turns a strange-looking growth comparison into a comparison of exponents — it is exactly the step in the exam item \(3^{\log_bn}\) vs \(n^3\) (Ch. 6, SoSe23 1g).
  5. Compare sizes by monotonicity, not by calculator. On \([0,\infty)\) the map \(x\mapsto x^n\) is strictly increasing, so for \(0\le a<b\): \(a^n<b^n\) and \(\sqrt[n]a<\sqrt[n]b\). Raising an inequality between nonnegative numbers to a power, or taking roots, is allowed and changes nothing — unlike multiplying by a negative, which flips it (rule 3 of B.5).

One more pattern that surprises people: for \(0<a<1\) a bigger exponent gives a smaller number (\(\left(\frac12\right)^3<\left(\frac12\right)^2\)), so \(\sqrt a>a\) there, while for \(a>1\) everything is the other way round. Both follow from rule 5 of B.5 read through \(x\mapsto a^x=e^{x\ln a}\): the sign of \(\ln a\) decides the direction.

B.7Reading the exam in German [Prüfungssprache]

This guide is in English, but the exam paper is in German. Most of the mathematical vocabulary appears in brackets throughout the chapters; what is easy to overlook are the instruction words and the sentences that tell you how much you have to write. Those decide your points as much as the mathematics does.

Instruction words [Arbeitsanweisungen]Extra
GermanEnglishwhat is expected
Zeigen Sie / Beweisen Sieshow / provea complete argument, every step justified by a named theorem or definition
Begründen Siejustify, give reasonsshorter: name the theorem or give the counterexample, no full proof
Berechnen Sie / Bestimmen Siecompute / determinea result plus the computation
Geben Sie … anstate …the answer alone is enough (often "ohne Begründung", without reasons)
Untersuchen Sie … auf Konvergenzinvestigate … for convergencedecide convergent/divergent and say which criterion you used
Entscheiden Sie, ob …decide whether …yes/no with a reason; a counterexample counts as a reason
Skizzieren Sie / Zeichnen Siesketch / drawa labelled drawing; axes, intercepts, asymptotes
Kreuzen Sie antickmultiple choice; usually no reason needed
Ermitteln / Ableiten / Auflösen nachfind out / differentiate / solve foras it says
Sentences that change how much you must write
  • "Bei dieser Aufgabe wird nur das Ergebnis bewertet." — only the result is graded. Write the answer; working earns nothing (but may still help you).
  • "Bei dieser Aufgabe sind alle Zwischenschritte zu begründen." — every intermediate step must be justified. Name the theorem you use ("nach dem Mittelwertsatz", "mit dem Quotientenkriterium"); a correct result without reasons loses most of the points.
  • "Es genügt, … anzugeben." — it is enough to state …; "ohne Beweis" — no proof required; "Hinweis" — hint, and it is nearly always meant to be used.
  • "Aussage i)* ist schwierig und wird als Bonus gewertet." — that item is a bonus; do it last (this exact sentence is in WiSe20 A2).
  • "(bitte wenden)" — please turn over; "Platz für den Rechenweg" — space for your working; "Zusatzblatt" — extra sheet.

Subject vocabulary (Folge, Reihe, Grenzwert, Häufungspunkt, Schranke, Stammfunktion, …) is given in brackets next to each notion in the chapters, so it is not repeated here. If a word in an old exam is not in this list, the chapter that owns the topic has it.

ΣSummary

  1. Sets: read \(\in,\subseteq,\cup,\cap,\setminus,\times\) and \(\{x\in A:\dots\}\); prove \(A=B\) by showing both inclusions.
  2. Proofs: \(A\Rightarrow B\) is not \(B\Rightarrow A\); the contrapositive \(\neg B\Rightarrow\neg A\) is the same statement; contradiction and induction are the other two standard tools.
  3. Maps: injective = no collisions, surjective = everything hit, bijective = both = invertible. Domain and codomain are part of the map.
  4. Structures: group (one operation, invertible) → ring (+, ·, no division) → field (division by nonzero), each adding rules to the previous one. ℤ is a ring, ℚ, ℝ, ℂ are fields.
  5. Consequences of the field axioms: \(a\cdot0=0\), \((-1)(-1)=1\), \(ab=0\Rightarrow a=0\) or \(b=0\).
  6. Order: "positive" with rules (a), (b) ⇒ all inequality rules; multiplying by a negative flips. ℚ, ℝ ordered; ℂ, \(\mathbb Z/p\) not. ℝ = the complete ordered field.
  7. Sums: \(\sum\) is linear, splits, shifts (\(j=k-1\)) and telescopes; empty sum \(=0\), empty product \(=1\). A product of two sums is not the sum of the products.
  8. Powers and roots: the five rules (\(a^xa^y=a^{x+y}\), \(\frac{a^x}{a^y}=a^{x-y}\), \((a^x)^y=a^{xy}\), \((ab)^x=a^xb^x\), \(\left(\frac ab\right)^x=\frac{a^x}{b^x}\)) hold for \(a,b>0\) and all real exponents, and for \(a,b\neq0\) with integer exponents. They never apply to a sum: \(\sqrt{a+b}\neq\sqrt a+\sqrt b\). \(\sqrt{x^2}=|x|\); fractional powers need a positive base; \(f^g=e^{g\ln f}\) is the way past a variable exponent.
  9. Exam German: zeigen/beweisen = full proof, begründen = give the reason, angeben = answer only; watch for "nur das Ergebnis wird bewertet" and "alle Zwischenschritte sind zu begründen".
