Analysis für Informatik · MA0902 · Chapter 1

Real NumbersReelle Zahlen

What the real numbers are, why analysis needs them, and the three tools this chapter gives you for the rest of the course: supremum and infimum, the Archimedean property, and inequalities. Exercise Sheet 1 and the recurring exam question type are worked into the text at the point where they belong.

Source labels: Skript 1.5 = stated in your lecture script · Blatt 1 · 1.2 = exercise sheet · Klausur WiSe20 = old exam · Extra = my own addition (intuition, a proof the script leaves out, or an extra example). A § marks a whole section of the script (§1.2 = Section 1.2) rather than a numbered result (1.2 = Definition 1.2).

0The big picture

Before you start

This chapter assumes you can read set notation, know what injective/surjective means, and know what a field is. If any of that is shaky, read the Basics page first (about an hour, with its own self-test).

Every new number set in this chapter exists because the previous one had a problem. Reading the chain left to right is the storyline of Section 1.1:

ℕ1, 2, 3, …
need subtraction
ℤ…, −1, 0, 1, …
need division
ℚfractions a/b
need a line without holes (√2)
ℝthe number line
need √−1
ℂthe plane ℝ²

The step that matters most for analysis is ℚ → ℝ. Analysis is about limits, and you can only take limits if they actually land somewhere. In ℚ, the numbers 1, 1.4, 1.41, 1.414, … creep towards a point that is not there, namely √2. The real numbers fill all such holes. The precise statement of "no holes" is the supremum axiom in 1.2, and it is the foundation for everything in Chapters 2–4.

So the chapter has three parts:

  1. 1.1 Which number sets exist, how they differ (countable vs. uncountable, ordered vs. not ordered).
  2. 1.2 What makes ℝ special: sup/inf exist, no infinitely large or small numbers, fractions are everywhere.
  3. 1.3 The inequalities you will use to estimate things in every later proof: triangle inequality and Cauchy–Schwarz.

1.1Number sets at a glance [Zahlenmengen im Überblick]

Quantifiers [Quantoren]

Before any numbers, the script introduces two symbols you will read thousands of times:

  • \(\forall x\): "for all \(x\)" [für alle]
  • \(\exists x\): "there exists an \(x\)" [es existiert]

The single most useful skill with them is negation, because every proof by contradiction starts by negating a statement. The rule: flip every \(\forall\) into \(\exists\), every \(\exists\) into \(\forall\), and negate the statement at the end.

ExampleSkript 1.9, proof

Statement: "every real number is smaller than some natural number": \[\forall a\in\mathbb R\ \exists n\in\mathbb N:\ a\). The opposite of "smaller" is "not smaller", which means "greater or equal".

The order of quantifiers matters. "\(\forall\) people \(\exists\) a mother" is true; "\(\exists\) a mother \(\forall\) people" (one mother of everyone) is false. You will see this again in the definition of convergence, where \(n_0\) is allowed to depend on \(\varepsilon\).

The number sets

NotationSkript §1.1
  • \(\mathbb N=\{1,2,3,\dots\}\) natural numbers [natürliche Zahlen]. Careful: in this course 0 ∉ ℕ. With 0: \(\mathbb N_0=\mathbb N\cup\{0\}\).
  • \(\mathbb Z=\{\dots,-2,-1,0,1,2,\dots\}\) integers [ganze Zahlen]
  • \(\mathbb Q=\{\tfrac ab: a\in\mathbb Z,\ b\in\mathbb N\}\) rational numbers [rationale Zahlen]
  • \(\mathbb R\) real numbers [reelle Zahlen]. \((\mathbb R,+,\cdot)\) is a commutative field [kommutativer Körper].

"Field" just means: you can add, subtract, multiply and divide (except by 0), and all the usual rules hold (commutative, associative, distributive, 0 and 1 behave as expected). ℚ is also a field; ℤ is not, since \(1/2\notin\mathbb Z\). The precise axioms, what follows from them (like \((-1)(-1)=1\) and "\(ab=0\Rightarrow a=0\) or \(b=0\)"), and how rings and groups differ are on the Basics page.

√2 is not rational

This is the proof that ℚ has holes, so it is the reason ℝ is needed at all.

LemmaSkript 1.1

\(\sqrt2\notin\mathbb Q\).

Proof, step by step

Proof by contradiction [Widerspruchsbeweis]: assume \(\sqrt2\in\mathbb Q\).

  1. Then \(\sqrt2=\frac ab\) with \(a\in\mathbb Z,\ b\in\mathbb N\), and we may cancel the fraction completely, so \(a\) and \(b\) are coprime [teilerfremd] (no common prime factor).
  2. Squaring: \(2=\frac{a^2}{b^2}\). Squaring does not create new prime factors, so \(a^2\) and \(b^2\) are still coprime. The fraction \(\frac{a^2}{b^2}\) is therefore fully cancelled.
  3. A fully cancelled fraction equal to the integer 2 must have denominator 1, so \(b^2=1\) and \(a^2=2\).
  4. No integer squares to 2 (\(1^2=1,\ 2^2=4\)). Contradiction. ∎

The script compresses steps 3–4 into "Das ist ein Widerspruch". The version you may know from school (\(a^2=2b^2\Rightarrow a\) even \(\Rightarrow b\) even, contradiction) is equally valid.

Countable and uncountable [abzählbar, überabzählbar]

Intuition. A set is countable if you can write its elements in one infinite list \[f(1),\ f(2),\ f(3),\ \dots\] so that every element of the set turns up somewhere in the list — maybe late, but at a definite, finite position. "Countable" means "can be numbered 1st, 2nd, 3rd, …, and nobody is left out".

DefinitionSkript 1.2

A set \(A\) is countable if there is a surjective map \(f:\mathbb N\to A\), i.e. for every \(a\in A\) there is an \(n\in\mathbb N\) with \(f(n)=a\).

Surjective [surjektiv] means "every element of \(A\) is hit at least once". The number \(n\) with \(f(n)=a\) is the position at which \(a\) appears in the list. Repetitions are allowed: the same element may appear at several positions. A set that is not countable is called uncountable [überabzählbar].

Warm-up examplesExtra
  • ℕ itself: \(f(n)=n\). Trivially countable.
  • The even numbers \(\{2,4,6,\dots\}\): \(f(n)=2n\). There are "as many" even numbers as natural numbers — infinite sets behave strangely.
  • ℤ: the list \(0,\ 1,\ -1,\ 2,\ -2,\ 3,\ -3,\dots\) reaches every integer. The integer \(k>0\) sits at position \(2k\), and \(-k\) at position \(2k+1\). The trick: don't list all positive numbers first (you would never get to the negative ones); alternate instead.

That last trick is the whole idea for ℚ, one dimension up: arrange the list so that you never get stuck in an infinite part before reaching the rest.

LemmaSkript 1.3

\(\mathbb Q\) is countable. \(\mathbb R\) is not countable.

The script proves only the second half and leaves the first as an exercise ("Übung"). Both halves are worked out below: first how to list ℚ, then why no list can ever contain all of ℝ.

Part 1 — Why ℚ is countable: Cantor's diagonal walk Extra (the script's "Übung")

The problem. Between any two fractions there are infinitely many others, so you cannot list fractions "in order of size" — there is no next fraction after 0. A naive list like \(\frac11,\frac12,\frac13,\frac14,\dots\) never ends, so it never reaches \(\frac21\). We need a cleverer order.

The idea: put the fractions in a grid. Every positive fraction is \(\frac pq\) with \(p,q\in\mathbb N\) (numerator \(p\), denominator \(q\)). Write \(\frac pq\) in column \(p\), row \(q\):

p = 1p = 2p = 3p = 4…
q = 11/12/13/14/1…
q = 21/22/23/24/2…
q = 31/32/33/34/3…
q = 41/42/43/44/4…
⋮⋮⋮⋮⋮⋱

Every positive rational number appears in this grid (usually several times: \(\frac12=\frac24=\frac36\)). Walking along a row or a column fails, since each is infinite. Instead, walk along the short diagonals:

  1. Group the grid by diagonals. Diagonal number \(d\) contains all fractions with \(p+q=d\). Diagonal 2 is just \(\frac11\); diagonal 3 is \(\frac12,\frac21\); diagonal 4 is \(\frac13,\frac22,\frac31\); in general diagonal \(d\) has exactly \(d-1\) entries. Each diagonal is finite — that is the whole point.
  2. List the diagonals one after the other, each from its left end to its right end: \[\underbrace{\tfrac11}_{d=2},\ \underbrace{\tfrac12,\ \tfrac21}_{d=3},\ \underbrace{\tfrac13,\ \tfrac22,\ \tfrac31}_{d=4},\ \underbrace{\tfrac14,\ \tfrac23,\ \tfrac32,\ \tfrac41}_{d=5},\ \dots\] Because every diagonal is finite, we finish it and move on to the next — we never get stuck.
  3. Every fraction is reached after finitely many steps. Take any \(\frac pq\). It lies on diagonal \(d=p+q\). Before that diagonal come the diagonals \(2,3,\dots,d-1\), with \(1+2+\dots+(d-2)=\frac{(d-2)(d-1)}2\) entries in total, and inside its own diagonal \(\frac pq\) is entry number \(p\). So it appears at position \[n(p,q)=\frac{(p+q-2)(p+q-1)}2+p,\] a definite natural number. Example: \(\frac23\): \(d=5\), so \(n=\frac{3\cdot4}2+2=8\). Check against the list above: \(\frac11,\frac12,\frac21,\frac13,\frac22,\frac31,\frac14,\frac23\) — eighth. ✓
  4. Add 0 and the negatives with the ℤ trick: put 0 first and each negative right after its positive twin: \[0,\ \tfrac11,\ -\tfrac11,\ \tfrac12,\ -\tfrac12,\ \tfrac21,\ -\tfrac21,\ \tfrac13,\ -\tfrac13,\ \dots\] If \(r>0\) is at position \(n\) in the positive list, then \(r\) is at position \(2n\) and \(-r\) at position \(2n+1\) here. Every rational number is hit, so this is a surjection \(f:\mathbb N\to\mathbb Q\). ∎

What about the repeats? \(\frac22\) is the same number as \(\frac11\), so 1 appears more than once. The script's definition only asks for a surjective map, so repeats are completely fine. (If you want each number exactly once — a bijection — just skip every fraction that is not fully cancelled, like \(\frac22\) or \(\frac24\). The widget can do both.)

Try it · counting ℚ along the diagonals

Press Next step (or Play) and watch the path visit the grid diagonal by diagonal. The list on the right is the numbering \(f(1), f(2), \dots\) that this produces. Greyed, crossed-out cells are repeats of an earlier number. Blue arrows show the walk inside a diagonal; when a diagonal is finished, a dashed line shows the jump to the start of the next one.

Where does a fraction appear?
Why the same trick cannot work for ℝExtra

The diagonal walk works because every fraction is described by two whole numbers \(p,q\) — finitely much information — so the fractions fit in a grid with finite diagonals. A real number like \(\sqrt2=1.41421356\ldots\) needs infinitely many digits to describe. There is no grid of reals with finite diagonals, and Cantor's argument below shows that no clever order at all can work: every attempted list of real numbers misses some number.

Part 2 — Why ℝ is not countable: Cantor's diagonal argument

Strategy (proof by contradiction): assume someone hands us a list that supposedly contains every number in \([0,1]\). We then construct, from the list itself, a number in \([0,1]\) that is guaranteed not to be in it. So no list can be complete.

ProofSkript 1.3, Beweis
  1. Assume ℝ is countable. Then \([0,1]\) is countable as well (drop from the list every entry outside \([0,1]\)), so there is a surjection \(f:\mathbb N\to[0,1]\): a list that contains every number in \([0,1]\).
  2. Write every entry of the list as a decimal: \(f(i)=0.d_{i1}d_{i2}d_{i3}\dots\), where \(d_{ij}\) is the \(j\)-th digit of the \(i\)-th number. Arranged in rows, these digits form an infinite table; the digits \(d_{11},d_{22},d_{33},\dots\) run down its diagonal.
  3. Build a new number \(x=0.d_1d_2d_3\dots\) digit by digit: \(d_i=2\) if \(d_{ii}=1\), otherwise \(d_i=1\). In words: the \(i\)-th digit of \(x\) is chosen to be different from the \(i\)-th digit of the \(i\)-th number.
  4. Then \(x\neq f(1)\) (they differ in digit 1), \(x\neq f(2)\) (they differ in digit 2), and in general \(x\neq f(i)\) because they differ in digit \(i\). So \(x\in[0,1]\) is missing from the list.
  5. That contradicts surjectivity. ∎

Extra Why only the digits 1 and 2? Some numbers have two decimal expansions (\(0.1999\ldots = 0.2000\ldots\)), so two different digit strings can be the same number. Using only 1 and 2 means \(x\) has no 0s or 9s, so it has exactly one expansion, and "different digit" really means "different number".

Try it · Cantor's diagonal

Here is a "complete list" of numbers in [0,1] (only the first 8 rows shown). The highlighted diagonal digits decide the new number x, which cannot appear anywhere in the list.

A computer-science application from the script:

LemmaSkript 1.11

The set \(B\) of computable numbers [berechenbare Zahlen] (real numbers for which some program outputs the first \(n\) decimal digits for every \(n\)) is countable, so \(B\subsetneq\mathbb R\). A number drawn uniformly from \([0,1]\) is not computable with probability 1.

Reason: a program is a finite string over a finite alphabet, and there are only countably many finite strings. Countably many programs can only compute countably many numbers, but ℝ is uncountable. So almost all real numbers can never be printed by any program.

Ordering [Anordnung]

ℝ is not just a field, it is an ordered field. The script defines "ordered" through a single predicate "\(a>0\)" (being positive) with two rules:

DefinitionSkript §1.1 · Anordnung
  1. For every \(a\in\mathbb R\), exactly one of the following holds: \(a=0\), \(a>0\), or \(-a>0\).
  2. If \(a>0\) and \(b>0\), then \(a+b>0\) and \(a\cdot b>0\).

Everything else is notation: \(a>b\) means \(a-b>0\); \(a<b\) means \(b-a>0\); \(a\ge b\) means \(a>b\) or \(a=b\).

From these two rules alone you can prove that every nonzero square is positive: if \(a>0\) then \(a\cdot a>0\) by (b); if \(-a>0\) then \(a^2=(-a)(-a)>0\), again by (b). That little fact is the key to the next result.

Complex numbers [komplexe Zahlen]

Intuition: a complex number is a point in the plane. We just add a rule for multiplying points.

DefinitionSkript §1.1

\(\mathbb C=\{z=(x,y):x,y\in\mathbb R\}=\mathbb R^2\). With the imaginary unit \(i=(0,1)\), every \(z=(x,y)\) can be written as \(z=x+iy\). Here \(x=\operatorname{Re}z\) is the real part [Realteil] and \(y=\operatorname{Im}z\) the imaginary part [Imaginärteil].

Addition: \((x+iy)+(x'+iy')=(x+x')+i(y+y')\)
Multiplication: \(i^2=-1\) and \((x+iy)(x'+iy')=(xx'-yy')+i(xy'+x'y)\)

You don't need to memorise the multiplication formula. Just expand the brackets as usual and replace \(i^2\) by \(-1\):

ExampleExtra
\[(2+3i)(1-i)=2-2i+3i-3i^2=2+i+3=5+i.\]

Check with the formula: \(xx'-yy'=2\cdot1-3\cdot(-1)=5\) and \(xy'+x'y=2\cdot(-1)+1\cdot3=1\). ✓

Try it · complex numbers are points

Drag the blue point z and the green point w. Tick what you want to see. Adding complex numbers is adding arrows (the parallelogram rule); the conjugate mirrors at the real axis; \(|z|\) is the distance to 0.

Extra Watch the product: its length is \(|z|\cdot|w|\) and its angle (measured from the positive real axis) is the angle of \(z\) plus the angle of \(w\). You don't need this yet — Chapter 5 proves it with Euler's formula — but it is the reason multiplication by \(i\) is a quarter turn: \(i\) has length 1 and angle 90°, so \(i\cdot i\) has angle 180°, i.e. \(i^2=-1\).

Exercise Sheet 1 adds three tools you will use constantly, especially in Chapter 5 (Euler's formula):

From the exercise sheetBlatt 1 · Aufgabe 1.3

The complex conjugate [komplex Konjugierte] of \(z=a+ib\) is \(\bar z=a-ib\): the point mirrored at the real axis. Then:

\[\text{(a)}\ \operatorname{Re}z=\tfrac12(z+\bar z),\qquad \text{(b)}\ \operatorname{Im}z=\tfrac1{2i}(z-\bar z),\qquad \text{(c)}\ |z|=\sqrt{z\bar z}.\]
Solution
  1. \(z+\bar z=(a+ib)+(a-ib)=2a\), so \(\tfrac12(z+\bar z)=a=\operatorname{Re}z\).
  2. \(z-\bar z=(a+ib)-(a-ib)=2ib\), so \(\tfrac1{2i}(z-\bar z)=b=\operatorname{Im}z\).
  3. \(z\bar z=(a+ib)(a-ib)=a^2-i^2b^2+(iab-iab)=a^2+b^2=|z|^2\). Taking the root gives the claim.

Why (c) matters: \(|z|=\sqrt{a^2+b^2}\) is the distance of the point \((a,b)\) from 0 (Pythagoras). Example: \(z=3+4i\) gives \(z\bar z=9+16=25\), so \(|z|=5\). And \(z\bar z\) is always a real number \(\ge0\), which is why the trick "multiply by the conjugate" gets rid of \(i\) in a denominator.

LemmaSkript 1.4

\(\mathbb C\) cannot be ordered. (There is no way to define "positive" on ℂ that satisfies rules (a) and (b).)

Proof
  1. Assume ℂ were ordered. As shown above, rules (a) and (b) force \(a^2>0\) for every \(a\neq0\).
  2. In particular \(1=1\cdot1>0\), so \(-1<0\).
  3. But \(-1=i^2\), and \(i\neq0\), so \(i^2>0\). Contradiction. ∎
Watch out

Never write \(z<w\) for complex numbers. Inequalities in ℂ only make sense for real quantities like \(|z|\), \(\operatorname{Re}z\), \(\operatorname{Im}z\). That is why all later statements for complex sequences are phrased with \(|a_n-a|\).

1.2Properties of the real numbers [Eigenschaften der reellen Zahlen]

Notation: intervalsSkript §1.2

For \(a\le b\): \((a,b)=\{x: a<x<b\}\) open [offen], \([a,b]=\{x:a\le x\le b\}\) closed [abgeschlossen], \((a,b]\) and \([a,b)\) half-open [halboffen]. A round bracket means "endpoint excluded", a square bracket "endpoint included".

Upper bounds, supremum and infimum

Intuition: an upper bound is a ceiling for a set, a number that nothing in the set exceeds. A set has many ceilings. The supremum is the lowest ceiling, the one that touches the set.

DefinitionSkript 1.5

A set \(M\subseteq\mathbb R\) is bounded above [nach oben beschränkt] if there is an \(s_0\in\mathbb R\) with \[a\le s_0\quad\text{for all }a\in M.\] Such an \(s_0\) is called an upper bound [obere Schranke] of \(M\). "Bounded below" and "lower bound" [untere Schranke] are defined the same way with \(\ge\).

ExampleSkript 1.6
  • \(M=(0,1)\): every \(s_0\ge1\) is an upper bound. The smallest one is \(1\), but \(1\notin M\): no upper bound lies inside \(M\).
  • \(M=[0,1]\): again the smallest upper bound is 1, and this time \(1\in M\).

This pair of examples is the whole difference between supremum and maximum.

Supremum axiomSkript §1.2 · Supremumsaxiom

Every nonempty subset \(M\subseteq\mathbb R\) that is bounded above has a least upper bound \(\sup M\in\mathbb R\), the supremum of \(M\).

It follows: every nonempty set that is bounded below has a greatest lower bound \(\inf M\in\mathbb R\), the infimum.

Why the infimum version follows (the script says only "Daraus folgt")

Extra Mirror the set at 0: let \(-M=\{-a:a\in M\}\). Multiplying by \(-1\) turns every inequality around, so

  1. \(c\) is a lower bound of \(M\) \(\iff\) \(c\le a\) for all \(a\in M\) \(\iff\) \(-a\le-c\) for all \(a\in M\) \(\iff\) \(-c\) is an upper bound of \(-M\).
  2. \(M\) is nonempty and bounded below, so \(-M\) is nonempty and bounded above, and the supremum axiom gives \(s=\sup(-M)\).
  3. By step 1, "the smallest upper bound of \(-M\)" becomes "the largest lower bound of \(M\)" after flipping the sign. So \(\inf M=-\sup(-M)\) exists. ∎

Example: \(M=\{1,2,3\}\), \(-M=\{-1,-2,-3\}\), \(\sup(-M)=-1\), so \(\inf M=1\). ✓

If \(\sup M\in M\), it is also called the maximum [Maximum]. If \(\inf M\in M\), it is the minimum [Minimum].

The script lists some conventions for the cases the axiom doesn't cover: \(\sup M=\infty\) if \(M\) is not bounded above, \(\inf M=-\infty\) if \(M\) is not bounded below, \(\sup\emptyset=-\infty\), and \(-\infty<a<\infty\) for every real \(a\).

Common mistakes
  • The supremum always exists (for nonempty bounded sets); the maximum may not. The max exists ⇔ sup ∈ M, and then max = sup.
  • \(\sup M\) does not have to be an element of \(M\). In \(\{-\frac1n:n\in\mathbb N\}\) the supremum is 0, but no \(-\frac1n\) equals 0 (Skript 1.7).
  • "∞ is the supremum" is only a convention. If \(\sup M=\infty\), then the maximum does not exist.

Why ℚ is not enough

RemarkSkript 1.8

ℚ does not satisfy the supremum axiom. The set \(M=\{a\in\mathbb Q: a^2\le2\}\) is nonempty and bounded above (e.g. by 2), but it has no least upper bound in ℚ. In ℝ, \(\sup M=\sqrt2\notin\mathbb Q\).

This is the precise meaning of "ℝ has no holes": whenever a set pushes upward against a ceiling, the lowest ceiling actually exists as a real number. In ℚ, the lowest ceiling of \(M\) would be √2, and that spot is empty.

How to prove that a number is the supremum

This is what exams ask for, so here is a reusable recipe. It follows directly from the definition ("least upper bound" = an upper bound, and nothing smaller is one). The script uses exactly this reasoning inside its proofs of Satz 1.9 and Satz 2.17.

Method · proving s = sup MExtra (follows from Skript 1.5)
  1. \(s\) is an upper bound: show \(a\le s\) for all \(a\in M\).
  2. No smaller number is an upper bound: for every \(\varepsilon>0\), find an element \(a\in M\) with \(a>s-\varepsilon\). (So \(s-\varepsilon\) is "pierced" by the set.)
  3. Max or not? Check whether \(s\in M\).

For the infimum, mirror everything: \(s\le a\) for all \(a\in M\), and for every \(\varepsilon>0\) some \(a\in M\) with \(a<s+\varepsilon\).

A useful shortcut for sets given by a sequence \(M=\{x_n:n\in\mathbb N\}\): if \(x_n\) is increasing, then \(x_1\) is the minimum, and the supremum is the limit — or \(\infty\) if the sequence is unbounded. If it is decreasing, \(x_1\) is the maximum and the infimum is the limit (or \(-\infty\)). (Chapter 2, Satz 2.17, turns "the limit is the sup" into a theorem.)

Four rules that save workExtra

For nonempty bounded sets \(A,B\subseteq\mathbb R\) and \(c\in\mathbb R\), write \(A+B=\{a+b:a\in A,\ b\in B\}\) and \(cA=\{ca:a\in A\}\). Then

  1. \(\sup(-A)=-\inf A\) and \(\inf(-A)=-\sup A\) — mirroring at 0 swaps the two (this is the proof of the infimum version of the axiom above);
  2. \(\sup(A\cup B)=\max\{\sup A,\sup B\}\);
  3. \(\sup(A+B)=\sup A+\sup B\);
  4. \(\sup(cA)=c\sup A\) for \(c>0\), but \(\sup(cA)=c\inf A\) for \(c<0\).
Proof of 3, and why the exam's function version only gives "≤"

"\(\le\)": every element of \(A+B\) is some \(a+b\le\sup A+\sup B\), so the right side is an upper bound, and \(\sup(A+B)\) is the least one.

"\(\ge\)": let \(\varepsilon>0\). By step 2 of the recipe there are \(a\in A\) with \(a>\sup A-\frac\varepsilon2\) and \(b\in B\) with \(b>\sup B-\frac\varepsilon2\). Then \(a+b\in A+B\) and \(a+b>\sup A+\sup B-\varepsilon\). Since this works for every \(\varepsilon>0\), no number below \(\sup A+\sup B\) is an upper bound. ∎

The catch: here \(a\) and \(b\) may be chosen independently. For functions, \(\sup_x(f(x)+g(x))\) has to use the same \(x\) in both terms, so the two suprema may be attained at different points and only "\(\le\)" survives — that is exactly Klausur WiSe18 A5 (worked in the exam section below).

Try it · the two-step test for "s = sup M"

Choose a set and a candidate \(s\). The test checks the two steps of the recipe: (1) is \(s\) an upper bound? (2) for the chosen \(\varepsilon\), is there an element above \(s-\varepsilon\)? Try a candidate that is too small, one that is too big, and then the right one — and make ε tiny to see that step 2 still works, just with a later element.

Exam question, fully workedKlausur WiSe20 · Aufgabe 1b

"Determine infimum, supremum, minimum and maximum (if they exist) of \(M=\left\{\frac{n+1}{n}: n\in\mathbb N\right\}\), with a short justification."

Solution

Look first. \(x_n=\frac{n+1}{n}=1+\frac1n\): the elements are \(2,\ \frac32,\ \frac43,\ \frac54,\dots\), falling towards 1.

Monotone: \(x_{n+1}=1+\frac1{n+1}<1+\frac1n=x_n\), so the sequence is decreasing. The exam solution checks this via \(\frac{x_{n+1}}{x_n}=\frac{n^2+2n}{n^2+2n+1}<1\).

Supremum and maximum. Decreasing, so every \(x_n\le x_1=2\): 2 is an upper bound, and it is an element of \(M\). So \(\sup M=\max M=2\).

Infimum. Every \(x_n=1+\frac1n>1\), so 1 is a lower bound (step 1). No larger number is a lower bound (step 2): given \(x'>1\), we need some \(n\) with \(1+\frac1n<x'\), i.e. \(n>\frac1{x'-1}\). Such an \(n\) exists because ℝ is Archimedean (Satz 1.9, below). So \(\inf M=1\).

Minimum? \(1+\frac1n=1\) has no solution, so \(1\notin M\): no minimum.

Try it · sup / inf / max / min explorer

Pick a set. The number line shows its elements (dots are single elements, bars are intervals; a hollow end means the endpoint is not included). Several of these sets come from old exams.

The Archimedean property [archimedisch]

SatzSkript 1.9

ℝ is Archimedean: for every \(a\in\mathbb R\) there is an \(n\in\mathbb N\) with \(a<n\). In particular, there are no infinitely large numbers in ℝ.

Proof, step by step
  1. Assume the statement is false. Its negation (see quantifiers) is: \(\exists a\in\mathbb R\ \forall n\in\mathbb N:\ a\ge n\). So \(a\) is an upper bound of ℕ, and ℕ is bounded above.
  2. ℕ is nonempty and bounded above, so by the supremum axiom \(s=\sup\mathbb N\) exists.
  3. \(s\) is the least upper bound, so \(s-1\) is not an upper bound: there is an \(n\in\mathbb N\) with \(s-1<n\).
  4. Then \(s<n+1\), and \(n+1\in\mathbb N\). So \(s\) is not an upper bound of ℕ. Contradiction. ∎

Notice step 3: it is exactly step 2 of the supremum recipe, with \(\varepsilon=1\).

ConsequenceSkript 1.9, Folgerung

For every \(a>0\) there is an \(n\in\mathbb N\) with \(\frac1n<a\). So there are no infinitely small positive numbers either.

Why: \(\frac1a\) is a real number, so by Archimedes some \(n>\frac1a\), and flipping gives \(\frac1n<a\). This is the most-used line in Chapter 2. It is exactly how the script proves \(\lim\frac1n=0\) (Beispiel 2.4): given \(\varepsilon\), choose \(n_0>\frac1\varepsilon\).

ExampleExtra

\(a=0.003\): we need \(n>\frac1{0.003}\approx333.3\), so \(n=334\) works: \(\frac1{334}\approx0.00299<0.003\).

SatzSkript 1.10

ℚ is dense [dicht] in ℝ: for all \(a<b\) in ℝ there is an \(r\in\mathbb Q\) with \(a<r<b\).

Consequence: every real number can be approximated arbitrarily well by fractions. For every \(n\) there is \(r\in\mathbb Q\) with \(a-10^{-n}<r<a\).

Proof (the script states it without proof, so I supply one)

Extra Idea: zoom in by a factor \(n\) until the gap between \(a\) and \(b\) is longer than 1. Then an integer must fit inside it.

  1. By Archimedes choose \(n\in\mathbb N\) with \(n>\frac1{b-a}\), so \(nb-na>1\).
  2. Let \(m\) be the smallest integer with \(m>na\). Such an \(m\) exists: the set \(S=\{m\in\mathbb Z:m>na\}\) is nonempty by Archimedes, and it is bounded below, because Archimedes applied to \(-na\) gives a \(k\in\mathbb N\) with \(-k<na\), so every element of \(S\) is \(>-k\). A nonempty set of integers that is bounded below has a smallest element. Minimality means \(m-1\le na\).
  3. Then \(na<m\le na+1<nb\).
  4. Divide by \(n\): \(a<\frac mn<b\), and \(r=\frac mn\in\mathbb Q\). ∎

Example: \(a=0.31,\ b=0.32\). The gap is 0.01, so take \(n=101\). Then \(na=31.31\), \(m=32\), \(r=\frac{32}{101}\approx0.3168\). ✓

Try it · finding a fraction between a and b

This replays the proof of Satz 1.10. Choose \(a\) and the gap \(b-a\). The proof zooms in by the factor \(n\) until the stretched gap \([na,\,nb]\) is longer than 1 — then a whole number \(m\) must fit inside, and \(\frac mn\) lies between \(a\) and \(b\).

Decimal representation [Dezimaldarstellung]

Every real number has a decimal expansion \(\pm d_0.d_1d_2d_3\ldots\) with \(d_0\in\mathbb N_0\) and digits \(d_i\in\{0,\dots,9\}\). The script connects decimals to the supremum in both directions:

  • Decimal → real number: \(d_0.d_1d_2\ldots\) means \(\sup\{d_0.d_1\ldots d_n: n\in\mathbb N\}\). Every finite truncation is rational (\(1.37=\frac{137}{100}\)), and the real number is the supremum of these truncations.
  • Real number → decimal: for \(a\ge0\) set \(p_n=\max\{p\in\mathbb N_0: p\le10^na\}\), and \(\frac{p_n}{10^n}\) gives the first \(n\) digits.
ExampleExtra (same scheme as Skript's 1.37)

\(a=\pi\): \(p_0=3,\ p_1=\max\{p\le31.4\ldots\}=31,\ p_2=314,\ p_3=3141\). So \(\frac{p_3}{10^3}=3.141\).

This is the supremum axiom at work: the truncations 3, 3.1, 3.14, 3.141, … form a bounded set of rationals, and π is its supremum. In ℚ that supremum would not exist.

One subtlety: 0.999… = 1Extra

The script says the second procedure gives a bijection between ℝ and decimal expansions. That is only true because the procedure never produces an expansion ending in endless 9s: for \(a=1\) it gives \(1.000\ldots\), never \(0.999\ldots\). Both strings describe the same number — by the first bullet, \(0.999\ldots=\sup\{0.9,\ 0.99,\ 0.999,\dots\}=1\), because 1 is an upper bound and for every \(\varepsilon>0\) some \(0.99\ldots9=1-10^{-n}\) exceeds \(1-\varepsilon\) (Archimedes). This is exactly why Cantor's argument above avoids the digits 0 and 9.

What you must know for 1.2 (script: "Minimale Inhalte"): supremum, infimum, minimum and maximum: know them and be able to determine them.

1.3Important inequalities [Wichtige Ungleichungen]

Why inequalities? From Chapter 2 on, almost every proof reads "make \(|a_n-a|\) smaller than \(\varepsilon\)". You rarely compute \(|a_n-a|\) exactly. You estimate it, and these inequalities are the estimating tools.

DefinitionSkript §1.3

The absolute value [Betrag]: \(|x|=x\) if \(x\ge0\), and \(|x|=-x\) if \(x<0\).

Picture: \(|x|\) is the distance of \(x\) from 0, and \(|x-y|\) is the distance between \(x\) and \(y\) on the number line. Read \(|a_n-a|<\varepsilon\) as "\(a_n\) is closer than \(\varepsilon\) to \(a\)".

Triangle inequality [Dreiecksungleichung]

SatzSkript 1.12

For all \(x,y\in\mathbb R\): \[|x+y|\le|x|+|y|,\] with equality if and only if \(xy\ge0\) (same sign). More generally \(\left|\sum_{i=1}^nx_i\right|\le\sum_{i=1}^n|x_i|\), with equality iff all \(x_i\) have the same sign.

Proof (not in the script, so I supply one)

Extra Use the fact \(|c|\le d \iff -d\le c\le d\).

  1. Always \(-|x|\le x\le|x|\) and \(-|y|\le y\le|y|\).
  2. Add the two chains: \(-(|x|+|y|)\le x+y\le|x|+|y|\).
  3. By the fact above, this says \(|x+y|\le|x|+|y|\). ∎

When is it an equality? If \(x,y\ge0\): \(|x+y|=x+y=|x|+|y|\). If \(x,y\le0\): \(|x+y|=-(x+y)=|x|+|y|\). If they have opposite signs, say \(x>0>y\), then \(x+y\) lies strictly between \(y\) and \(x\), so \(|x+y|<\max(|x|,|y|)\le|x|+|y|\) — strict. So equality holds exactly when \(xy\ge0\).

The version with \(n\) terms follows by induction [vollständige Induktion]: it holds for \(n=2\) (just proved), and if \(\left|\sum_{i=1}^nx_i\right|\le\sum_{i=1}^n|x_i|\), then treating \(\sum_{i=1}^nx_i\) as a single number and applying the two-term case gives \(\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}x_i\right|\le\left|\sum_{i=1}^nx_i\right|+|x_{n+1}|\le\sum_{i=1}^{n+1}|x_i|\). (Induction as a method is explained in Chapter 2, where the course first leans on it.)

Intuition: if you walk \(x\) steps and then \(y\) steps, you end up at most \(|x|+|y|\) away from the start. You land exactly that far only if you never turned around.

SatzSkript 1.13

Reverse triangle inequality [umgekehrte Dreiecksungleichung]: for all \(x,y\in\mathbb R\) \[|x+y|\ge\big||x|-|y|\big|,\] with equality if and only if \(xy\le0\) (opposite signs).

Intuition: walking forward and then back leaves you at least the difference of the two distances away.

Proof: the script gives none. It is Tutoriumsaufgabe 1.1a below (replace \(y\) by \(-y\) there, using \(|-y|=|y|\)). Equality case: if \(x,y\) have opposite signs, \(x+y\) is the difference of two distances, e.g. \(3+(-5)=-2\) and \(\big|3-5\big|=2\). If they have the same sign, \(|x+y|=|x|+|y|\), which is bigger than \(\big||x|-|y|\big|\) unless one of them is 0.

Try it · both triangle inequalities

Move x and y. The blue arrow is x, the green arrow is y attached to its tip, so the second tip sits at x + y.

From the exercise sheetBlatt 1 · Tutoriumsaufgabe 1.1a

Prove the reverse triangle inequality in the form \[\big||x|-|y|\big|\le|x-y|\qquad\forall x,y\in\mathbb R.\] (Same statement as Satz 1.13, with \(y\) replaced by \(-y\), since \(|-y|=|y|\).)

Solution, with the reasoning spelled out

Strategy: \(|c|\le d\) means both \(c\le d\) and \(-c\le d\). So we show \(|x|-|y|\le|x-y|\) and \(|y|-|x|\le|x-y|\) separately, each time using the normal triangle inequality with a clever split.

  1. Write \(x=(x-y)+y\). Triangle inequality: \(|x|\le|x-y|+|y|\), so \(|x|-|y|\le|x-y|\).
  2. Write \(y=(y-x)+x\). Triangle inequality: \(|y|\le|y-x|+|x|=|x-y|+|x|\), so \(|y|-|x|\le|x-y|\).
  3. Both \(c=|x|-|y|\) and \(-c\) are \(\le|x-y|\), so \(\big||x|-|y|\big|\le|x-y|\). ∎

The official solution does the same with \(z=-y\), starting from \(|a+b|-|b|\le|a|\).

Reusable trick: "add and subtract" (\(x=(x-y)+y\)). You will use it constantly, e.g. in the proof of the squeeze theorem (Skript 2.14): \(|b_n-\alpha|\le|b_n-a_n|+|a_n-\alpha|\).

What it says in words: if \(x\) and \(y\) are close, then \(|x|\) and \(|y|\) are close. Later this shows: \(a_n\to a\) implies \(|a_n|\to|a|\).

Young's ε-inequality

From the exercise sheetBlatt 1 · Tutoriumsaufgabe 1.1b

For all \(x,y\in\mathbb R\) and all \(\varepsilon>0\): \[xy\le\frac{x^2}{2\varepsilon}+\frac{\varepsilon y^2}{2}.\]

Solution

Idea: squares are never negative. Pick a square that contains \(xy\) when you expand it.

\[0\le\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt\varepsilon}-\sqrt\varepsilon\,y\right)^2=\frac{x^2}{\varepsilon}-2xy+\varepsilon y^2.\]

Add \(2xy\) and divide by 2: \(xy\le\frac{x^2}{2\varepsilon}+\frac{\varepsilon y^2}{2}\). ∎

Special case \(\varepsilon=1\): \(xy\le\frac{x^2+y^2}{2}\), which is just \(0\le(x-y)^2\). Keep this one in mind: it is exactly the key step (1.3) in the proof of Cauchy–Schwarz below.

Why the ε? It lets you split a product \(xy\) into an \(x\)-part and a \(y\)-part, and make one of them as small as you like ("rob Peter to pay Paul"). With \(\varepsilon=0.01\): \(xy\le 50x^2+0.005y^2\).

Vectors in ℝⁿ

DefinitionSkript §1.3

\(\mathbb R^n=\{x=(x_1,\dots,x_n): x_i\in\mathbb R\}\). The length [Länge / Norm] and the dot product [Skalarprodukt] are \[\|x\|=\sqrt{\sum_{i=1}^nx_i^2},\qquad \langle x,y\rangle=\sum_{i=1}^nx_iy_i,\qquad\text{so }\|x\|^2=\langle x,x\rangle.\]

Example: \(x=(3,4)\): \(\|x\|=\sqrt{9+16}=5\). This is Pythagoras in \(n\) dimensions.

Cauchy–Schwarz inequality

SatzSkript 1.15

For all \(x,y\in\mathbb R^n\): \[|\langle x,y\rangle|\le\|x\|\cdot\|y\|,\] with equality iff \(x=0\), \(y=0\), or \(x=\lambda y\) for some \(\lambda\in\mathbb R\) (the vectors are parallel).

Intuition Extra: from linear algebra, \(\langle x,y\rangle=\|x\|\|y\|\cos\theta\), where \(\theta\) is the angle between the vectors. Since \(|\cos\theta|\le1\), the inequality is no surprise, and equality means \(\cos\theta=\pm1\): parallel vectors. But this course has not defined angles yet, so the script proves it using only algebra.

Proof, step by step
  1. Trivial case: if \(\|x\|=0\), then \(x=0\) and both sides are 0. Same for \(y\). From now on \(\|x\|,\|y\|>0\).
  2. Remove signs: by the triangle inequality, \(|\langle x,y\rangle|=\left|\sum x_iy_i\right|\le\sum|x_iy_i|\). It suffices to show \(\sum|x_i||y_i|\le\|x\|\|y\|\).
  3. Normalise: set \(a_i=\frac{|x_i|}{\|x\|}\) and \(b_i=\frac{|y_i|}{\|y\|}\). These are the entries of \(x\) and \(y\) rescaled to length 1: \(\sum a_i^2=\sum b_i^2=1\).
  4. The key step (Young with ε = 1): \(0\le(a_i-b_i)^2\), so \(a_ib_i\le\frac12(a_i^2+b_i^2)\).
  5. Sum over i: \(\sum a_ib_i\le\frac12\left(\sum a_i^2+\sum b_i^2\right)=\frac12(1+1)=1\).
  6. Undo the normalisation: \(\sum\frac{|x_i|}{\|x\|}\frac{|y_i|}{\|y\|}\le1\), i.e. \(\sum|x_iy_i|\le\|x\|\|y\|\). Together with step 2, done. ∎

Equality needs equality in both step 2 (all \(x_iy_i\) have the same sign) and step 4 (\(a_i=b_i\) for all \(i\), i.e. \(|x_i|=\frac{\|x\|}{\|y\|}|y_i|\)). Together this is exactly \(x_i=\lambda y_i\) for all \(i\).

Why normalise? Without it, step 5 would give \(\sum|x_i||y_i|\le\frac12(\|x\|^2+\|y\|^2)\). That is true but too weak. Rescaling both vectors to length 1 makes the right side exactly 1.

ExampleExtra

\(x=(1,2),\ y=(3,4)\): \(\langle x,y\rangle=3+8=11\) and \(\|x\|\|y\|=\sqrt5\cdot5\approx11.18\). Indeed \(11\le11.18\). They are almost equal because the vectors point in almost the same direction.
\(x=(1,2),\ y=(2,4)=2x\): \(\langle x,y\rangle=10=\sqrt5\cdot\sqrt{20}\). Equality, as the theorem predicts for parallel vectors.

SatzSkript 1.14

Triangle inequality in ℝⁿ: \(\|x+y\|\le\|x\|+\|y\|\) for all \(x,y\in\mathbb R^n\). Geometrically: one side of a triangle is never longer than the other two together.

The script only says "the proof uses Cauchy–Schwarz". Exercise 1.2(b) carries it out: see the next box, property (N2).

Norms: different ways to measure length

From the exercise sheetBlatt 1 · Abgabeaufgabe 1.2

A norm [Norm] on ℝⁿ is a map \(\|\cdot\|:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R\) with, for all \(x,y\in\mathbb R^n\), \(\lambda\in\mathbb R\):

  • (N1) Definiteness [Definitheit]: \(\|x\|=0\iff x=0\)
  • (N2) Triangle inequality: \(\|x+y\|\le\|x\|+\|y\|\)
  • (N3) Absolute homogeneity [absolute Homogenität]: \(\|\lambda x\|=|\lambda|\,\|x\|\)

These are the three properties anything must have to behave like "length".

(a) A norm is never negative

Hint from the sheet: look at \(\|x-x\|\).

\[0\overset{(N1)}=\|0\|=\|x+(-x)\|\overset{(N2)}\le\|x\|+\|-x\|\overset{(N3)}=\|x\|+|-1|\,\|x\|=2\|x\|.\]

So \(\|x\|\ge0\). Nice: non-negativity doesn't need to be an axiom, it follows from (N1)–(N3).

(b) The Euclidean norm \(\|x\|_2=\sqrt{\langle x,x\rangle}\) is a norm

(N1) \(\|x\|_2=0\iff\sum x_j^2=0\iff\) every \(x_j=0\) (a sum of non-negative squares is 0 only if each square is 0) \(\iff x=0\).

(N2) Square the left side and expand: \[\|x+y\|_2^2=\sum(x_j+y_j)^2=\|x\|_2^2+\|y\|_2^2+2\langle x,y\rangle\le\|x\|_2^2+\|y\|_2^2+2\|x\|_2\|y\|_2=(\|x\|_2+\|y\|_2)^2,\] using Cauchy–Schwarz for the middle term. Taking square roots (both sides \(\ge0\)) gives the triangle inequality. This is the proof of Satz 1.14.

(N3) \(\|\lambda x\|_2=\sqrt{\sum\lambda^2x_j^2}=\sqrt{\lambda^2}\sqrt{\sum x_j^2}=|\lambda|\,\|x\|_2\). Note \(\sqrt{\lambda^2}=|\lambda|\), not \(\lambda\).

(c) \(\|x\|_1=\sum|x_i|\) is a norm, but \(\sum x_i\) is not

(N1) \(\sum|x_j|=0\iff\) all \(|x_j|=0\iff x=0\). (N2) \(\sum|x_j+y_j|\le\sum(|x_j|+|y_j|)=\|x\|_1+\|y\|_1\), using the real triangle inequality (Satz 1.12) in each coordinate. (N3) \(\sum|\lambda x_j|=|\lambda|\sum|x_j|\).

\(\sum x_i\) is not a norm: counterexample \(x=(1,-1)\neq0\), but \(1+(-1)=0\). (N1) fails. (It can even be negative, e.g. \(x=(-1,0)\).)

(d) \(\|x\|_\infty=\max\{|x_1|,\dots,|x_n|\}\) is a norm

(N1) The largest \(|x_j|\) is 0 \(\iff\) all are 0. (N2) \(\max|x_j+y_j|\le\max(|x_j|+|y_j|)\le\max|x_j|+\max|y_j|\). The second step holds because each \(|x_j|\le\max_k|x_k|\) and each \(|y_j|\le\max_k|y_k|\). (N3) \(\max|\lambda x_j|=|\lambda|\max|x_j|\).

(e) Unit "circles". The sets \(\{x\in\mathbb R^2:\|x\|=1\}\) look completely different for the three norms: a circle for \(\|\cdot\|_2\), a diamond (rotated square) for \(\|\cdot\|_1\), and an axis-parallel square for \(\|\cdot\|_\infty\). Drag the point below to see them.

Try it · three norms, three "circles"

Drag the point (or use the sliders). The three shapes are the unit sets ‖x‖₂ = 1 (circle), ‖x‖₁ = 1 (diamond) and ‖x‖∞ = 1 (square).

Things to notice Extra: you always get ‖x‖∞ ≤ ‖x‖₂ ≤ ‖x‖₁, which is why the square lies outside the circle and the diamond inside it. On the axes all three norms agree.

What you must know for 1.3 (script: "Minimale Inhalte"): be able to apply the different variants of the triangle inequality and the Cauchy–Schwarz inequality.

!What the exam asks from Chapter 1

I went through all nine uploaded exams (WiSe18 – WiSe24). Chapter 1 appears in five of them, and almost always in the same form. Here is the complete list:

ExamTaskType
SoSe19 A1 (10 P)sup, inf, max, min of three sets: \(\{n\in\mathbb N:n^2=(2k-1)n\text{ for some }k\}\), \(\{x:x^2-1>0\}\), \(\{x:|x-1|-1<0\}\)sup/inf
WiSe19 1asup and min of \(\{\frac n{n+2}:n\in\mathbb N\}\)sup/inf
WiSe20 1bsup, inf, max, min of \(\{\frac{n+1}n:n\in\mathbb N\}\)sup/inf
SoSe23 1d\(\inf\) and \(\sup\) of \(a_n=e^{1/n}+e^{-1/n}\)sup/inf
WiSe18 A5 (8 P)\(\sup(f+g)\le\sup f+\sup g\), with an example of strict inequalitysup proof
WiSe19 1fdetermine \(\{z\in\mathbb C:z=|z|^2\}\)complex numbers

Conclusion: the one skill you must have cold is determining and justifying sup, inf, max and min. Countability, Cantor, Archimedes and the inequalities are not asked as standalone tasks — but Archimedes and the triangle inequality are used inside nearly every ε-proof of Chapter 2, so you need them anyway. The four tasks not yet worked above follow (the other exam sets are worked in the explorer).

Exam questionKlausur SoSe19 · A1a

Determine sup, inf, max and min of \(A=\{n\in\mathbb N:\ n^2=(2k-1)n\text{ for some }k\in\mathbb N\}\).

Solution — and a warning about the official one
  1. Understand the set first. For \(n\neq0\) divide \(n^2=(2k-1)n\) by \(n\): \(n=2k-1\). So \(n\) belongs to \(A\) exactly when \(n\) is odd. With \(\mathbb N=\{1,2,3,\dots\}\) (the convention of your script): \(A=\{1,3,5,7,\dots\}\).
  2. Infimum and minimum. Every element is \(\ge1\) and \(1\in A\) (take \(k=1\)). So \(\inf A=\min A=1\).
  3. Supremum. \(A\) contains \(2k-1\) for every \(k\), and these grow beyond every bound (Archimedes). So \(A\) is not bounded above: \(\sup A=\infty\), no maximum.

Warning. The official SoSe19 solution answers \(\inf A=\min A=0\), because that exam counted \(0\) as a natural number (\(0^2=(2k-1)\cdot0\) holds for every \(k\)). In your course \(0\notin\mathbb N\) (Skript §1.1), so the answer is 1. In an exam, state the convention you use in one line — then either answer is gradeable.

Exam questionKlausur SoSe23 · 1d

Let \(a_n=e^{1/n}+e^{-1/n}\), \(n\in\mathbb N\). Determine \(\inf_{n}a_n\) and \(\sup_na_n\).

Solution
  1. Look first. \(a_1=e+\frac1e\approx3.086\), \(a_2=e^{0.5}+e^{-0.5}\approx2.255\), \(a_{10}\approx2.010\). The terms fall towards 2.
  2. The sequence is decreasing. Put \(g(t)=e^t+e^{-t}\). For \(0<s<t\): \[g(t)-g(s)=(e^t-e^s)+(e^{-t}-e^{-s})=(e^t-e^s)\left(1-\tfrac1{e^{s+t}}\right)>0,\] since \(e^t>e^s\) and \(e^{s+t}>1\). (Check the factorisation by multiplying out: \(e^{-t}-e^{-s}=-\frac{e^t-e^s}{e^{s+t}}\).) So \(g\) is increasing on \((0,\infty)\). As \(n\) grows, \(t=\frac1n\) shrinks, so \(a_n=g(\frac1n)\) decreases.
  3. Supremum = maximum = the first term: \(\sup a_n=\max a_n=a_1=e+e^{-1}\).
  4. Infimum. Each \(a_n>2\): by the inequality \(\frac{u+v}2\ge\sqrt{uv}\) (equivalent to \((\sqrt u-\sqrt v)^2\ge0\)) with \(u=e^{1/n}\), \(v=e^{-1/n}\), \(uv=1\): \(a_n\ge2\), and "=" only if \(u=v\), i.e. \(\frac1n=0\), impossible. And \(a_n\to e^0+e^0=2\) (continuity of exp, Chapter 4 — or simply: the terms get as close to 2 as you like). So \(\inf a_n=2\), not attained: no minimum.
Exam questionKlausur WiSe18 · A5

Let \(f,g:D\to\mathbb R\) with \(D\subseteq\mathbb R\). (a) Show: if \(\sup_{x\in D}|f(x)-g(x)|=0\), then \(\sup(f+g)=\sup f+\sup g\). (b) Give an example with \(\sup(f+g)<\sup f+\sup g\). (c) Show that always \(\sup_{x\in D}(f(x)+g(x))\le\sup_{x\in D}f(x)+\sup_{x\in D}g(x)\).

Solution

Notation: \(\sup_{x\in D}f(x)\) means the supremum of the set of values \(\{f(x):x\in D\}\).

(c) first, because it is the heart. Take any \(x\in D\). Since \(\sup f\) is an upper bound of all values of \(f\), \(f(x)\le\sup f\); likewise \(g(x)\le\sup g\). Adding: \(f(x)+g(x)\le\sup f+\sup g\). This holds for every \(x\), so \(\sup f+\sup g\) is an upper bound of \(\{f(x)+g(x):x\in D\}\). The supremum is the least upper bound, hence \(\sup(f+g)\le\sup f+\sup g\). ∎

(b) \(D=[0,1]\), \(f(x)=x\), \(g(x)=-x\). Then \(f+g\equiv0\), so \(\sup(f+g)=0\), but \(\sup f+\sup g=1+0=1\). The two suprema are reached at different points (\(f\) at \(x=1\), \(g\) at \(x=0\)); the sum cannot use both.

(a) If \(f=g\), then \(\sup(f+g)=\sup(2f)=2\sup f=\sup f+\sup g\) (multiplying all values by 2 multiplies every upper bound, and hence the least one, by 2).

Where (a) starts: \(\sup_{x\in D}|f(x)-g(x)|=0\) means \(|f(x)-g(x)|\le0\) for every \(x\), and an absolute value is never negative, so \(f(x)=g(x)\) for all \(x\) — the exam's way of writing "\(f=g\)". Then the computation above applies.

The reusable pattern from (c): to show \(\sup S\le c\), show that \(c\) is an upper bound of \(S\). The exam does not assume \(f\) and \(g\) bounded; with the conventions \(\sup=\infty\) for an unbounded set the statements stay true, and in an exam it is worth one line to say which case you are in.

Exam questionKlausur WiSe19 · 1f

Determine \(A=\{z\in\mathbb C:\ z=|z|^2\}\).

Solution
  1. \(|z|^2\) is a real number \(\ge0\). So if \(z=|z|^2\), then \(z\) itself is real and \(\ge0\): \(z=x\) with \(x\ge0\), and then \(|z|=x\).
  2. The equation becomes \(x=x^2\), i.e. \(x(x-1)=0\), so \(x=0\) or \(x=1\).
  3. Check: \(0=|0|^2\) ✓, \(1=|1|^2\) ✓. So \(A=\{0,1\}\).

Alternative: write \(z=a+ib\); then \(a+ib=a^2+b^2\). Comparing imaginary parts gives \(b=0\), real parts \(a=a^2\).

Drill: sup, inf, max, min

Ten sets of the kind the exam uses. Decide the four values before you click — and, for each bound, which of the two steps of the recipe you would write down.

What you must be able to do after Chapter 1
  • For the exam: determine sup, inf, max, min of a set given by a formula, an inequality, or a sequence — with justification (upper bound + ε-step, or monotonicity + limit). Solve simple equations in ℂ by comparing real and imaginary parts.
  • For Chapter 2 you will use constantly: (1) negating statements with quantifiers; (2) Archimedes in the form "for every \(\varepsilon>0\) there is \(n\) with \(\frac1n<\varepsilon\)"; (3) the triangle inequality and its reverse to estimate \(|a_n-a|\); (4) the supremum axiom (it is what makes monotone bounded sequences converge, Satz 2.17).
  • For Chapters 3 and 5: computing with complex numbers, \(|z|\), \(\bar z\).

ΣSummary sheet

The whole chapter as one chain of reasoning:

  1. ℚ is a perfectly good field, but it has holes: √2 ∉ ℚ (Lemma 1.1), and \(\{a\in\mathbb Q:a^2\le2\}\) has no supremum in ℚ (Remark 1.8).
  2. ℝ closes these holes. That is the supremum axiom: nonempty + bounded above ⇒ sup exists.
  3. Filling the holes makes ℝ much bigger: ℚ is countable, ℝ is uncountable (Cantor, Lemma 1.3). So almost no real number is computable (Lemma 1.11).
  4. From the supremum axiom: ℝ is Archimedean (Satz 1.9) ⇒ for every \(a>0\) some \(\frac1n<a\) ⇒ ℚ is dense (Satz 1.10) ⇒ every real is approximated by fractions / decimals.
  5. ℝ is ordered; ℂ = ℝ² gains \(i^2=-1\) but loses the order (Lemma 1.4). In ℂ only \(|z|\) can be compared.
  6. To estimate distances, use the triangle inequality (1.12), its reverse (1.13), Cauchy–Schwarz (1.15) and the triangle inequality in ℝⁿ (1.14). These are the tools for all ε-proofs from Chapter 2 on.
ResultStatementTypical use
Supremum axiomnonempty, bounded above ⇒ sup M ∈ ℝ existsexistence of limits (Ch. 2: monotone sequences)
sup recipe(1) upper bound, (2) ∀ε ∃a∈M: a > s−εexam: find sup/inf/max/min
Satz 1.9∀a ∃n∈ℕ: a < n; so 1/n < a for a > 0choosing n₀ in ε-proofs
Satz 1.10between any a < b lies a rationalapproximation by fractions
Satz 1.12|x+y| ≤ |x|+|y|, = iff xy ≥ 0splitting |aₙ − a| into pieces
Satz 1.13 / Blatt 1.1a| |x|−|y| | ≤ |x−y|lower bounds; aₙ→a ⇒ |aₙ|→|a|
Blatt 1.1bxy ≤ x²/(2ε) + εy²/2splitting products
Satz 1.15|⟨x,y⟩| ≤ ‖x‖‖y‖, = iff parallelproving the triangle inequality in ℝⁿ
Blatt 1.2N1–N3; ‖·‖₁, ‖·‖₂, ‖·‖∞ are normschecking whether something is a norm
Blatt 1.3Re z = (z+z̄)/2, Im z = (z−z̄)/(2i), |z|² = z z̄computing with ℂ (Ch. 5: sin, cos)

Exam focus

Sup/inf/max/min of a set is the Chapter 1 exam task (five of nine exams, see "What the exam asks" above). Every exam set is in the explorer and fully worked on this page.

Exam checklist for sup/inf tasks
  1. Write out the first few elements, or rewrite the set as an interval (solve the inequality, e.g. \(|x-1|<1\iff 0<x<2\)).
  2. Monotone sequence? Then the first element is max (decreasing) or min (increasing), and the limit is the other bound.
  3. Justify the "not attained" bound with the ε-step (usually via Archimedes).
  4. Decide max/min: is the bound an element of the set?
  5. Unbounded ⇒ sup = ∞ / inf = −∞, and max/min don't exist.
