Analysis für Informatik · MA0902 · Chapter 6

DifferentiabilityDifferenzierbarkeit

The derivative answers: which straight line approximates \(f\) best near \(x_0\)? To say "best" precisely, the chapter first introduces the Landau symbols \(o\) and \(O\), the language of error terms that computer scientists know from running times. Then comes the definition of the derivative, the derivatives of exp, sin, cos from their series, and the rules (product, quotient, chain, inverse function) that let you differentiate everything else.

Source labels: Skript 6.7 = stated in your lecture script · Blatt 8 · 8.2 = exercise sheet · Klausur WiSe24 = old exam · Extra = my own addition (intuition, a proof the script leaves out, or an extra example). A § marks a whole section of the script (§6.2 = Section 6.2) rather than a numbered result (6.2 = Beispiel 6.2).

0The big picture

  1. 6.1 \(f=O(g)\): \(f\) is at most a constant times \(g\) (near \(x_0\)). \(f=o(g)\): \(f\) is negligible compared to \(g\) (\(\frac fg\to0\)).
  2. 6.2 \(f\) is differentiable at \(x_0\) if \(f(x)=f(x_0)+f'(x_0)(x-x_0)+o(|x-x_0|)\): the tangent line has an error that is small even compared to the distance \(|x-x_0|\). Equivalently, the difference quotient converges. Differentiable ⇒ continuous, not conversely (\(|x|\)).
  3. 6.3 Rules: sum, product, quotient, chain rule, derivative of the inverse. With them, \((x^n)'=nx^{n-1}\), \((a^x)'=a^x\ln a\), \((\ln x)'=\frac1x\), \((\tan)'=1+\tan^2\), \((\arctan)'=\frac1{1+x^2}\), …
Before you start
  • Limits of functions \(\lim_{x\to x_0}f(x)\): defined through sequences (Skript 5.18, Def. 4.11), including one-sided limits (§4.1). Every derivative is such a limit.
  • The key limits \(\frac{\sin x}x\to1\) and \(\frac{1-\cos x}{x^2/2}\to1\) (Lemma 5.19, §5.3) and the growth ladder log ≪ power ≪ exp (§5.2) — the Landau questions are the ladder in new notation. The third standard limit \(\frac{e^x-1}x\to1\) is not in Chapter 5: it is Beispiel 6.13 of this chapter (it is the derivative of exp at 0), even though the Chapter-5 exam solutions already lean on it.
  • Composition \(f\circ g\) and inverse functions (Basics, §5.1) — for the chain rule and the inverse rule.
  • \(\|x\|\) = length of a vector in \(\mathbb R^d\) (§1.3), used when \(x_0\in\mathbb R^d\).

6.1Landau symbols [Landau-Symbole]

DefinitionSkript 6.1, 6.5

Let \(f,g\) be functions and \(x_0\in\mathbb R^d\) (or \(x_0=\pm\infty\)).

  1. \(f(x)=O(g(x))\) for \(x\to x_0\) if there are \(\varepsilon>0\), \(C>0\) such that \(|f(x)|\le C|g(x)|\) for all \(x\) with \(\|x-x_0\|<\varepsilon\). For \(x_0=\infty\): for all \(x>M\) (some \(M\)). "\(f\) is bounded by \(g\) up to a constant."
  2. \(f(x)=o(g(x))\) for \(x\to x_0\) if \(\displaystyle\lim_{x\to x_0}\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}=0\). "\(f\) is negligible compared to \(g\)."
Reading "f = g + o(h)"Extra — notation used from 6.7 on

\(o(h(x))\) inside a formula stands for "some function that is \(o(h(x))\)". So \[f(x)=g(x)+o(h(x))\quad(x\to x_0)\qquad\text{means}\qquad r(x):=f(x)-g(x)\ \text{satisfies}\ \lim_{x\to x_0}\frac{r(x)}{h(x)}=0.\] Read it as "\(f\) equals \(g\) up to an error that is negligible compared to \(h\)". Example: \(e^x=1+x+o(x)\) for \(x\to0\), because \(\frac{e^x-1-x}x=\frac{e^x-1}x-1\to0\). Same for \(O\): \(\sin x=x+O(x^3)\) for \(x\to0\).

Always say "for x → what"

The same pair can behave oppositely at 0 and at ∞: \(x^3=o(x^2)\) for \(x\to0\), but \(x^2=o(x^3)\) for \(x\to\infty\). Near 0, higher powers are smaller; near ∞, lower powers are smaller. Also, "\(=\)" here is a one-way statement: \(x^2=O(x)\) for \(x\to0\) does not mean \(O(x)=x^2\).

ExamplesSkript 6.2, 6.3, 6.6
  • \(f=O(1)\) for \(x\to x_0\): \(f\) is bounded near \(x_0\). \(f=o(1)\): \(f(x)\to0\). And \(o(1)\) implies \(O(1)\).
  • \(f(h)=o(h)\) for \(h\to0\) implies \(f(h)=o(1)\), since \(f(h)=\frac{f(h)}h\cdot h\to0\cdot0\). But \(\sqrt h=o(1)\) and \(\sqrt h\neq o(h)\), since \(\frac{\sqrt h}h=\frac1{\sqrt h}\to\infty\).
  • \(f(x)=5x^7+2x^3-x^2\): for \(x\to0\), \(f=O(x^2)\): for \(|x|\le1\), \(|f(x)|\le5x^2+2x^2+x^2=8x^2\). For \(x\to\infty\), \(f=O(x^7)\): for \(x\ge1\), \(|f(x)|\le8x^7\).
Bemerkung · the practical testSkript 6.4

If \(\displaystyle\lim_{x\to x_0}\left|\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\right|=c\) exists (finite), then \(f=O(g)\).

Proof: near \(x_0\), \(\left|\frac fg\right|\le c+1\). So in practice: compute \(\lim\frac fg\). Limit 0 ⇒ \(o(g)\) (and \(O(g)\)). Finite limit ⇒ \(O(g)\). Limit ∞ ⇒ neither.

From the exercise sheetBlatt 8 · Abgabeaufgabe 8.2

Find simple \(g,h\) with \(f=o(g)\) and \(f=O(h)\).

fx₀O(h)o(g)why
\(5x^6+4x^5+2x^2\)0\(x^2\)\(x\)\(f/x^2\to2\); lowest power dominates near 0
same∞\(x^6\)\(x^7\)\(f/x^6\to5\); highest power dominates near ∞
\(x^3+3x^2-24x+28\)2\((x-2)^2\)\(x-2\)factor: \(f=(x-2)^2(x+7)\), \(f/(x-2)^2\to9\)
same−7\(x+7\)\(1\)\(f/(x+7)\to81\)
\(e^x-1\)0\(x\)\(1\)\(\frac{e^x-1}x\to1\)
\(e^x\)−∞\(e^x\)\(1\)\(e^x\to0\)

Lesson from (c), the third row: near a zero \(x_0\) of a polynomial, factor out \((x-x_0)\) as often as possible. The multiplicity of the zero gives the order.

From the exercise sheetBlatt 8 · Tutoriumsaufgabe 8.1 · calculating with o and O
  • Products are fine: \(f_j=o(g_j)\Rightarrow f_1f_2=o(g_1g_2)\); \(f_j=O(g_j)\Rightarrow f_1f_2=O(g_1g_2)\) (multiply the limits / the constants).
  • Sums need care: \(f_1+f_2=O(g_1+g_2)\) can fail because \(g_1+g_2\) may cancel: for \(x\to0\), \(x^2-x=O(-x)\) and \(x^2+x=O(x)\), but the sum \(2x^2\neq O(0)\). What is true: \(f_1+f_2=O(|g_1|+|g_2|)\) and similarly for \(o\).
Try it · o or O? Watch the ratio |f/g|

The plot shows \(\left|\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\right|\) as \(x\) approaches \(x_0\) (the horizontal axis counts how close you are, on a log scale). Ratio → 0 means \(o(g)\); bounded means \(O(g)\); → ∞ means neither.

Exam questionsWiSe20 3a · WiSe19 A3 · WiSe24 3a/3b · SoSe23 1g
WiSe20 3a: all \(p\) with \(\frac{3x^2\cos x}{x+\sin(x)/x}=o(x^p)\) for \(x\to0\)

Denominator: \(x+\frac{\sin x}x\to0+1=1\). Numerator \(\simeq3x^2\). So \(\frac{f(x)}{x^p}\simeq3x^{2-p}\to0\iff2-p>0\iff p<2\). (Check \(p=2\): the ratio → 3, which is \(O\) but not \(o\).) Note: the uploaded Musterlösung prints “\(p>2\)”. That contradicts the computation (for \(p>2\) the ratio \(3x^{2-p}\) blows up), so I believe it is a typo. Worth asking your tutor if you want certainty.

WiSe19 A3: all \(a\) with \((2x^7-x^2)e^x+x^5e^{-x}+e^{2x}=o(x^ae^{2x})\) for \(x\to\infty\)

Divide by \(x^ae^{2x}\): \((2x^{7-a}-x^{2-a})e^{-x}+x^{5-a}e^{-3x}+x^{-a}\). The first two terms → 0 for every \(a\) (exp beats every power). The last, \(x^{-a}\to0\iff a>0\). Answer: \(a\in(0,\infty)\).

WiSe24 3a: \(a\) with \(2e^x-a=o(1)\) for \(x\to0\); 3b: smallest \(a\) with \(200x^2+10^{-5}x^3+\sin x=O(x^a)\) for \(x\to\infty\)

3a: \(o(1)\) means "→ 0". \(2e^x\to2\), so \(a=2\).

3b: \(|\dots|\le Cx^3\) for large \(x\) (the tiny coefficient does not matter!), so \(a=3\) works. For \(a<3\), \(\frac{10^{-5}x^3}{x^a}\to\infty\), so it fails. Answer \(a=3\).

SoSe23 1g: \(n^2+2n\log n+24=o(n^2\log n)\)? \(3^{\log n}=O(n^3)\)?

First: divide, \(\frac1{\log n}+\frac2{n}+\frac{24}{n^2\log n}\to0\): true. Second: \(3^{\ln n}=e^{\ln3\cdot\ln n}=n^{\ln3}\approx n^{1.0986}\le n^3\): true. (Trick: \(a^{\ln b}=b^{\ln a}\).)

Extra — which log? The exam writes \(\log\) without a base, so the answer should not depend on one. It does not: in general \(3^{\log_b n}=n^{\log_b3}\) by the same rewriting, and \(\log_b3\le\log_23\approx1.585<3\) for every base \(b\ge2\), so \(3^{\log_b n}=O(n^3)\) either way. In the first statement the base cancels out of \(\frac1{\log n}\to0\) as well.

What you must know for 6.1: working with o and O.

6.2Differentiability [Differenzierbarkeit]

Goal: approximate \(f\) near \(x_0\) by a straight line that describes how \(f\) changes there.

DefinitionSkript 6.7

\(f:I\to\mathbb R\) on an open interval \(I\) is differentiable at \(x_0\in I\) if there is a number \(f'(x_0)\) such that the linearisation holds: \[f(x)=f(x_0)+f'(x_0)(x-x_0)+o(|x-x_0|)\qquad(x\to x_0).\] The line \(x\mapsto f(x_0)+f'(x_0)(x-x_0)\) is the tangent [Tangente], its slope \(f'(x_0)\) the derivative [Ableitung].

Equivalent: the difference quotientSkript §6.2, after 6.9

Divide the linearisation by \(x-x_0\) (for \(x\neq x_0\)): "the error \(f(x)-f(x_0)-f'(x_0)(x-x_0)\) is \(o(|x-x_0|)\)" means \(\frac{f(x)-f(x_0)-f'(x_0)(x-x_0)}{x-x_0}\to0\), i.e. \(\frac{f(x)-f(x_0)}{x-x_0}-f'(x_0)\to0\). (Dividing by \(|x-x_0|\) or by \(x-x_0\) makes no difference for "→ 0".) Now set \(x=x_0+h\). The definition is equivalent to \[f'(x_0)=\lim_{h\to0}\frac{f(x_0+h)-f(x_0)}{h}.\] \(\frac{f(x_0+h)-f(x_0)}h\) is the difference quotient [Differenzenquotient]: the slope of the secant through \((x_0,f(x_0))\) and \((x_0+h,f(x_0+h))\). The tangent is the limit of secants.

Definition 6.10 Skript 6.10: \(f\) is differentiable if it is differentiable at every point of its domain. Notation: \(f'(x_0)=\frac{df}{dx}(x_0)=Df(x_0)\).

Try it · secants approach the tangent
Try it · why the tangent is the best line (Lemma 6.8)

Every line through \((x_0,f(x_0))\) has an error that goes to 0. What singles out the tangent is that its error goes to 0 faster than the distance: the right plot shows \(\frac{\text{error}}{x-x_0}\). For the tangent it tends to 0; for any other slope \(a\) it tends to \(f'(x_0)-a\neq0\).

LemmaSkript 6.8, 6.9

6.8 The tangent approximates \(f\) better than any other line: another line \(a(x-x_0)+b\) has error \(O(1)\) if \(b\neq f(x_0)\), and error \(O(x-x_0)\) if \(b=f(x_0)\) but \(a\neq f'(x_0)\). Only the tangent achieves \(o(|x-x_0|)\).

Why: \(f(x)-[a(x-x_0)+b]=\big(f(x_0)-b\big)+\big(f'(x_0)-a\big)(x-x_0)+o(|x-x_0|)\). If \(b\neq f(x_0)\), the error tends to the nonzero constant \(f(x_0)-b\). If \(b=f(x_0)\) but \(a\neq f'(x_0)\), dividing by \(x-x_0\) gives \(f'(x_0)-a+o(1)\not\to0\). Extra That last line says slightly more than the script's \(O(x-x_0)\): the order is exact, because the quotient tends to the nonzero number \(f'(x_0)-a\), so the error is not \(o(|x-x_0|)\).

6.9 Differentiable at \(x_0\) ⇒ continuous at \(x_0\): let \(x\to x_0\) in the linearisation; everything after \(f(x_0)\) tends to 0.

Not conversely: \(|x|\) is continuous but not differentiable at 0 — the script states this without an argument. Extra Here is the argument: the difference quotient \(\frac{|h|}h\) is \(+1\) for \(h>0\) and \(-1\) for \(h<0\), so it has no limit (a corner has no single tangent).

Derivatives from the definitionSkript 6.11 – 6.14
  • 6.11 \((ax+b)'=a\): \(\frac{a(x_0+h)+b-ax_0-b}h=a\).
  • 6.12 \((x^2)'=2x\): \(\frac{(x_0+h)^2-x_0^2}h=\frac{2x_0h+h^2}h=2x_0+h\to2x_0\).
  • 6.13 \((e^x)'=e^x\): at 0, the series gives \(\frac{e^h-1}h=\frac1h\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{h^k}{k!}=1+h\,c(h)\) with \(c(h)=\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{h^j}{(j+2)!}\), and \(|c(h)|\le\sum_j\frac{|h|^j}{j!}=e^{|h|}\) because \((j+2)!\ge j!\). So \(|h\,c(h)|\le|h|e^{|h|}\to0\) and the quotient → 1. At any \(x\): \(\frac{e^{x+h}-e^x}h=e^x\cdot\frac{e^h-1}h\to e^x\) (functional equation).
  • 6.14 \((\sin)'=\cos\): addition theorem, \(\frac{\sin(x+h)-\sin x}h=\sin x\,\frac{\cos h-1}h+\cos x\,\frac{\sin h}h\to\sin x\cdot0+\cos x\cdot1\). Here \(\frac{\cos h-1}h=-\frac h2\cdot\frac{1-\cos h}{h^2/2}\to0\) (Lemma 5.19). Extra For cosine the script only says "analogously"; the computation is \(\frac{\cos(x+h)-\cos x}h=\cos x\,\frac{\cos h-1}h-\sin x\,\frac{\sin h}h\to-\sin x\), using \(\cos(x+h)=\cos x\cos h-\sin x\sin h\).
Try it · from slopes to the derivative function

Top: \(f\) with its tangent at \(x_0\). Bottom: the slope of that tangent is plotted as a point \((x_0,f'(x_0))\). Drag \(x_0\) across — the points trace out the graph of \(f'\). Where \(f\) rises, \(f'>0\); at a peak or valley, \(f'=0\).

Exam questionKlausur SoSe19 · Aufgabe 4

\(f:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R\) with (i) \(f(x+y)=f(x)f(y)\), (ii) \(f(0)=1\), (iii) \(f\) differentiable at 0 with \(f'(0)=1\). (a) Show with the definition that \(f\) is differentiable everywhere with \(f'=f\). (b)–(d) Show \(g(x)=f(x)e^{-x}\) is constant \(=1\), so \(f=\exp\).

Solution

(a) Exactly the argument for \(e^x\) above, using only (i)–(iii): \[\frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}h\overset{(i)}=f(x)\,\frac{f(h)-1}h\overset{(ii)}=f(x)\,\frac{f(h)-f(0)}h\to f(x)f'(0)=f(x).\] (b) Product rule: \(g'=f'e^{-x}-fe^{-x}=0\), so \(g\) is constant (Satz 7.13, Chapter 7). (c) \(g(0)=f(0)e^0=1\). (d) \(f(x)e^{-x}=1\iff f(x)=e^x\).

Moral: exp is the only differentiable function turning sums into products with \(f'(0)=1\).

From the exercise sheetBlatt 9 · Abgabeaufgabe 9.3 · SoSe21 A6

The sheet asks about two functions: (a) \(f(x)=2x^3\sin\frac1x\) for \(x\neq0\), \(f(0)=0\), and (b) \(f(x)=x^2\sin\frac1x\) for \(x\neq0\), \(f(0)=0\). Differentiable on ℝ? Is \(f'\) continuous?

Solution

Away from 0 use the rules. At 0 you must use the definition (the rules don't apply at the gluing point). Part (b) is the interesting one, so take it first:

(b) \(x^2\sin\frac1x\): \(\frac{f(h)-f(0)}h=h\sin\frac1h\to0\) (bounded × null sequence). So \(f'(0)=0\). For \(x\neq0\): \(f'(x)=2x\sin\frac1x-\cos\frac1x\). As \(x\to0\), the first term → 0, but \(\cos\frac1x\) has no limit (it is 0 at \(x=\frac1{\pi/2+\pi n}\), 1 at \(x=\frac1{2\pi n}\)). So \(f\) is differentiable everywhere, but \(f'\) is not continuous at 0.

(a) \(2x^3\sin\frac1x\): \(f'(0)=\lim2h^2\sin\frac1h=0\), and \(f'(x)=6x^2\sin\frac1x-2x\cos\frac1x\to0=f'(0)\): here \(f'\) is continuous. One power of \(x\) more is enough to tame the oscillation.

SoSe21 A6 asked the same about \(x^2\sin\frac1x\): "(i) \(f'\) continuous on ℝ" false; "(ii) \(f\) differentiable on ℝ" true; "(iii) \(f\) continuously differentiable on \(\mathbb R\setminus\{0\}\)" true.

What you must know for 6.2: the definitions of derivative and tangent; the derivatives of exp, sin and cos.

6.3Rules of differentiation [Ableitungsregeln]

SatzSkript 6.15

If \(f,g\) are differentiable at \(x\):

  1. \((cf)'=cf'\)
  2. \((f+g)'=f'+g'\) [Summenregel]
  3. \((fg)'=f'g+fg'\) [Produktregel]
  4. \(\left(\frac fg\right)'=\frac{gf'-fg'}{g^2}\) where \(g(x)\neq0\) [Quotientenregel]
Proof of the product rule

Add and subtract \(f(x)g(x+h)\) (the Chapter-1 trick once more): \[\frac{f(x+h)g(x+h)-f(x)g(x)}h=\frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}h\,g(x+h)+f(x)\,\frac{g(x+h)-g(x)}h\to f'(x)g(x)+f(x)g'(x).\] We used \(g(x+h)\to g(x)\), i.e. that \(g\) is continuous, which holds because \(g\) is differentiable (6.9). ∎

Proof of the quotient rule Extra — the script proves only the product rule

First \(\frac1g\): \(\frac1h\left(\frac1{g(x+h)}-\frac1{g(x)}\right)=-\frac{g(x+h)-g(x)}h\cdot\frac1{g(x+h)g(x)}\to-\frac{g'(x)}{g(x)^2}\) (again \(g(x+h)\to g(x)\neq0\), and \(g(x+h)\neq0\) for small \(h\) by continuity). Then the product rule for \(f\cdot\frac1g\): \[\Big(\frac fg\Big)'=f'\cdot\frac1g+f\cdot\Big(-\frac{g'}{g^2}\Big)=\frac{f'g-fg'}{g^2}.\ \ ∎\] Sum rule and constant factor: directly from the limit rules for the difference quotient.

Powers and polynomialsSkript 6.16, 6.17

\((x^n)'=nx^{n-1}\) (induction: base \(n=1\): \((x)'=1=1\cdot x^0\); step: \((x^{n+1})'=(x\cdot x^n)'=1\cdot x^n+x\cdot nx^{n-1}=(n+1)x^n\) by the product rule and the induction hypothesis). Hence \(\left(\sum a_kx^k\right)'=\sum ka_kx^{k-1}\). E.g. \((4x^{12}-7x^4+3x^3+7)'=48x^{11}-28x^3+9x^2\).

LemmaSkript 6.18

\((\tan)'=\frac{\cos\cdot\cos-\sin\cdot(-\sin)}{\cos^2}=\frac1{\cos^2x}=1+\tan^2x\) (quotient rule and \(\sin^2+\cos^2=1\)).

Satz · chain ruleSkript 6.19, 6.20 · Kettenregel

If \(g\) is differentiable at \(x_0\) and \(f\) at \(g(x_0)\), then \[(f\circ g)'(x_0)=f'(g(x_0))\cdot g'(x_0).\] "Outer derivative, evaluated at the inner function, times inner derivative."

Definition 6.19 (needed for the precise statement): \(x_0\) is an interior point [innerer Punkt] of \(A\subseteq\mathbb R\) if some interval \((x_0-\varepsilon,x_0+\varepsilon)\) lies completely in \(A\). Satz 6.20 requires \(x_0\) interior in the domain of \(g\) and \(g(x_0)\) interior in the domain of \(f\), so that both difference quotients can be formed from both sides. Example: 0 is an interior point of \((-1,1)\), but not of \([0,1]\).

Proof idea

Where \(g(x)\neq g(x_0)\), expand the quotient: \[\frac{f(g(x))-f(g(x_0))}{x-x_0}=\underbrace{\frac{f(g(x))-f(g(x_0))}{g(x)-g(x_0)}}_{\to f'(g(x_0))}\cdot\underbrace{\frac{g(x)-g(x_0)}{x-x_0}}_{\to g'(x_0)}.\] (The first factor converges because \(g(x)\to g(x_0)\), by continuity of \(g\).) Where \(g(x)=g(x_0)\), the quotient is 0; if this happens for \(x\) arbitrarily close to \(x_0\), then \(g'(x_0)=0\), so the claimed value \(f'(g(x_0))\,g'(x_0)\) is 0 as well. ∎

ExamplesSkript 6.21, 6.25
  • \(a^x=e^{x\ln a}\) for \(a>0\) (the script says so; \(\ln a\) needs it): outer \(e^u\), inner \(x\ln a\). \((a^x)'=e^{x\ln a}\ln a=a^x\ln a\), for every \(x\in\mathbb R\).
  • \(x^a=e^{a\ln x}\) on \((0,\infty)\), with \(a\neq0\) in Lemma 6.25 (for \(a=0\) the function is the constant 1 and the formula \(ax^{a-1}\) reads 0, which is still right): \((x^a)'=e^{a\ln x}\cdot\frac ax=ax^{a-1}\). Special cases \((\sqrt x)'=\frac1{2\sqrt x}\), \(\left(\frac1x\right)'=-\frac1{x^2}\), \(\left(\frac1{x^2}\right)'=-\frac2{x^3}\).
Satz · derivative of the inverseSkript 6.22

If \(f\) is bijective, differentiable at \(y_0\) with \(f'(y_0)\neq0\), then \(f^{-1}\) is differentiable at \(x_0=f(y_0)\) with \[(f^{-1})'(x_0)=\frac1{f'(f^{-1}(x_0))}.\]

Where it comes from: differentiate \(f^{-1}(f(y))=y\) with the chain rule: \((f^{-1})'(f(y))\cdot f'(y)=1\). Extra — what the script's proof does and does not do The script's proof assumes that \(f^{-1}\) is differentiable and only computes its value. To close that gap one writes the difference quotient of \(f^{-1}\) at \(x_0\) as the reciprocal of the difference quotient of \(f\) at \(y_0\) — legitimate because \(f\) is injective, so the denominator is nonzero — and then needs \(f^{-1}(x)\to f^{-1}(x_0)\) as \(x\to x_0\), i.e. continuity of \(f^{-1}\). Satz 5.3 supplies exactly that, but only under its own hypotheses (\(f\) continuous and strictly monotone on an interval), which is the situation of all the applications below. Geometrically: mirroring at \(y=x\) turns slope \(m\) into slope \(\frac1m\) — and a horizontal tangent (\(m=0\)) becomes vertical, which is why \(f'(y_0)\neq0\) is needed (e.g. \(\sqrt[3]x\) at 0).

ApplicationsSkript 6.23, 6.26, 6.27
  • \((\ln)'(x)=\frac1{\exp(\ln x)}=\frac1x\).
  • \((\arcsin)'(x)=\frac1{\cos(\arcsin x)}=\frac1{\sqrt{1-\sin^2(\arcsin x)}}=\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\) on \((-1,1)\) (cos ≥ 0 on \([-\frac\pi2,\frac\pi2]\), so \(\cos y=+\sqrt{1-\sin^2y}\)). For arccos: \((\arccos)'(x)=\frac1{-\sin(\arccos x)}=-\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\), since \(\sin\ge0\) on \([0,\pi]\).
  • \((\arctan)'(x)=\frac1{1+\tan^2(\arctan x)}=\frac1{1+x^2}\).
LemmaSkript 6.24

\(\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\left(1+\frac xn\right)^n=e^x\) for all \(x\in\mathbb R\) (for \(x=1\): this is how \(e\) was defined in Chapter 2).

Proof: take logs (ln is continuous): \(n\ln(1+\frac xn)=x\cdot\frac{\ln(1+\frac xn)-\ln1}{\frac xn}\to x\cdot(\ln)'(1)=x\), a difference quotient of ln at 1.

Method · differentiating a messy expressionExtra
  1. Simplify first (log rules, cancel, rewrite roots as powers). E.g. \(\log\frac1{1+e^x}=-\log(1+e^x)\).
  2. Identify the outermost operation: sum → sum rule; product → product rule; quotient → quotient rule; function of something → chain rule.
  3. Variable in the base and in the exponent (\(x^x\), \(x^{2x}\), \((1+x^3)^{\cos x}\))? Rewrite as \(e^{(\text{exponent})\cdot\ln(\text{base})}\) first.
  4. Check with a simple value or special case if you can.
From the exercise sheetsBlatt 8 · 8.3 (a)–(e) · Blatt 9 · 9.2 (a)–(i)
Blatt 8.3 (a)–(e), in the sheet's order
  • (a) \((8x^5+8x^3+12x^2+5)'=40x^4+24x^2+24x\) (Lemma 6.16 term by term).
  • (b) \((e^{2x^2+7x+3})'=(4x+7)e^{2x^2+7x+3}\) (chain rule).
  • (c) \(\left(\frac1x\right)'=\frac{x\cdot0-1\cdot1}{x^2}=-\frac1{x^2}\) on \(\mathbb R\setminus\{0\}\) (quotient rule).
  • (d) On \(\mathbb R\setminus\{-3\}\), using \(x^2+6x+9=(x+3)^2\): \[\left(\frac{2x^4+5x^3+x+2}{x^2+6x+9}\right)'=\frac{(x+3)^2(8x^3+15x^2+1)-2(x+3)(2x^4+5x^3+x+2)}{(x+3)^4}=\frac{4x^4+29x^3+45x^2-x-1}{(x+3)^3}.\] Cancel one factor \((x+3)\) before expanding, or the algebra gets unpleasant.
  • (e) \((x^5e^{-2x^2+3x+1})'=\big(5x^4+x^5(-4x+3)\big)e^{-2x^2+3x+1}=(-4x^6+3x^5+5x^4)e^{-2x^2+3x+1}\) (product + chain rule).
Blatt 9.2 (a)–(i), in the sheet's order — only (a)–(d) were to be handed in
  • (a) \((4\sin^2x+4\cos^2x)'=8\sin x\cos x-8\cos x\sin x=0\). Of course: the function is the constant 4.
  • (b) \((\tan x)'=\frac1{\cos^2x}\) on \((-\frac\pi2,\frac\pi2)\) — this is Lemma 6.18 above.
  • (c) \(\left(\sqrt{\frac{x+1}{x-1}}\right)'=\frac12\left(\frac{x+1}{x-1}\right)^{-1/2}\cdot\frac{-2}{(x-1)^2}=-\frac1{(x-1)^{3/2}\sqrt{x+1}}\) on \((1,\infty)\).
  • (d) \((x^2\sin(3x+2))'=2x\sin(3x+2)+3x^2\cos(3x+2)\).
  • (e) \(\left(xe^{1/x}\right)'=e^{1/x}+x\,e^{1/x}\left(-\frac1{x^2}\right)=e^{1/x}\left(1-\frac1x\right)\).
  • (f) \((\sin(e^{\cos x}))'=\cos(e^{\cos x})\cdot e^{\cos x}\cdot(-\sin x)\) (chain rule twice).
  • (g) \((\arccos)'(x)=\frac1{-\sin(\arccos x)}=-\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\) on \((-1,1)\) — the inverse-function rule, as in Beispiel 6.26 above.
  • (h) Variable exponent, so rewrite: \(x^{2x}=e^{2x\ln x}\), hence \((x^{2x})'=x^{2x}(2\ln x+2)=2x^{2x}(\ln x+1)\) on \((0,\infty)\).
  • (i) \((1+x^3)^{\cos x}=e^{\cos x\ln(1+x^3)}\), derivative \((1+x^3)^{\cos x}\left(-\sin x\ln(1+x^3)+\frac{3x^2\cos x}{1+x^3}\right)\) on \((-1,\infty)\).

(h) and (i) are the case the method box above warns about: a variable in the base and in the exponent must be rewritten with \(e\) and \(\ln\) before any rule applies.

Blatt 9.1: even and odd functions

\(f\) even: \(f(-x)=f(x)\); odd: \(f(-x)=-f(x)\). A polynomial with only even (odd) powers is even (odd). \(x^4-3\), \(\cos\), \(\sin(x^2)\) are even; \(\sin\), \(\tan\) are odd; \(x^3-3\) is neither. Derivative swaps parity: differentiate \(f(-x)=f(x)\) with the chain rule: \(-f'(-x)=f'(x)\), so even \(f\) ⇒ odd \(f'\), and odd \(f\) ⇒ even \(f'\).

Exam questionsKlausur WiSe24 4a · SoSe23 4b

WiSe24 4a: \(f(x)=\log\frac1{1+e^x}\). Simplify: \(f=-\log(1+e^x)\), so \(f'(x)=-\frac{e^x}{1+e^x}\).

SoSe23 4b: \(f(x)=\ln\ln x\): \(f'(x)=\frac1{\ln x}\cdot\frac1x=\frac1{x\ln x}\).

What you must know for 6.3: the sum, product, quotient and chain rules and the rule for the inverse function; the derivatives of important functions, in particular \(x^n,a^x,x^a,\tan,\arctan\).

Mixed exercises from the script [Kapitel 13]

Three true/false statements about \(o\) and \(O\) from the script’s exercise pool. The method is always the same: translate the symbol into a limit, then compute the limit.

Aufgabe 13.8Skript 13.8

True or false: \(\sin x\cos x\ln(1+x)=o(x)\) for \(x\to0\)?

Solution

Translate: the claim says \(\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sin x\cos x\ln(1+x)}x=0\). Split the quotient so that the known limit \(\frac{\sin x}x\to1\) (Lemma 5.19) appears: \[\frac{\sin x\cos x\ln(1+x)}x=\underbrace{\frac{\sin x}x}_{\to1}\cdot\underbrace{\cos x}_{\to1}\cdot\underbrace{\ln(1+x)}_{\to\ln1=0}\to1\cdot1\cdot0=0.\] True.

Aufgabe 13.9Skript 13.9

True or false: \(x^xe^{-x^2}=O(e^{-x})\) for \(x\to\infty\)?

Solution

Translate: the claim says \(\frac{x^xe^{-x^2}}{e^{-x}}=x^xe^{-x^2+x}\) stays bounded for large \(x\). Write everything as one exponential — the standard move when a variable sits in the exponent (Chapter 5): \(x^x=e^{x\ln x}\), so \[x^xe^{-x^2}e^{x}=e^{\,x\ln x-x^2+x}.\] In the exponent, \(-x^2\) dominates \(x\ln x\) and \(x\) (again \(\frac{\ln x}x\to0\)), so the exponent tends to \(-\infty\) and the whole expression tends to 0. A function with limit 0 is certainly bounded near \(\infty\): true.

Note the direction of the definitions: \(o\) (limit 0) is stronger than \(O\) (bounded quotient). Having shown the limit is 0 we have even proved \(x^xe^{-x^2}=o(e^{-x})\).

Aufgabe 13.10Skript 13.10

True or false: \(x^5(\ln x)^{12}=o(x^4)\) for \(x\downarrow0\)?

Solution

Translate: \(\lim_{x\downarrow0}\frac{x^5(\ln x)^{12}}{x^4}=\lim_{x\downarrow0}x(\ln x)^{12}\stackrel?=0\). Substitute \(y=\frac1x\), so \(y\to\infty\) and \(\ln x=-\ln y\), hence \((\ln x)^{12}=(\ln y)^{12}\) (even power): \[x(\ln x)^{12}=\frac{(\ln y)^{12}}y\to0\] by Satz 5.8 (\(y\) beats every power of the logarithm). True.

\(x\downarrow0\) means the one-sided limit from the right, which is the only side where \(\ln x\) is defined.

!What the exam asks from Chapter 6

Chapter 6 appears in six of the nine exams. Landau items are answer-only and quick; derivatives show up everywhere later (curve sketching, Taylor, ODEs), so fluency matters more than any single task.

TypeExam tasksWhat earns the points
Landau: find all p / smallest a / true–falseWiSe20 3a, WiSe19 A3, WiSe24 3a–b, SoSe23 1gtranslate into \(\lim\frac fg\); growth ladder; note the direction \(x\to0\) vs \(x\to\infty\)
Compute a derivativeWiSe24 4a, SoSe23 4bsimplify first (log rules), then chain/product rule
Differentiability from the definitionSoSe19 A4, SoSe21 A6at the special point use the difference quotient; elsewhere the rules
Flagged: official solution

WiSe20 3a: the Musterlösung prints \(p>2\); the computation gives \(p<2\) (see the solution above).

Extra Beyond these three types, a chapter-6 step is buried inside many later tasks, and that is where most of the marks for this chapter actually are: every curve sketch needs \(f'\) and \(f''\) (WiSe23 A3, WiSe18 A1, WiSe20 A4, §7.7); SoSe21 4b needs the rewrite \(x^x=e^{x\ln x}\) before it can be differentiated at all; l’Hospital (WiSe18 1a, WiSe24 1c) differentiates numerator and denominator separately; WiSe22 4b differentiates an integral with the fundamental theorem; and the several-variable tasks (WiSe19 1g, WiSe24 5a, SoSe23 A3) are these same rules applied one variable at a time.

Drill: derivatives and Landau

Ten quick items in exam style. Do them on paper first, then check.

What you must be able to do after Chapter 6
  • For the exam: the three task types above, and the derivative table in the summary by heart.
  • For Chapter 7: \(f'(x_0)=0\) at interior extrema, the mean value theorem, monotonicity from the sign of \(f'\), l’Hospital (which needs \(f'\), \(g'\)).
  • For Chapter 8: antiderivatives are derivatives read backwards; substitution is the chain rule backwards, integration by parts the product rule backwards.
  • For Chapter 10: Taylor's formula \(f(a+h)=\sum\frac{f^{(k)}(a)}{k!}h^k+o(h^n)\) extends the linearisation \(f(a)+f'(a)h+o(h)\) — the \(o\)-notation of §6.1 is used throughout.
  • For Chapters 11–12: partial derivatives are ordinary derivatives in one variable; total differentiability \(f(x+h)=f(x)+Df(x)h+o(\|h\|)\) is the same definition in \(\mathbb R^n\); checking an ODE solution means differentiating it.

ΣSummary sheet

f(x)f′(x)f(x)f′(x)
\(x^a\)\(ax^{a-1}\)\(\sin x\)\(\cos x\)
\(e^x\)\(e^x\)\(\cos x\)\(-\sin x\)
\(a^x\)\(a^x\ln a\)\(\tan x\)\(1+\tan^2x=\frac1{\cos^2x}\)
\(\ln x\)\(\frac1x\)\(\arcsin x\)\(\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\)
\(\ln|x|\) Extra\(\frac1x\) (\(x\neq0\))\(\arctan x\)\(\frac1{1+x^2}\)

Domains: \(x^a\) and \(\ln x\) on \((0,\infty)\), \(a^x\) for \(a>0\) on all of \(\mathbb R\), \(\arcsin\) on \((-1,1)\), \(\tan\) away from \(\frac\pi2+k\pi\). The \(\ln|x|\) row is not in Chapter 6 — the script only does \(\ln\) on \((0,\infty)\) (Beispiel 6.23) — but it is the antiderivative you need in Chapter 8, and it follows from the chain rule: for \(x<0\), \((\ln(-x))'=\frac{-1}{-x}=\frac1x\).

  1. \(f=O(g)\): \(|f|\le C|g|\) near \(x_0\). \(f=o(g)\): \(f/g\to0\). Check via \(\lim|f/g|\). Near 0 the lowest power dominates, near ∞ the highest.
  2. \(f'(x_0)=\lim_{h\to0}\frac{f(x_0+h)-f(x_0)}h\iff f(x)=f(x_0)+f'(x_0)(x-x_0)+o(|x-x_0|)\).
  3. Differentiable ⇒ continuous. \(|x|\) at 0: continuous, not differentiable. \(x^2\sin\frac1x\): differentiable, \(f'\) not continuous.
  4. At gluing points of piecewise functions, use the definition (difference quotient), not the rules.
  5. Rules: \((fg)'=f'g+fg'\), \(\left(\frac fg\right)'=\frac{f'g-fg'}{g^2}\), \((f\circ g)'=f'(g)\,g'\), \((f^{-1})'=\frac1{f'(f^{-1})}\).

?Self-test

Analysis für Informatik · MA0902 · Chapter 7

Applications of the DerivativeAnwendungen der Ableitung

This chapter turns derivatives into information about functions: where the extrema are, where the function increases, how it curves, and how to compute tricky limits (l’Hospital). The engine behind all of it is one theorem, the mean value theorem. The chapter ends with the full recipe for a curve sketch [Kurvendiskussion], a guaranteed exam task.

Source labels: Skript 7.4 = stated in your lecture script · Blatt 10 · 10.2 = exercise sheet · Klausur WiSe23 = old exam · Extra = my own addition (intuition, a proof the script leaves out, or an extra example). A § marks a whole section of the script (§7.7 = Section 7.7, curve sketching) rather than a numbered result (7.7 = Definition 7.7).

0The big picture

  1. 7.1 At an interior local extremum, \(f'=0\) (necessary, not sufficient).
  2. 7.2 Mean value theorem: the average slope \(\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}\) is attained as a derivative \(f'(\xi)\) somewhere in between. This is the bridge from "derivative info" to "function info".
  3. 7.3 Consequences: \(f'>0\) ⇒ strictly increasing; \(f'=0\) ⇒ constant; sign change of \(f'\) ⇒ extremum; inequalities like \(1+x\le e^x\).
  4. 7.4 l’Hospital: for "\(\frac00\)" or "\(\frac\infty\infty\)", replace \(\frac fg\) by \(\frac{f'}{g'}\).
  5. 7.5–7.6 Higher derivatives; \(f''\ge0\) ⇔ convex; \(f'(x_0)=0\) and \(f''(x_0)>0\) ⇒ local minimum.
  6. 7.7 Curve sketching puts it all together.
Before you start
  • Derivatives and all rules (§6.3) — every section here computes \(f'\) or \(f''\).
  • "A continuous function on \([a,b]\) attains max and min" (Satz 4.29, §4.4) — the first line of the proof of Rolle's theorem.
  • "Limits keep ≤" (Satz 2.13) and one-sided limits (§4.1) — for the necessary condition \(f'=0\).
  • Growth rates and the key limits (§5.2, §5.3) — boundary behaviour in curve sketches, and an alternative to l’Hospital.

7.1Extrema [Extrema]

DefinitionSkript 7.1

\(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb R\) has at \(x_0\) a

  • global maximum [globales Maximum] if \(f(x_0)\ge f(x)\) for all \(x\in[a,b]\);
  • local maximum [lokales Maximum] if there is \(\varepsilon>0\) with \(f(x_0)\ge f(x)\) for all \(x\in(x_0-\varepsilon,x_0+\varepsilon)\cap[a,b]\).

Minima analogously. Maxima and minima are extrema; with strict inequalities (for \(x\neq x_0\)) they are strict.

In words: global = best value on the whole domain; local = best value in some small window around \(x_0\). Every global extremum is also a local one; a function can have many local maxima but only one global maximum value. (Skript Figure 6: several local maxima, one global.)

Lemma · necessary conditionSkript 7.2

If \(f\) is differentiable on \((a,b)\) and has a local extremum at an interior point \(x_0\in(a,b)\), then \(f'(x_0)=0\).

Proof

Say local max: \(f(x_0+h)-f(x_0)\le0\) for small \(|h|\). Divide by \(h\): the difference quotient is \(\le0\) for \(h>0\) and \(\ge0\) for \(h<0\). Its limit \(f'(x_0)\) is therefore both \(\le0\) and \(\ge0\) (Satz 2.13), so \(=0\). ∎

Two classic traps
  • Not sufficient: \(f(x)=x^3\) has \(f'(0)=0\) but no extremum at 0 (it increases through 0). Skript 7.3
  • Boundary points are not covered: \(x^2\) on \([-1,1]\) has maxima at \(\pm1\) with \(f'(\pm1)=\pm2\neq0\). Always check the endpoints separately.
Procedure for the extrema of a differentiable f on [a, b]Skript §7.1 · Vorgehensweise
  1. Find the zeros of \(f'\) in \((a,b)\) (the critical points).
  2. Throw out the zeros that are not extrema (sign of \(f'\) does not change, or \(f''\) test).
  3. Check the endpoints \(a,b\): does \(f\) have a local max or min there?
  4. The largest local max is the global max, the smallest local min the global min.

Extra — the two cases the script's recipe does not mention The script states the recipe for a closed interval \([a,b]\). If the domain is open or unbounded, step 3 has no endpoint values to read off: compute the limits at the open ends instead. And if such a limit is \(+\infty\) (resp. \(-\infty\)), then there is no global maximum (resp. minimum) on that side, however large the local ones are — that is exactly what settles WiSe23 A3 and SoSe21 4b below.

What you must know for 7.1: local and global min/max; \(f'(x)=0\) is necessary for a local extremum of a differentiable function; finding local and global extrema.

7.2The mean value theorem [Mittelwertsatz]

SatzSkript 7.4

Let \(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb R\) be continuous on \([a,b]\) and differentiable on \((a,b)\). Then there is \(\xi\in(a,b)\) with \[\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}=f'(\xi).\]

Picture: some tangent is parallel to the secant. Car version: if you drove 100 km in 1 hour, at some moment your speedometer showed exactly 100 km/h. Order in the script: Rolle (7.5) and the generalised MVT (7.6) are proved first; the MVT is the special case \(g(x)=x\).

Try it · the tangent parallel to the secant
Korollar · Rolle's theoremSkript 7.5 · Satz von Rolle

If additionally \(f(a)=f(b)\), there is \(\xi\in(a,b)\) with \(f'(\xi)=0\).

Proof

If \(f\) is constant, any \(\xi\) works (\(f'=0\) everywhere). Otherwise \(f\) attains max and min on the compact \([a,b]\) (Satz 4.29), and they differ. Since \(f(a)=f(b)\), at least one of them is attained at an interior point \(\xi\). There \(f'(\xi)=0\) (Lemma 7.2). ∎

Satz · generalised MVTSkript 7.6

Let \(f,g\) be continuous on \([a,b]\), differentiable on \((a,b)\), with \(g'\neq0\) on \((a,b)\). Then \(g(a)\neq g(b)\) and there is \(\xi\in(a,b)\) with \[\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{g(b)-g(a)}=\frac{f'(\xi)}{g'(\xi)}.\]

Proof (reduce to Rolle)

\(g(a)\neq g(b)\): otherwise Rolle gives \(g'(\xi)=0\). Now tilt \(f\) so that both ends are level: \[F(x)=f(x)-\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{g(b)-g(a)}\,(g(x)-g(a)).\] Then \(F(a)=F(b)=f(a)\). Rolle: \(0=F'(\xi)=f'(\xi)-\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{g(b)-g(a)}g'(\xi)\). Divide by \(g'(\xi)\neq0\). The ordinary MVT is the case \(g(x)=x\). ∎

Exam questions using the MVTWiSe19 2b · WiSe22 4a · WiSe18 A4b · SoSe23 A4

WiSe19 2b: "State the mean value theorem." Learn the statement with both hypotheses (continuous on \([a,b]\), differentiable on \((a,b)\)) and \(\xi\in(a,b)\).

WiSe22 4a: \(f\) continuous on \([a,b]\), differentiable on \((a,b)\), \(f(a)<f(b)\). Show \(f'(\xi)>0\) for some \(\xi\).

MVT: \(f'(\xi)=\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}\), numerator and denominator \(>0\). Done in one line.

WiSe18 A4b: \(f\in C^1(\mathbb R)\) ⇒ \(f\) is Lipschitz on \([a,b]\)

For \(x,y\in[a,b]\), MVT: \(f(x)-f(y)=f'(z)(x-y)\) with \(z\) between them. \(f'\) is continuous on the compact \([a,b]\), so \(L:=\max_{[a,b]}|f'|\) exists (Satz 4.29). Hence \(|f(x)-f(y)|\le L|x-y|\).

SoSe23 A4: MVT for \(f(x)=\ln\ln x\) on \([n,n+1]\) ⇒ \(\sum_{n\ge2}\frac1{n\ln n}\) diverges

\(f'(x)=\frac1{x\ln x}\). MVT: \(\ln\ln(n+1)-\ln\ln n=\frac1{\xi\ln\xi}\) for some \(\xi\in(n,n+1)\). Since \(g(x)=x\ln x\) is increasing for \(x>\frac1e\) (\(g'=\ln x+1\)), \(\frac1{\xi\ln\xi}<\frac1{n\ln n}\). So \[\sum_{n=2}^k\frac1{n\ln n}>\sum_{n=2}^k\left(\ln\ln(n+1)-\ln\ln n\right)=\ln\ln(k+1)-\ln\ln2\to\infty\] (telescoping!). A slick divergence proof; Chapter 9 gets it again with the integral test.

WiSe20 A6 / SoSe21 A5: fixed-point iteration \(x_{n+1}=f(x_n)\) with \(\alpha=\sup|f'|\)

MVT: \(|x_{n+1}-x_n|=|f(x_n)-f(x_{n-1})|=|f'(z)||x_n-x_{n-1}|\le\alpha|x_n-x_{n-1}|\). If \(\alpha<1\), the steps shrink geometrically and the sequence converges (Extra: then \(|x_{n+1}-x_n|\le\alpha^n|x_1-x_0|\), so \(\sum(x_{k+1}-x_k)\) converges absolutely by comparison with the geometric series, and \(x_n=x_0+\sum_{k<n}(x_{k+1}-x_k)\) converges.) But \(\alpha<1\) is not necessary: \(f(x)=x^2\), \(|x_0|<1\) gives \(x_n=x_0^{2^n}\to0\) although \(f'\) is unbounded. (WiSe20 answer: (ii).) In SoSe21 A5, \(f(x)=\frac x4+\frac{\cos x}3\): \(|f'(x)|=\left|\frac14-\frac{\sin x}3\right|\le\frac14+\frac13=\frac7{12}<1\), so the sequence converges for every \(x_0\), always to the same limit, the unique fixed point (answer (i)).

What you must know for 7.2: the mean value theorem, the generalised MVT and Rolle's theorem, and how to apply them.

7.3Monotonicity [Monotonie]

Recall · monotone functionsSkript 7.7, 7.8

\(f\) is (monotonically) increasing if \(x\le y\Rightarrow f(x)\le f(y)\), strictly increasing if \(x<y\Rightarrow f(x)<f(y)\); decreasing analogously (Def. 7.7 = Def. 4.12). Examples (7.8): \(x\) strictly increasing; a constant is both increasing and decreasing; \(x^2\) strictly increasing on \([0,\infty)\), strictly decreasing on \((-\infty,0]\).

Satz · monotonicity criterionSkript 7.9 · Monotoniekriterium

Let \(f\) be continuous on \([a,b]\), differentiable on \((a,b)\).

  1. \(f'>0\) on \((a,b)\) ⇒ \(f\) strictly increasing on \([a,b]\).
  2. \(f'<0\) on \((a,b)\) ⇒ strictly decreasing.
  3. \(f'\ge0\) on \((a,b)\) \(\iff\) increasing.
  4. \(f'\le0\) on \((a,b)\) \(\iff\) decreasing.
Proof of (a)

For \(x<y\): MVT gives \(\frac{f(y)-f(x)}{y-x}=f'(\xi)>0\), and \(y-x>0\), so \(f(y)>f(x)\).

(b)–(d): "⇒" in (c) is the same argument with \(f'(\xi)\ge0\). "⇐" in (c): if \(f\) is increasing, then for \(h>0\) the difference quotient \(\frac{f(x+h)-f(x)}h\ge0\), and its limit \(f'(x)\ge0\) (Satz 2.13). (b) and (d) follow by applying (a) and (c) to \(-f\). ∎

(a) has no converse: \(x^3\) is strictly increasing on \([-1,1]\), but \(f'(0)=0\). Example 7.10: \(\ln\) is strictly increasing because \((\ln)'(x)=\frac1x>0\) (we knew it already as the inverse of exp).

Lemma · two inequalities you should knowSkript 7.11
\[1+x\le e^x\ \ (x\in\mathbb R),\qquad\ln(1+x)\le x\ \ (x>-1),\]

each with equality only at \(x=0\).

Proof (the standard technique for proving inequalities)

Let \(f(x)=e^x-1-x\), \(f'(x)=e^x-1\): negative for \(x<0\), positive for \(x>0\). So \(f\) decreases then increases: global minimum at 0, where \(f(0)=0\). Hence \(f\ge0\), with equality only at 0. Apply the increasing function ln to \(1+x\le e^x\) to get the second inequality. ∎

Method: to prove \(g\le h\), study \(f=h-g\): find its minimum with \(f'\) and show it is \(\ge0\).

Satz · sufficient criterion for extremaSkript 7.12

\(f\) differentiable on \((a,b)\), \(f'(x_0)=0\). If \(f'\ge0\) left of \(x_0\) and \(f'\le0\) right of it, then \(x_0\) is a (global on \((a,b)\)) maximum; for the reverse sign pattern, a minimum. Restricted to \((x_0-\varepsilon,x_0+\varepsilon)\): local extremum. In short: a sign change of \(f'\) from + to − means a max, from − to + a min.

Proof

Take the maximum case. On \((a,x_0)\) we have \(f'\ge0\), so \(f\) is increasing there by the monotonicity criterion (Satz 7.9(c)); on \((x_0,b)\) we have \(f'\le0\), so \(f\) is decreasing. Increasing up to \(x_0\) gives \(f(x)\le f(x_0)\) for every \(x<x_0\); decreasing after \(x_0\) gives \(f(x)\le f(x_0)\) for every \(x>x_0\). Together \(f(x)\le f(x_0)\) on all of \((a,b)\), which is the definition of a maximum there. (Both halves also use that \(f\) is continuous at \(x_0\) itself, which holds because \(f\) is differentiable, Lemma 6.9.) For the minimum, apply this to \(-f\). ∎

Note what the criterion does not need: no second derivative. That is why it still works at points where \(f''(x_0)=0\) and the \(f''\)-test below says nothing.

Method · the sign chart of f′Extra — how to find "the sign of f′" in practice
  1. Factor \(f'\) as far as possible and drop factors that are always positive (\(e^{\dots}\), \(x^2+1\), squares away from their zero).
  2. Mark the zeros (and the points where \(f'\) is undefined) on the number line. Between two consecutive marks the sign of a continuous \(f'\) cannot change (IVT!).
  3. Determine the sign on each piece: from the signs of the factors, or by plugging in one test point.
  4. Read off: + means increasing, − decreasing; + → − is a max, − → + a min, no change means no extremum.

Example \(f'(x)=(3-x)x^2e^{-x}\) (WiSe23 A3): \(e^{-x}>0\) always; \(x^2>0\) except at 0; \(3-x\) is + left of 3, − right of 3.

\((-\infty,0)\)0\((0,3)\)3\((3,\infty)\)
\(x^2\)+0+++
\(3-x\)+++0−
\(f'\)+0+0−
\(f\)↗no extremum↗max↘
Satz · f′ = 0 ⇔ constantSkript 7.13, 7.14

On an interval: \(f'=0\iff f\) is constant. (MVT: \(f(y)-f(x)=f'(\xi)(y-x)=0\).) Hence if \(f'=g'\), then \(f=g+c\).

The interval matters Extra: \(f(x)=\text{sign}(x)\) on \(\mathbb R\setminus\{0\}\) has \(f'=0\) but is not constant. This is why antiderivatives are unique only "up to a constant" on an interval (Chapter 8).

From the exercise sheetBlatt 10 · Abgabeaufgabe 10.4

(a) \(f'=0\) on an interval ⇒ \(f\) constant (the MVT proof above). (b) If \(f'=cf\) on an open interval, then \(f(x)=be^{cx}\).

Solution (b)

Consider \(g(x)=f(x)e^{-cx}\). Product rule: \(g'=f'e^{-cx}-cfe^{-cx}=(f'-cf)e^{-cx}=0\). By (a), \(g=b\) is constant, so \(f=be^{cx}\). This is the first differential equation of the course (Chapter 12), the same trick as SoSe19 A4.

Exam questions · where is f increasing?Klausur WiSe22 · 4b · SoSe23 · A4c
WiSe22 4b: where is \(f(x)=\int_0^x(t-1)^9e^{-t}\,dt\) (on \((0,\infty)\)) strictly increasing, where strictly decreasing?

Do not compute the integral. The fundamental theorem (Satz 8.6, §8.3) gives the derivative directly: \[f'(x)=(x-1)^9e^{-x}.\] Now the sign chart: \(e^{-x}>0\) always, and \((x-1)^9\) has the sign of \(x-1\) (an odd power keeps the sign). So \(f'<0\) on \((0,1)\) and \(f'>0\) on \((1,\infty)\), and the monotonicity criterion 7.9(a),(b) gives: \(f\) is strictly decreasing on \((0,1]\), strictly increasing on \([1,\infty)\), with a global minimum at \(x=1\).

This is the cleanest illustration of why §7.3 and Chapter 8 belong together: all the monotonicity information sits in \(f'\), and the fundamental theorem hands you \(f'\) for free.

SoSe23 A4c: monotonicity of \(g(x)=x\ln x\) on \((0,\infty)\)

\(g'(x)=\ln x+1\), which is \(<0\) for \(x<\frac1e\) and \(>0\) for \(x>\frac1e\). So \(g\) is strictly decreasing on \((0,\frac1e]\) and strictly increasing on \([\frac1e,\infty)\), with minimum \(g(\frac1e)=-\frac1e\).

The rest of SoSe23 A4 uses exactly this: because \(x\ln x\) is increasing beyond \(\frac1e\), \(\frac1{\xi\ln\xi}<\frac1{n\ln n}\) for \(\xi>n\ge2\) — the step the divergence proof above needs.

What you must know for 7.3: the definition of monotonicity and the monotonicity criterion; determining monotonicity and extrema of a given function.

7.4Computing limits: l’Hospital [Regel von l’Hospital]

SatzSkript 7.15

Let \(f,g\) be differentiable on \((a,b)\) (\(a,b\) may be \(\pm\infty\)), \(g'(x)\neq0\), and \(x_0\in\{a,b\}\). Suppose

  1. \(\lim_{x\to x_0}f=0=\lim_{x\to x_0}g\) ("\(\frac00\)"), or
  2. \(\lim f=\infty=\lim g\) ("\(\frac\infty\infty\)").

If \(\displaystyle\lim_{x\to x_0}\frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}\) exists, then \(\displaystyle\lim_{x\to x_0}\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}=\lim_{x\to x_0}\frac{f'(x)}{g'(x)}\).

Proof of case (a) at a finite endpoint

Extend \(f,g\) continuously by \(f(a)=g(a)=0\). For \(x\in(a,b)\), the generalised MVT gives \(\xi\in(a,x)\) with \[\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}=\frac{f(x)-f(a)}{g(x)-g(a)}=\frac{f'(\xi)}{g'(\xi)}.\] As \(x\to a\), also \(\xi\to a\) (squeeze), so the right side tends to \(\lim\frac{f'}{g'}\). ∎

The script proves only this case; the "\(\frac\infty\infty\)" case and \(x_0=\pm\infty\) are used without proof (the latter reduces to the former by substituting \(x=\frac1t\)).

Check before you differentiate
  • Only for "\(\frac00\)" or "\(\frac\infty\infty\)". \(\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\cos x}{x+1}=1\), but "l’Hospital" would wrongly give \(\frac{-\sin0}1=0\).
  • Differentiate numerator and denominator separately. This is not the quotient rule.
  • Other forms (\(0\cdot\infty\), \(\infty-\infty\), \(1^\infty\), \(0^0\)) must first be rewritten as a quotient (common denominator; or take ln).
Try it · f/g and f′/g′ approach the same value

Near \(x_0\), the quotient \(\frac fg\) (blue) and the quotient of derivatives \(\frac{f'}{g'}\) (green dashed) run into the same limit — that is l’Hospital. The last example is the trap: not "\(\frac00\)", and the two curves go to different values.

ExamplesSkript 7.16 – 7.18
  • 7.16 \(\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sin x}x=\lim\frac{\cos x}1=1\).
  • 7.17 \(\lim_{x\to0}\left(\frac1{\sin x}-\frac1x\right)=\lim\frac{x-\sin x}{x\sin x}\overset{\frac00}=\lim\frac{1-\cos x}{\sin x+x\cos x}\overset{\frac00}=\lim\frac{\sin x}{2\cos x-x\sin x}=\frac02=0\) (twice; first a common denominator).
  • 7.18 \(\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{\ln x}{x^\alpha}=\lim\frac{1/x}{\alpha x^{\alpha-1}}=\lim\frac1{\alpha x^\alpha}=0\) for \(\alpha>0\). Consequence: \(\frac{\ln\ln n}{(\ln n)^\alpha}\to0\).
From the exercise sheetBlatt 11 · Abgabeaufgabe 11.3
(a) \(\lim_{x\to\infty}\frac{5^x}{x^2}\), (b) \(\lim_{x\to0}\frac{\sin x-x\cos x}{x\sin x}\), (c) \(\lim_{x\to\infty}x\ln(1+\frac1x)\), (d*) \(\lim_{x\downarrow0}(\sin x)^{1/\ln x}\)

(a) "\(\frac\infty\infty\)" twice: \(\frac{5^x\ln5}{2x}\to\frac{5^x(\ln5)^2}2\to\infty\).

(b) "\(\frac00\)": \(\frac{x\sin x}{x\cos x+\sin x}\), again "\(\frac00\)": \(\frac{x\cos x+\sin x}{2\cos x-x\sin x}\to\frac02=0\).

(c) "\(\infty\cdot0\)" → rewrite \(\frac{\ln(1+1/x)}{1/x}\) ("\(\frac00\)"): \(\frac{\frac1{1+1/x}\cdot(-\frac1{x^2})}{-\frac1{x^2}}=\frac x{x+1}\to1\).

(d*) "\(0^0\)": take ln: \(\frac{\ln\sin x}{\ln x}\) ("\(\frac{-\infty}{-\infty}\)") → \(\frac{\cos x/\sin x}{1/x}=\cos x\cdot\frac x{\sin x}\to1\). So the limit is \(e^1=e\).

Exam questionsWiSe24 1c · WiSe18 1a · WiSe20 3b
WiSe24 1c: \(\lim_{x\to0}\frac{x^4}{(1-\cos x)^2}\)

Official solution with Landau: \(1-\cos x=\frac{x^2}2(1+o(1))\), so \((1-\cos x)^2=\frac{x^4}4(1+o(1))\) and the quotient → 4. (l’Hospital also works but needs four rounds; the \(o\)-expansion is much faster. Cf. Lemma 5.19.)

WiSe18 1a: \(\lim_{x\to\infty}x^2e^{-x}\) and \(\lim_{x\to-\infty}x^2e^{-x}\)

\(x\to\infty\): \(\frac{x^2}{e^x}\) "\(\frac\infty\infty\)", l’Hospital twice → \(\frac2{e^x}\to0\). \(x\to-\infty\): both factors → ∞, no quotient issue: \(\infty\).

WiSe20 3b: \(\lim_{x\to0}\ln(x^{-1})\,(e^x-1)\) (only the result counted: 0)

Which limit is meant? \(\ln(x^{-1})\) needs \(x>0\), so "\(x\to0\)" can only be the right-hand limit \(x\downarrow0\).

The shape is "\(\infty\cdot0\)", so it must be rewritten before l’Hospital applies. Two routes:

  • Factor into two known limits (no l’Hospital at all): \(\ln(x^{-1})=-\ln x\), so the product is \(-(x\ln x)\cdot\frac{e^x-1}x\). Here \(x\ln x\to0\) (Blatt 7.1a) and \(\frac{e^x-1}x\to1\) (Beispiel 6.13), so the limit is \(-0\cdot1=0\).
  • Or make it a quotient: \(\frac{-\ln x}{1/(e^x-1)}\) is "\(\frac\infty\infty\)" — workable but messy. The first route is much cleaner, which is the general lesson: before reaching for l’Hospital, check whether the expression splits into standard limits.
WiSe23 A2: for which \(a\) is \(f_a(x)=\frac{x\sin x}{1-\cos x}\) (\(x\neq0\)), \(f_a(0)=a\), continuous on \((-\pi,\pi)\)?

Away from 0 the function is a quotient of continuous functions with \(1-\cos x\neq0\), hence continuous. So everything hangs on \(L=\lim_{x\to0}f_a(x)\), and \(f_a\) is continuous at 0 exactly when \(a=L\).

With l’Hospital (the official route), twice, since numerator and denominator both → 0: \[\frac{x\sin x}{1-\cos x}\overset{\frac00}=\frac{\sin x+x\cos x}{\sin x}\overset{\frac00}=\frac{2\cos x-x\sin x}{\cos x}\longrightarrow\frac21=2.\] Without it (Chapter 5 only): \(\frac{x\sin x}{1-\cos x}=\frac{\sin x}x\cdot2\cdot\frac{x^2/2}{1-\cos x}\to1\cdot2\cdot1=2\) by Lemma 5.19.

So \(f_a\) is continuous on \((-\pi,\pi)\) iff \(a=2\); for every other \(a\) the only point of discontinuity is \(x=0\). (The same task is worked from the continuity side in §4.1.)

What you must know for 7.4: the rule of l’Hospital and how to apply it.

7.5Higher derivatives [Höhere Ableitungen]

DefinitionSkript 7.19, 7.20

\(f^{(0)}=f,\ f^{(1)}=f',\ f^{(2)}=f'',\ f^{(n+1)}=(f^{(n)})'\) if it exists (\(f^{(n)}\) must be defined on a neighbourhood). \(f\in C^n(a,b)\): \(f',\dots,f^{(n)}\) exist on \((a,b)\) and are continuous. \(f\in C^\infty\): all derivatives exist.

LemmaSkript 7.21 – 7.23

Polynomials (Lemma 7.22) and exp (7.23: \((e^x)^{(n)}=e^x\)) are \(C^\infty\). For \(f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^na_kx^k\): \(\displaystyle a_l=\frac{f^{(l)}(0)}{l!}\): the coefficients are read off from the derivatives at 0. (Each derivative lowers the degree by one; \(f^{(l)}(0)\) keeps only the constant term \(l!\,a_l\).) Example: \(f=x^3-2x^2+7x+4\): \(f'=3x^2-4x+7\), \(f''=6x-4\), \(f'''=6\), \(f^{(n)}=0\) for \(n\ge4\). (Beispiel 7.21.) This is the seed of Taylor's formula (Chapter 10).

Warning (script): to define \(f^{(n+1)}(x)\), the function \(f^{(n)}\) must exist on a whole neighbourhood \((x-\varepsilon,x+\varepsilon)\), not only at \(x\).

Extra A consequence of Definition 7.20 worth stating separately: "\(n\) times differentiable" is weaker than \(C^n\), because \(C^n\) also demands that the derivatives be continuous. The standard example is \(x^2\sin\frac1x\) (with value 0 at 0) from Chapter 6: differentiable everywhere, but \(f'\) is not continuous at 0, so it is not \(C^1\).

Exam questionKlausur SoSe21 · Aufgabe 6

This distinction is an exam item in its own right. For \(f(x)=x^2\sin\frac1x\) (\(x\neq0\)), \(f(0)=0\), decide: (i) \(f'\) is continuous on \(\mathbb R\); (ii) \(f\) is differentiable on \(\mathbb R\); (iii) \(f\) is continuously differentiable on \(\mathbb R\setminus\{0\}\).

Answer: (i) false, (ii) true, (iii) true

(ii) holds: away from 0 by the rules, at 0 because \(\frac{f(h)-f(0)}h=h\sin\frac1h\to0\), so \(f'(0)=0\). (iii) holds: on \(\mathbb R\setminus\{0\}\), \(f'(x)=2x\sin\frac1x-\cos\frac1x\) is built from continuous functions. (i) fails at the single point 0: \(\cos\frac1x\) has no limit there, so \(f'\) is not continuous at 0 — even though \(f'(0)\) exists. Worked in full in §6.2.

The trap is reading (iii) as if it implied (i). It does not: (iii) says nothing about the point 0, which is exactly where \(f'\) misbehaves.

What you must know for 7.5: computing higher derivatives.

7.6Convexity [konvex / konkav] — the script calls the section Krümmungsverhalten, curvature behaviour

DefinitionSkript 7.24

\(f\) is convex [konvex] if for all \(x_0\neq x_1\) and \(\lambda\in(0,1)\): \[f((1-\lambda)x_0+\lambda x_1)\le(1-\lambda)f(x_0)+\lambda f(x_1).\] Strictly convex with \(<\). \(f\) is concave if \(-f\) is convex.

Picture: the graph lies below every chord. Walking in the \(+x\) direction, the graph turns left. The left side of the inequality is the function at a point between \(x_0,x_1\); the right side is the chord at the same point.

SatzSkript 7.25

For twice differentiable \(f\) on \((a,b)\): \(f\) convex \(\iff f''\ge0\); \(f''>0\Rightarrow\) strictly convex. Concave: \(f''\le0\); \(f''<0\Rightarrow\) strictly concave.

The converse of "\(f''>0\Rightarrow\) strictly convex" fails: \(x^4\) is strictly convex but \(f''(0)=0\) (Skript 7.26). Examples (Skript 7.27–7.29): \(x^2\) (\(f''=2\)) and \(e^x\) (\(f''=e^x\)) strictly convex; \(\ln x\) strictly concave (\(f''=-\frac1{x^2}\)).

Proof of "\(f''\ge0\Rightarrow\) convex" Extra — the script states 7.25 without proof

Take \(x_0<x_1\), \(\lambda\in(0,1)\), \(x=(1-\lambda)x_0+\lambda x_1\). Convexity at \(x\) is equivalent to "slope of the left chord ≤ slope of the right chord": \[\frac{f(x)-f(x_0)}{x-x_0}\le\frac{f(x_1)-f(x)}{x_1-x}.\] (Multiply out: \(x-x_0=\lambda(x_1-x_0)\), \(x_1-x=(1-\lambda)(x_1-x_0)\).) By the MVT the left side is \(f'(\xi_1)\) with \(\xi_1\in(x_0,x)\), the right side \(f'(\xi_2)\) with \(\xi_2\in(x,x_1)\). Since \(f''\ge0\), \(f'\) is increasing (7.9c) and \(\xi_1<\xi_2\), so \(f'(\xi_1)\le f'(\xi_2)\). With \(f''>0\) everything is strict. ∎

"⇐": if \(f\) is convex, the chord slopes increase, and letting the chord shrink shows \(f'\) is increasing, so \(f''\ge0\).

Try it · graph below every chord?

Pick two points \(x_0,x_1\) (sliders). The chord between them is amber. Convex ⇔ for every choice the graph between the points stays below the chord. The strip at the bottom shows the sign of \(f''\) (green = \(\ge0\), red = \(<0\)).

Satz · second-derivative testSkript 7.30, 7.31, 7.32

\(f\in C^2(a,b)\), \(f'(x_0)=0\). Then \(f''(x_0)>0\Rightarrow\) strict local min; \(f''(x_0)<0\Rightarrow\) strict local max.

Proof of the min case

\(f''\) is continuous and \(f''(x_0)>0\), so \(f''>0\) on some \((x_0-\varepsilon,x_0+\varepsilon)\). Then \(f'\) is strictly increasing there (7.9(a) applied to \(f'\)), and since \(f'(x_0)=0\): \(f'<0\) left of \(x_0\), \(f'>0\) right of it. Now 7.9(b) makes \(f\) strictly decreasing on \((x_0-\varepsilon,x_0]\) and 7.9(a) makes it strictly increasing on \([x_0,x_0+\varepsilon)\), so \(f(x)>f(x_0)\) for every \(x\neq x_0\) in that window: a strict local minimum. ∎

Extra — why not just cite 7.12? Satz 7.12 needs only \(f'\ge0\) / \(f'\le0\) and therefore concludes only a (non-strict) extremum. Strictness comes from the strict inequalities \(f'<0\), \(f'>0\) that \(f''(x_0)>0\) hands you, via 7.9(a),(b). That is the step the script's own proof of 7.30 takes.

If \(f'(x_0)=f''(x_0)=0\), no conclusion: \(x^3\) (no extremum), \(x^4\) (min), \(-x^4\) (max) all have \(f'(0)=f''(0)=0\). Then use the sign of \(f'\) around \(x_0\). (Exactly the trap in WiSe23 A3.)

Exam questionKlausur SoSe21 · Aufgabe 3

\(f\) strictly convex, \(\alpha<0\), \(x\neq y\). Which holds? (i) \(\alpha f(x)+(1-\alpha)f(y)<f(\alpha x+(1-\alpha)y)\), (ii) the reverse, (iii) not enough information.

Solution: (i)

With \(\alpha<0\), the point \(z=\alpha x+(1-\alpha)y\) lies outside the segment, beyond \(y\). So \(y\) lies between \(x\) and \(z\): \(y=\frac{-\alpha}{1-\alpha}x+\frac1{1-\alpha}z\) (weights in \((0,1)\), summing to 1). Convexity at \(y\): \(f(y)<\frac{-\alpha}{1-\alpha}f(x)+\frac1{1-\alpha}f(z)\). Multiply by \(1-\alpha>0\): \(\alpha f(x)+(1-\alpha)f(y)<f(z)\). That is (i). (Outside the segment, the graph lies above the extended chord.) The official solution checks it with \(f=x^2\), \(x=0\).

What you must know for 7.6: the sufficient criterion for local min/max; determining the curvature behaviour of a function.

7.7Curve sketching [Kurvendiskussion]

The recipeSkript §7.7
  1. Domain and behaviour at the boundary (limits at \(\pm\infty\) and at gaps).
  2. Derivatives \(f',f''\) (state differentiability, e.g. "as a composition of \(C^\infty\) functions").
  3. Critical points: solve \(f'(x)=0\) (only inside the domain!).
  4. Classify: sign of \(f''\) there, or sign change of \(f'\) (needed when \(f''=0\)).
  5. Monotonicity from the sign of \(f'\) on the intervals between critical points.
  6. Global extrema: compare local extrema with boundary values/limits.
  7. Curvature from the sign of \(f''\) (convex where \(f''\ge0\), concave where \(f''\le0\)).
  8. Sketch.

Extra — inflection points The word is not in this chapter of the script (it appears once, undefined, in Chapter 10), and the script's own six-step recipe stops at "Krümmung". But it is the natural thing to record while reading the sign of \(f''\), so: an inflection point [Wendepunkt] is a point where \(f''\) changes sign, i.e. where the curvature flips between convex and concave. Example: \(x^3\) at 0. Since the script does not ask for them, an exam will not either — treat them as a free extra line in your sketch, not as something you must find.

Script example: \(f(x)=x+\frac1x\) on \((0,\infty)\): → ∞ at both ends; \(f'=1-\frac1{x^2}=0\iff x=1\) (\(-1\) is not in the domain!); \(f''=\frac2{x^3}>0\), so min at 1 with \(f(1)=2\), global because of the boundary behaviour; decreasing on \((0,1)\), increasing on \((1,\infty)\); strictly convex.

Try it · curve sketch lab

Under the graph: the sign charts of \(f'\) (monotonicity) and \(f''\) (curvature), computed from the derivatives. The analysis below lists the critical points and their classification. Most functions are from the sheets and exams.

Exam question · full curve sketchKlausur WiSe23 · Aufgabe 3 (11 points)

\(f(x)=x^3e^{-x}\) on ℝ. (a) \(f'\) and its zeros. (b) Local extrema with classification; name critical points that are not extrema. (c) Global extrema.

Solution

(a) Product rule: \(f'=3x^2e^{-x}-x^3e^{-x}=(3-x)x^2e^{-x}\). Since \(e^{-x}>0\): zeros \(x=0\) (double) and \(x=3\).

(b) Sign of \(f'\): positive on \((-\infty,0)\) and \((0,3)\), negative on \((3,\infty)\). So \(x=3\) is a local max (+ to −). At \(x=0\) there is no sign change: not an extremum (a "plateau point"). The \(f''\)-test alone fails at 0: \(f''=(x^3-6x^2+6x)e^{-x}\), \(f''(0)=0\); at 3: \(f''(3)=-9e^{-3}<0\) ✓.

(c) \(f\) increases on \((-\infty,3]\), decreases on \([3,\infty)\) ⇒ \(x=3\) is the global max, \(f(3)=27e^{-3}\). No global min, since \(f(x)\to-\infty\) as \(x\to-\infty\).

More exam curve tasksWiSe18 A1 · WiSe20 A4 · SoSe21 4b
WiSe18 A1 (= Blatt 10.2d): \(f(x)=x^2e^{-x}\)

\(f'=(2x-x^2)e^{-x}\), zeros 0 and 2. Signs: − on \((-\infty,0)\), + on \((0,2)\), − on \((2,\infty)\): local min at 0, local max at 2. Limits: \(\infty\) at \(-\infty\), 0 at \(+\infty\). Since \(f\ge0=f(0)\): global min at 0; no global max. (Second-derivative route: \(f''=(x^2-4x+2)e^{-x}\), \(f''(0)=2>0\), \(f''(2)=-2e^{-2}<0\).)

WiSe20 A4: \(f(x)=10+\int_{-3}^x(3t^2-12)\,dt\) on \([-3,\infty)\)

By the fundamental theorem (Chapter 8), \(f'(x)=3x^2-12=3(x-2)(x+2)\), and \(f(x)=x^3-12x+1\). Candidates: \(x=\pm2\) and the boundary \(x=-3\). Values \(f(-3)=10\), \(f(-2)=17\), \(f(2)=-15\), \(f\to\infty\). Signs of \(f'\): + on \([-3,-2)\), − on \((-2,2)\), + beyond. So: local min 10 at the boundary \(-3\), local max 17 at \(-2\), global min \(-15\) at 2, no global max.

SoSe21 4b: extrema of \(x^x\) on \((0,\infty)\)

\(x^x=e^{x\ln x}\), \(f'=x^x(\ln x+1)\). \(x^x>0\), so \(f'=0\iff\ln x=-1\iff x=\frac1e\). Sign − before, + after ⇒ global min at \(\frac1e\), value \(e^{-1/e}\).

From the exercise sheet · optimisationBlatt 10 · 10.1, 10.3
10.1 The perfect tin can: open cylinder with volume 60 cm³, minimise the sheet metal

Volume \(\pi r^2h=60\Rightarrow h=\frac{60}{\pi r^2}\). Area (bottom + side) \(f(r)=\pi r^2+2\pi rh=\pi r^2+\frac{120}r\) on \((0,\infty)\). \(f'(r)=2\pi r-\frac{120}{r^2}=0\iff r^3=\frac{60}\pi\iff r=\sqrt[3]{60/\pi}\). \(f\to\infty\) at both ends, so this unique critical point is the global min: \(f\approx67.34\) cm².

Recipe: write the target as a function of one variable using the constraint, fix the domain, then find the global extremum.

10.3 Among rectangles with perimeter U, the square has maximal area

\(y=\frac U2-x\), area \(f(x)=\frac U2x-x^2\) on \((0,\frac U2)\). \(f'=\frac U2-2x=0\iff x=\frac U4\); \(f'\) changes from + to − ⇒ global max; then \(y=\frac U4\) too.

10.2 Extrema on restricted domains (boundary points!)
  • \(2x^2-16x+36\) on ℝ: global min at 4. On \([0,\infty)\): additionally a local max at the boundary 0 (\(f\) decreases from there), not global.
  • \(-4x^3+24x^2+12x+6\): \(f'=-12(x^2-4x-1)=0\iff x=2\pm\sqrt5\). Local min at \(2-\sqrt5\), local max at \(2+\sqrt5\); no global extrema (limits \(\pm\infty\)).
  • \(x^2-4x\) on \([-2,5]\): local min at 2 (\(-4\), global); local maxima at both ends, \(f(-2)=12\) global, \(f(5)=5\).
  • \(\frac{x-1}{x+1}\) on \((-1,4]\): \(f'=\frac2{(x+1)^2}>0\): global max at 4 (\(\frac35\)); no min (\(\to-\infty\) at \(-1\)).
  • \(x^2e^x\): \(f'=x(x+2)e^x\): local max at \(-2\) (\(4e^{-2}\)), global min at 0; no global max.
Try it · the perfect tin can (Blatt 10.1)

Open cylinder, volume fixed at 60 cm³. Choose the radius \(r\); the height follows from the constraint \(h=\frac{60}{\pi r^2}\). The graph shows the metal area \(A(r)=\pi r^2+\frac{120}r\). Thin cans need a lot of side, flat cans a lot of bottom — the minimum lies in between, where \(A'(r)=0\).

What you must know for 7.7: for a given function, determining boundary behaviour, derivatives, extrema, monotonicity and curvature.

!What the exam asks from Chapter 7

Chapter 7 is in every exam, usually with the biggest single task (a curve sketch worth 8–11 points):

TypeExam tasksWhat earns the points
Curve sketch / all extremaWiSe18 A1, WiSe20 A4, SoSe21 4b, WiSe23 A3factorised \(f'\), sign chart, classification (sign change when \(f''=0\)), boundary values and limits for global extrema
MVT proofsWiSe19 2b, WiSe22 4a, SoSe23 A4, WiSe18 A4b, WiSe20 A6, SoSe21 A5state the MVT with both hypotheses; apply it to the right function and interval
Limits with l’HospitalWiSe18 1a, WiSe24 1c, WiSe20 3b, WiSe23 A2check "0/0" or "∞/∞" first; rewrite the other forms into a quotient; often a standard limit or an \(o\)-expansion is faster
Monotonicity from \(f'\)WiSe22 4b, SoSe23 A4cget \(f'\) (in WiSe22 4b from the fundamental theorem, without computing the integral), factor it, read the sign, cite 7.9
\(C^n\) vs \(n\)-times differentiableSoSe21 A6the derivative may exist everywhere and still be discontinuous; decide each statement separately
