Exam questionKlausur WiSe20 · A2b

\(f\) continuous on \([0,\infty)\), differentiable on \((0,\infty)\), \(f(0)=0\), \(f(x)>0\) for \(x>0\). Which hold? (i) \(\liminf_{x\downarrow0}\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}=-\infty\), (ii) \(\lim_{x\downarrow0}\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}=-\infty\), (iii) \(\liminf_{x\downarrow0}\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}=\infty\).

Solution: official answer (iii), but see the flag below

(i), (ii) false: \(f(x)=\sqrt x\) gives \(\frac{f'}f=\frac1{2x}\to+\infty\).

What the argument proves. Put \(L(x)=\ln f(x)\) on \((0,\infty)\); then \(L'=\frac{f'}f\) (chain rule), and \(L(x)\to-\infty\) as \(x\downarrow0\) because \(f(x)\to f(0)=0\) (continuity). Suppose \(\limsup_{x\downarrow0}\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}<\infty\): then there are \(M\) and \(t>0\) with \(L'(\xi)<M\) for all \(\xi\in(0,t)\). For \(0<x<t\), the MVT gives \(L(t)-L(x)=L'(\xi)(t-x)<M(t-x)\le|M|t\), so \(L(x)>L(t)-|M|t\) stays bounded below — contradicting \(L(x)\to-\infty\). Hence \(\limsup_{x\downarrow0}\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}=\infty\). (The official solution integrates \(L'\) instead of using the MVT; same idea.)

Flag: the official solution uses exactly this argument for (iii), which is about the liminf. But "\(\liminf<\infty\)" only gives points arbitrarily close to 0 with \(L'<M\), not a whole interval \((0,t)\). Counterexample: \(f(x)=x\big(2+\sin\frac1x\big)\), \(f(0)=0\), satisfies all hypotheses, and \(\frac{f'(x)}{f(x)}=\frac1x-\frac{\cos(1/x)}{x^2(2+\sin(1/x))}\) equals \(2\pi n-2\pi^2n^2\to-\infty\) at \(x=\frac1{2\pi n}\). So for this \(f\) the liminf is \(-\infty\) and (iii) fails. As stated, none of (i)–(iii) holds for every such \(f\); what always holds is \(\limsup=\infty\). Worth asking your tutor.

Drill: classify points and compute limits

What you must be able to do after Chapter 7
  • For the exam: a complete curve sketch without gaps; MVT short proofs; l’Hospital with its conditions.
  • For Chapter 8: "\(F'=G'\) on an interval ⇒ \(F=G+c\)" (7.14) is why antiderivatives are unique up to a constant; the fundamental theorem is proved with the mean value theorem of integration.
  • For Chapter 9: monotone functions allow comparing sums with integrals (integral test).
  • For Chapter 10: Lemma 7.22 (\(a_l=\frac{f^{(l)}(0)}{l!}\)) becomes Taylor's formula; the Lagrange remainder is a generalised MVT.
  • For Chapter 11: the second-derivative test becomes the Hessian test (eigenvalues instead of the sign of \(f''\)).

ΣSummary sheet

  1. Interior local extremum ⇒ \(f'=0\). Not conversely (\(x^3\)); endpoints separately.
  2. MVT: \(\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{b-a}=f'(\xi)\). Rolle: equal ends ⇒ \(f'(\xi)=0\). Generalised: \(\frac{f(b)-f(a)}{g(b)-g(a)}=\frac{f'(\xi)}{g'(\xi)}\).
  3. \(f'>0\Rightarrow\) strictly increasing; \(f'\ge0\iff\) increasing; \(f'=0\iff\) constant (on intervals).
  4. Extremum tests: sign change of \(f'\) (always works), or \(f''(x_0)\neq0\) (fails when \(f''(x_0)=0\)).
  5. \(1+x\le e^x\), \(\ln(1+x)\le x\). Prove inequalities by minimising the difference.
  6. l’Hospital only for \(\frac00,\frac\infty\infty\); rewrite other forms first; often an \(o\)-expansion is faster.
  7. \(f''\ge0\iff\) convex (graph below chords).

?Self-test

Analysis für Informatik · MA0902 · Chapter 8

IntegrationIntegration

The integral measures the (signed) area under a graph, defined as the limit of rectangle sums. The fundamental theorem of calculus then reveals that integrating undoes differentiating, so areas can be computed with antiderivatives. Two techniques, integration by parts and substitution, are the reverse of the product and chain rules. Every exam contains at least one integral to compute.

Source labels: Skript 8.6 = stated in your lecture script · Blatt 11 · 11.1 = exercise sheet · Klausur WiSe23 = old exam · Extra = my own addition (intuition, a proof the script leaves out, or an extra example). A § marks a whole section of the script (§8.1 = Section 8.1) rather than a numbered result (8.1 = Definition 8.1).

0The big picture

  1. 8.1 Chop \([a,b]\) into small pieces, add up rectangles \(f(\xi_j)\cdot\text{width}\) (Riemann sums), let the pieces shrink. The limit is \(\int_a^bf(x)\,dx\).
  2. 8.2 The integral is linear, monotone, additive over intervals; every continuous function is integrable; mean value theorem of integration.
  3. 8.3 Fundamental theorem: \(F(x)=\int_a^xf(t)\,dt\) has \(F'=f\). Consequence: \(\int_a^bf=F(b)-F(a)\) for any antiderivative \(F\).
  4. 8.4 Rules: \(\int fg'=[fg]-\int f'g\) (by parts); \(\int f(g(t))g'(t)\,dt=\int f(x)\,dx\) (substitution).
Before you start
  • Σ notation and limits of sequences (§3.1, §2.1) — the integral is a limit of sums.
  • Max–min theorem and IVT (§4.4, §4.2) — together they give the mean value theorem of integration, which proves the fundamental theorem.
  • The derivative table and the product and chain rules (§6.3) — integration by parts and substitution are these rules read backwards.
  • "\(F'=G'\) on an interval ⇒ \(F=G+c\)" (Lemma 7.14, §7.3).

8.1Definition of the integral

For a constant function \(f=c\) on \([a,b]\), the area is \(c(b-a)\). For other functions, approximate by rectangles:

Riemann sumsSkript §8.1 · Definition 8.1

A partition [Zerlegung] \(Z\): \(a=x_0<x_1<\dots<x_n=b\), with mesh [Feinheit] \(|Z|=\max_j|x_j-x_{j-1}|\). Choose intermediate points \(\xi_j\in[x_{j-1},x_j]\). The Riemann sum is \[S_Z=\sum_{j=1}^nf(\xi_j)(x_j-x_{j-1}).\] \(f\) is (Riemann) integrable if for every sequence of partitions with \(|Z_n|\to0\) and every choice of intermediate points, the Riemann sums converge to the same limit \(I(f)=\int_a^bf(x)\,dx\).

Why "every partition sequence and every choice of \(\xi_j\)": for a fixed partition, the Riemann sum depends on where in each strip you measure the height. Integrable means that this freedom stops mattering as the strips get thin: all choices lead to the same number. Extra A function where it never stops mattering: \(f(x)=1\) for rational \(x\), \(0\) for irrational \(x\) on \([0,1]\). Every strip contains both kinds of points (rationals by Satz 1.10; irrationals too, e.g. \(r+\frac{\sqrt2}n\) for rational \(r\) and large \(n\)), so choosing rational \(\xi_j\) gives sums 1, irrational \(\xi_j\) gives sums 0 — not integrable.

Conventions: \(\int_a^af=0\), \(\int_b^af=-\int_a^bf\). The name of the variable does not matter: \(\int_a^bf(x)\,dx=\int_a^bf(t)\,dt\). Equidistant partition: \(x_j=a+j\frac{b-a}n\), mesh \(\frac{b-a}n\to0\).

Try it · Riemann sums

Try "smallest value" and "largest value" in the widget: they aim at the lower and upper sum, the smallest and largest Riemann sum a given partition allows. (The widget finds each strip's smallest and largest value by sampling 25 points in it, so for a wiggly \(f\) it is very close rather than exact.) For a continuous \(f\) the two squeeze together as \(n\) grows — and every other choice of \(\xi_j\) lies between them, which is why all choices share the same limit.

What you must know for 8.1: the definition of the integral.

8.2Important properties

SatzSkript 8.2, 8.3

For integrable \(f,g\) on \([a,b]\):

  1. Normalisation: \(\int_a^b1\,dx=b-a\).
  2. Positivity: \(f\ge0\Rightarrow\int_a^bf\ge0\).
  3. Monotonicity: \(f\ge g\Rightarrow\int_a^bf\ge\int_a^bg\).
  4. Linearity: \(\int(\lambda f+\mu g)=\lambda\int f+\mu\int g\).
  5. Additivity: \(\int_a^bf=\int_a^cf+\int_c^bf\) (for any \(a,b,c\) where defined).
  6. Triangle inequality: \(\left|\int_a^bf\right|\le\int_a^b|f|\).

8.3 Every continuous \(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb R\) is integrable.

Why the properties hold Extra — the script says they "follow from the definition"

Each property already holds for every Riemann sum and survives the limit. (a) For \(f=1\): \(S_Z=\sum(x_j-x_{j-1})=b-a\) (telescoping). (b), (c): \(f\ge g\) gives \(S_Z(f)\ge S_Z(g)\), and limits keep ≥ (Satz 2.13). (d): \(S_Z(\lambda f+\mu g)=\lambda S_Z(f)+\mu S_Z(g)\), then the limit rules. (e): use partitions that contain \(c\) as a point. (f): \(|S_Z(f)|\le S_Z(|f|)\) by the triangle inequality for sums (Satz 1.12).

Satz 8.3 is stated without proof in the script; its proof needs "uniform continuity" (beyond this course). You only need to apply it.

"Signed" area: parts below the \(x\)-axis count negatively. \(\int_0^{2\pi}\cos x\,dx=[\sin x]_0^{2\pi}=0\) (Skript 8.14): the area above and below cancel exactly.

Satz · mean value theorem of integrationSkript 8.4, 8.5

\(f\) continuous, \(p\ge0\) integrable (a weight). Then there is \(\xi\in[a,b]\) with \(\int_a^bf(x)p(x)\,dx=f(\xi)\int_a^bp(x)\,dx\). With \(p=1\): \(\int_a^bf=f(\xi)(b-a)\): the integral equals the area of a rectangle whose height is some value of \(f\), the average value.

Proof

\(f\) has min \(\underline m\) and max \(\overline m\) on \([a,b]\) (Satz 4.29). Since \(p\ge0\): \(\underline mp\le fp\le\overline mp\), and monotonicity of the integral gives \(\underline m\int p\le\int fp\le\overline m\int p\), so \(\int fp=\mu\int p\) for some \(\mu\in[\underline m,\overline m]\). By the IVT, \(\mu=f(\xi)\) for some \(\xi\). ∎

Extra — the case the script skips Dividing by \(\int p\) to get \(\mu\) needs \(\int p>0\); the script writes "Folglich existiert \(\mu\)" without saying so. The degenerate case is harmless: if \(\int p=0\), then \(\underline m\int p\le\int fp\le\overline m\int p\) forces \(\int fp=0\) as well, so both sides of the claim are 0 and any \(\xi\in[a,b]\) works. Worth one line if you are asked to prove 8.4.

Try it · the average height (Anwendung 8.5)

The integral equals the area of one rectangle over \([a,b]\) whose height \(\mu=\frac1{b-a}\int_a^bf\) is the average value. The IVT guarantees that \(f\) actually takes this value somewhere (green dots): \(\mu=f(\xi)\). Move \(b\) and watch the rectangle adjust.

From the exercise sheet · symmetryBlatt 12 · 12.3 · Klausur WiSe18 A2, WiSe20 5a, SoSe21 1b

For integrable \(f\) on \([-a,a]\): \(f\) even ⇒ \(\int_{-a}^af=2\int_0^af\); \(f\) odd ⇒ \(\int_{-a}^af=0\).

Blatt 12.3 states this for continuous \(f\), but the two exams deliberately weaken that: WiSe18 A2 says only "integrable on every bounded interval" and WiSe20 5a "\(f:[-a,a]\to\mathbb R\) integrable and odd". The proof below never uses continuity, so it gives the stronger version — do not add a hypothesis the task did not give you.

Proof (substitution y = −x)

Split \(\int_{-a}^a=\int_{-a}^0+\int_0^a\). In the first, substitute \(x=-y\), \(dx=-dy\): \(\int_{-a}^0f(x)\,dx=\int_a^0f(-y)(-1)\,dy=\int_0^af(-y)\,dy\). If \(f\) is even this is \(\int_0^af\) (total \(2\int_0^af\)); if odd it is \(-\int_0^af\) (total 0).

Exam use (SoSe21 1b): \(\int_{-N}^N\sum_ka_k\sin(b_kx)\,dx=0\) immediately: linearity + every \(\sin(b_kx)\) is odd. No computation needed.

Exam question · positivity alone settles itKlausur SoSe23 · Aufgabe 6

\(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb R\) continuous with \(\int_a^bf(x)\,dx<0\), and \(f(x_0)>0\) for some \(x_0\in(a,b)\). Show that \(f(c)=0\) for some \(c\in(a,b)\).

Solution — two properties, no computation
  1. \(f\) must go negative somewhere. Suppose \(f\ge0\) on \((a,b)\). Then positivity of the integral (Satz 8.2(b)) gives \(\int_a^bf\ge0\), contradicting \(\int_a^bf<0\). So there is \(y_0\in(a,b)\) with \(f(y_0)<0\).
  2. Now the intermediate value theorem. \(f\) is continuous, \(f(x_0)>0>f(y_0)\), so on the closed interval between \(x_0\) and \(y_0\) — which lies inside \((a,b)\) — the IVT (Satz 4.10) gives a \(c\) with \(f(c)=0\). ∎

The point of the task: you are not meant to compute anything. It tests whether you can turn "the integral is negative" into "the function is negative somewhere" — which is exactly property (b) read backwards — and then reach for the IVT. The same task is worked from the continuity side in §4.2.

What you must know for 8.2: applying the properties of the integral.

8.3Antiderivatives [Stammfunktionen]

Satz · fundamental theorem of calculusSkript 8.6 · Hauptsatz der Differential- und Integralrechnung

Let \(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb R\) be continuous. Then \(F(x)=\int_a^xf(t)\,dt\) is differentiable on \((a,b)\) with \[F'(x)=f(x).\] In this sense, integration is the inverse of differentiation.

Proof

\(\frac{F(x+h)-F(x)}h=\frac1h\int_x^{x+h}f(t)\,dt=f(\xi_h)\) for some \(\xi_h\in[x,x+h]\) (MVT of integration). As \(h\to0\), \(\xi_h\to x\), and \(f\) is continuous, so \(f(\xi_h)\to f(x)\). ∎

In detail: \(F(x+h)-F(x)=\int_a^{x+h}f-\int_a^xf=\int_x^{x+h}f\) by additivity (8.2e); the MVT of integration with \(p=1\) turns this into \(f(\xi_h)\cdot h\); and \(x\le\xi_h\le x+h\) (or the reverse for \(h<0\)) gives \(\xi_h\to x\) by squeeze.

Picture: if you move the right edge by \(h\), the area grows by a thin strip of height ≈ \(f(x)\) and width \(h\). So the rate of change of the area is the height \(f(x)\).

Try it · the area function F(x) = ∫ₐˣ f(t) dt

Top: \(f\) with the accumulated area (above the axis counts +, below −). Bottom: \(F\). The slope of \(F\) at \(x\) equals the height of \(f\) at \(x\).

DefinitionSkript 8.7, 8.8

\(F\) is an antiderivative [Stammfunktion] of \(f\) on \((a,b)\) if \(F'=f\) there. Written \(\int f(x)\,dx\) (indefinite integral [unbestimmtes Integral]). Antiderivatives are unique up to an additive constant: \(F+c\) is one too, and two antiderivatives differ by a constant (Lemma 7.14).

Satz · computing integralsSkript 8.9

If \(f\) is continuous on \([a,b]\) and \(F\) is any antiderivative, then \[\int_a^bf(x)\,dx=F(b)-F(a)=\big[F(x)\big]_a^b.\]

Proof: \(G(x)=\int_a^xf\) is an antiderivative (8.6), so \(G=F+c\), and \(\int_a^bf=G(b)-G(a)=F(b)-F(a)\); the constant cancels.

f(x)∫f(x)dxf(x)∫f(x)dx
\(x^a,\ a\neq-1\)\(\frac{x^{a+1}}{a+1}\)\(\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\)\(\arcsin x\)
\(\frac1x\) (on \(x\neq0\))\(\ln|x|\)\(-\frac1{\sqrt{1-x^2}}\)\(\arccos x\)
\(e^x\)\(e^x\)\(\frac1{1+x^2}\)\(\arctan x\)
\(\sin x\)\(-\cos x\)\(a^x\) (\(a>0,\ a\neq1\)) Blatt 11.2d\(\frac{a^x}{\ln a}\)
\(\cos x\)\(\sin x\)\(\ln x\) (\(x>0\)) Bsp. 8.17\(x\ln x-x\)

The first eight entries are the script's own table (after Satz 8.9). The last two are added: \(\int\ln x\,dx\) is Beispiel 8.17 further down this page, and \(\int a^x\,dx=\frac{a^x}{\ln a}\) comes from the solution to Blatt 11.2(d) — both are standard exam fare, which is why they sit here rather than only in the text.

Skript 8.13: the simplest use of the table — term by term (linearity), then \(F(1)-F(0)\): \[\int_0^1(x^7+5x^3+2)\,dx=\left[\frac{x^8}8+\frac54x^4+2x\right]_0^1=\frac18+\frac54+2=\frac{27}8.\]

Why \(\ln|x|\): for \(x<0\), \((\ln(-x))'=\frac{-1}{-x}=\frac1x\). Always check an antiderivative by differentiating it. That takes seconds.

Partial fractionsSkript 8.10 – 8.12 · Partialbruchzerlegung

To integrate \(\frac1{\text{product of distinct linear factors}}\), split it into simple fractions:

  1. Ansatz: \(\frac1{(x-1)(x-2)(x+1)}=\frac a{x-1}+\frac b{x-2}+\frac c{x+1}\).
  2. Multiply out: \(1=a(x-2)(x+1)+b(x-1)(x+1)+c(x-1)(x-2)\), compare coefficients of \(x^2,x,1\) (or plug in \(x=1,2,-1\): each kills two terms!). Result \(a=-\frac12,b=\frac13,c=\frac16\).
  3. Integrate each: \(-\frac12\ln|x-1|+\frac13\ln|x-2|+\frac16\ln|x+1|\).

Simpler case (Skript 8.10): \(\frac1{x^2-4}=\frac14\left(\frac1{x-2}-\frac1{x+2}\right)\Rightarrow\int=\frac14\ln\left|\frac{x-2}{x+2}\right|\). And (Skript 8.11) \(\int\frac{dx}{x^2+4}=\frac14\int\frac{dx}{(x/2)^2+1}=\frac12\arctan\frac x2\) (no real factorisation: use arctan; check by differentiating).

Why plugging in works Extra: the equation \(1=a(x-2)(x+1)+\dots\) holds for all \(x\) except the three zeros; both sides are polynomials, so it holds for those too (continuity). At \(x=1\): \(1=a(1-2)(1+1)=-2a\), so \(a=-\frac12\). Before the ansatz: if the numerator's degree is ≥ the denominator's, divide first (as in Blatt 11.2: \(\frac{x^2+x+1}x=x+1+\frac1x\)).

Exam questions on the fundamental theoremSoSe19 A6 · WiSe22 4b · WiSe20 A4
SoSe19 A6: \(f\) continuous and \(>0\) on \([a,b]\). Prove \(\int_a^bf>0\) using the FTC.

\(F(x)=\int_a^xf\) is differentiable on \((a,b)\) with \(F'=f>0\), so \(F\) is strictly increasing (monotonicity criterion; \(F\) is continuous on \([a,b]\)). Hence \(\int_a^bf=F(b)-F(a)>0\).

WiSe22 4b: where is \(f(x)=\int_0^x(t-1)^9e^{-t}\,dt\) (\(x>0\)) increasing/decreasing?

FTC: \(f'(x)=(x-1)^9e^{-x}\). \(e^{-x}>0\) and \((x-1)^9\) has the sign of \(x-1\). So \(f\) is strictly decreasing on \((0,1)\), strictly increasing on \((1,\infty)\). No need to compute the integral!

WiSe20 A4 (\(f(x)=10+\int_{-3}^x(3t^2-12)dt\)) is solved in Chapter 7.

What you must know for 8.3: finding an antiderivative; the fundamental theorem (important!); computing integrals with an antiderivative; partial fractions.

8.4Integration rules [Integrationsregeln]

Integration by parts [Partielle Integration]

SatzSkript 8.15

For continuously differentiable \(f,g\) on \([a,b]\): \[\int_a^bf(x)g'(x)\,dx=\big[f(x)g(x)\big]_a^b-\int_a^bf'(x)g(x)\,dx.\]

Proof: integrate the product rule \((fg)'=f'g+fg'\) from \(a\) to \(b\).

Which factor is f, which is g′?Extra
  • f = the factor that gets simpler when differentiated: polynomials (\(x\to1\)), \(\ln x\to\frac1x\), \(\arctan x\to\frac1{1+x^2}\), \(\arcsin\).
  • g′ = the factor you can integrate easily: \(e^x\), \(\sin\), \(\cos\), \(x^n\).
  • A lone \(\ln x\) or \(\arctan x\): write it as \(1\cdot\ln x\) with \(g'=1\).
  • The integral comes back — then solve for it. \(e^x\sin x\) needs parts twice before \(I\) reappears on the right. \(\sin^2x\) needs it only once: after one round you are left with \(\int\cos^2x\), and \(\cos^2=1-\sin^2\) turns that back into \(I\). Either way you end with an equation in \(I\), not an antiderivative — isolate \(I\).

Write the four pieces in a small table (\(f\), \(g'\), \(f'\), \(g\)) like the script does. It prevents sign errors.

ExamplesSkript 8.16 – 8.19
\(\int_0^2xe^x\,dx\)

\(f=x,\ g'=e^x,\ f'=1,\ g=e^x\): \([xe^x]_0^2-\int_0^2e^x=2e^2-(e^2-1)=e^2+1\).

\(\int\ln x\,dx\)

\(f=\ln x,\ g'=1,\ f'=\frac1x,\ g=x\): \(\int_1^x\ln t\,dt=[t\ln t]_1^x-\int_1^x1\,dt=x\ln x-x+1\). So \(x\ln x-x\) is an antiderivative (check: \(\ln x+1-1\) ✓).

\(\int_0^\pi\sin^2x\,dx=\frac\pi2\) (Skript 8.18)

\(f=\sin x,\ g'=\sin x,\ f'=\cos x,\ g=-\cos x\): \(I=[-\sin x\cos x]_0^\pi+\int_0^\pi\cos^2x=0+\int_0^\pi(1-\sin^2x)=\pi-I\). So \(2I=\pi\).

SoSe21 4a asks the same up to \(\frac{n\pi}2\): \(\int_0^{n\pi/2}\sin^2x\,dx=\frac{n\pi}4\) (the boundary term \(\sin x\cos x\) vanishes at multiples of \(\frac\pi2\)).

\(\int_0^\pi e^x\sin x\,dx=\frac12(e^\pi+1)\) (also Klausur WiSe23 A4, 7 points)

First: \(f=\sin x,\ g'=e^x\): \(I=[e^x\sin x]_0^\pi-\int_0^\pi e^x\cos x=-\int_0^\pi e^x\cos x\). Second: \(f=-\cos x,\ g'=e^x\): \(I=[-e^x\cos x]_0^\pi-\int_0^\pi e^x\sin x=e^\pi+1-I\). So \(I=\frac12(e^\pi+1)\). The exam asked to name the technique (partial integration, twice) and evaluate \(\sin,\cos\) numerically.

From the exercise sheetBlatt 11 · 11.1, 11.2
11.1(II), all four parts: \(\int\sin x\cos x\), \(\int x^2\sqrt{1-x}\), \(\int x^2\cos x\), \(\int e^{-x}\cos5x\)
  • (a) \(\int\sin x\cos x=\sin^2x-\int\cos x\sin x\Rightarrow\int\sin x\cos x=\frac12\sin^2x+C\). (The integral comes back after one round — solve for it.)
  • (b) \(\int x^2\sqrt{1-x}\,dx\): take \(f=x^2\), \(g'=(1-x)^{1/2}\), so \(g=-\frac23(1-x)^{3/2}\). Two rounds of parts, each lowering the power of \(x\) by one and raising the root's exponent by 1: \[\int x^2\sqrt{1-x}\,dx=-\tfrac23x^2(1-x)^{3/2}-\tfrac8{15}x(1-x)^{5/2}-\tfrac{16}{105}(1-x)^{7/2}+C.\] Check by differentiating, or compare with the definite version \(\int_0^1x^2\sqrt{1-x}\,dx=\frac{16}{105}\), which Blatt 12.2 does by substitution \(y=\sqrt{1-x}\) — the same integrand, two different techniques, and both are worth knowing.
  • (c) \(\int x^2\cos x=x^2\sin x-2\int x\sin x=x^2\sin x+2x\cos x-2\sin x+C\) (by parts twice, each time lowering the power).
  • (d) \(\int e^{-x}\cos5x=-e^{-x}\cos5x-5\int e^{-x}\sin5x=\dots-25\int e^{-x}\cos5x\) ⇒ \(\int e^{-x}\cos5x=\frac{e^{-x}(5\sin5x-\cos5x)}{26}+C\).
11.1(I) \(\int_0^1x^p(1-x)^q\,dx=\frac{p!\,q!}{(p+q+1)!}\) (induction on q)

\(q=0\): \(\int_0^1x^p=\frac1{p+1}=\frac{p!0!}{(p+1)!}\) ✓. Step: by parts with \(g'=x^p\), \(f=(1-x)^{q+1}\); the boundary term vanishes at 0 and 1: \[\int_0^1x^p(1-x)^{q+1}=\frac{q+1}{p+1}\int_0^1x^{p+1}(1-x)^q=\frac{q+1}{p+1}\cdot\frac{(p+1)!\,q!}{(p+q+2)!}=\frac{p!\,(q+1)!}{(p+q+2)!}.\]

11.2 definite integrals
  • \[\int_0^2(3x^4+5x^2+6x+2)\,dx=\left[\frac35x^5+\frac53x^3+3x^2+2x\right]_0^2=\frac{96}5+\frac{40}3+16=48+\frac8{15}.\]
  • \(\int_0^3(x^2+4x+3)e^x=16e^3-1\) (by parts twice).
  • Divide first! \(\displaystyle\int_2^4\frac{x^2+x+1}x\,dx=\int_2^4\left(x+1+\frac1x\right)dx=8+\ln2\).
  • \(\int_0^12^{x+1}=2\left[\frac{2^x}{\ln2}\right]_0^1=\frac2{\ln2}\).
  • \(\int_0^12x(1-e^x)=[x^2]_0^1-2\int_0^1xe^x=1-2=-1\).
  • \(\int_0^{5\pi/2}\sin\frac x5\,dx=5\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin y\,dy=5\) (substitution \(y=\frac x5\)).
Exam questions · by partsWiSe19 4a · WiSe22 2a · SoSe23 2b · WiSe20 3c
WiSe19 4a: \(\int_0^4te^{-5t}\,dt\)

\(u=t,\ v'=e^{-5t},\ u'=1,\ v=-\frac15e^{-5t}\): \(\left[-\frac t5e^{-5t}\right]_0^4+\frac15\int_0^4e^{-5t}=-\frac45e^{-20}+\frac1{25}(1-e^{-20})=\frac1{25}-\frac{21}{25}e^{-20}\).

WiSe22 2a: the antiderivative \(F\) of \(x\cos x\) with \(F(0)=0\)

\(\int x\cos x=x\sin x-\int\sin x=x\sin x+\cos x+c\). \(F(0)=1+c=0\Rightarrow c=-1\): \(F(x)=x\sin x+\cos x-1\). (Variant with \(F(0)=3\): \(c=2\).)

SoSe23 2b: all antiderivatives of \(\cos(x)e^x\)

Twice by parts and solve: \(F(x)=\frac12(\sin x+\cos x)e^x+c\). Check: \(F'=\frac12(\cos x-\sin x+\sin x+\cos x)e^x=\cos x\,e^x\) ✓.

WiSe20 3c: all antiderivatives of \(\frac{\ln\sqrt x}2\)

Simplify first: \(\frac{\ln\sqrt x}2=\frac{\ln x}4\). With \(\int\ln x=x\ln x-x\): \(F(x)=\frac14(x\ln x-x)+c\).

Substitution [Substitutionsregel]

SatzSkript 8.20

Let \(g:[a,b]\to\mathbb R\) be continuously differentiable and \(f\) continuous on \(g([a,b])\). Then \[\int_a^bf(g(t))\,g'(t)\,dt=\int_{g(a)}^{g(b)}f(x)\,dx.\]

Proof: if \(F'=f\), then \((F\circ g)'=f(g)g'\) (chain rule), so both sides equal \(F(g(b))-F(g(a))\).

About "\(dx=g'(t)\,dt\)": this is a memory aid, not a new rule: it reminds you which factor must be present for the chain rule to run backwards (\(\frac{dx}{dt}=g'(t)\)). For an indefinite integral: \(\int f(g(t))g'(t)\,dt=F(g(t))+C\).

How to substituteSkript 8.20 · Merkregel
  1. Spot an inner function \(g(t)\) whose derivative also appears (up to a constant).
  2. Write \(x=g(t)\), \(dx=g'(t)\,dt\).
  3. Change the limits: \(t=a\Rightarrow x=g(a)\), \(t=b\Rightarrow x=g(b)\). (Or, for indefinite integrals, substitute back at the end.)
  4. Integrate in \(x\).

Three cases worth naming: shift \(\int_a^bf(t+c)\,dt=\int_{a+c}^{b+c}f\) (Lemma 8.21; for \(f\) periodic with period \(c\) this gives \(\int_a^b=\int_{a+c}^{b+c}\)); scaling \(\int_a^bf(ct)\,dt=\frac1c\int_{ac}^{bc}f\) (Lemma 8.22); and the log-derivative pattern \(\int_a^b\frac{g'}g=\ln\frac{g(b)}{g(a)}\) for \(g>0\), which the script gives as a worked example rather than a named rule (Beispiel 8.23) — e.g. \(\int\tan t\,dt=-\ln|\cos t|\). Only the first two are Lemmas; quote 8.23 as an example, not as a theorem.

From the exercise sheetBlatt 12 · Abgabeaufgabe 12.2
Definite: \(\int_0^{\ln2}e^x\sqrt{e^x-1}\), \(\int_0^1\frac{2x-1}{1+x^2}\), \(\int_0^1x^2\sqrt{1-x}\)
  • \(y=e^x-1\), \(dy=e^xdx\), limits \(0\to1\): \(\int_0^1\sqrt y\,dy=\frac23\).
  • Split: \(\int_0^1\frac{2x}{1+x^2}-\int_0^1\frac1{1+x^2}\). First with \(y=1+x^2\): \(\ln2\). Second: \(\arctan1=\frac\pi4\). Result \(\ln2-\frac\pi4\).
  • \(y=\sqrt{1-x}\), \(x=1-y^2\), \(dx=-2y\,dy\): \(2\int_0^1(1-y^2)^2y^2\,dy=2\left(\frac17-\frac25+\frac13\right)=\frac{16}{105}\). (Substitution "backwards": \(x\) expressed through \(y\).)
Indefinite: \(\int2x\sin x^2\), \(\int\frac{4x\arcsin x^2}{\sqrt{1-x^4}}\), \(\int\sin(\ln x)\), \(\int\arcsin x\)
  • \(y=x^2\): \(\int\sin y\,dy=-\cos x^2\).
  • \(y=\arcsin x^2\), \(dy=\frac{2x}{\sqrt{1-x^4}}dx\): \(2\int y\,dy=(\arcsin x^2)^2\).
  • \(y=\ln x\), \(x=e^y\), \(dx=e^ydy\): \(\int e^y\sin y\,dy=\frac12e^y(\sin y-\cos y)\) (Ex. 8.19) \(=\frac x2(\sin\ln x-\cos\ln x)\).
  • By parts with \(1\cdot\arcsin x\): \(x\arcsin x-\int\frac x{\sqrt{1-x^2}}=x\arcsin x+\sqrt{1-x^2}\).
Exam questions · substitutionSoSe23 2a · WiSe24 6a
SoSe23 2a: \(\int_0^{\pi/2}\sin x\,e^{\cos x}\,dx\)

\(y=\cos x\), \(dy=-\sin x\,dx\), limits \(1\to0\): \(-\int_1^0e^y\,dy=\int_0^1e^y\,dy=e-1\). (Variant \(\int_0^\pi\): \(\int_{-1}^1e^y=e-\frac1e\).)

WiSe24 6a: \(\int_0^1x^{2024}e^{-x^{2025}}\,dx\)

The derivative of the exponent, \(-2025x^{2024}\), is there up to a constant. Antiderivative \(-\frac1{2025}e^{-x^{2025}}\) (check by the chain rule). Value \(\frac{1-e^{-1}}{2025}\). Don't be scared by big numbers: look for \(g'\) next to \(g\).

What you must know for 8.4: integration by parts and the substitution rule.

Mixed exercises from the script [Kapitel 13]

Three integrals from the script’s exercise pool, one per technique.

Aufgabe 13.3 · simplify before you integrateSkript 13.3

\(\displaystyle I=\int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{\sin(2x)}{\cos x+\cos^2x}\,dx\).

Solution

The denominator vanishes at \(x=\frac\pi2\) (since \(\cos\frac\pi2=0\)), which looks like an improper integral. But use \(\sin2x=2\sin x\cos x\) and cancel: \[\frac{\sin2x}{\cos x+\cos^2x}=\frac{2\sin x\cos x}{\cos x(1+\cos x)}=\frac{2\sin x}{1+\cos x},\] which is perfectly well defined on all of \([0,\frac\pi2]\) (the denominator is \(\ge1\)). So the integral is an ordinary one. Substitute \(u=\cos x\), \(du=-\sin x\,dx\); the limits become \(u(0)=1\) and \(u(\frac\pi2)=0\): \[I=-2\int_1^0\frac{du}{1+u}=2\int_0^1\frac{du}{1+u}=2\big[\ln(1+u)\big]_0^1=2\ln2.\]

Lesson: simplify first. A zero in the denominator that cancels is not a singularity.

Aufgabe 13.4 · substitute twiceSkript 13.4

\(\displaystyle F=\int x\sin(x^2)\cos^2(x^2)\,dx\).

Solution

First \(u=x^2\), \(du=2x\,dx\) (the lone factor \(x\) is the signal): \(F=\frac12\int\sin u\cos^2u\,du\). Then \(z=\cos u\), \(dz=-\sin u\,du\): \[F=-\frac12\int z^2\,dz=-\frac16z^3=-\frac16\cos^3(x^2)+C.\] Check by differentiating (chain rule twice): \(\frac{d}{dx}\left(-\frac16\cos^3(x^2)\right)=-\frac12\cos^2(x^2)\cdot(-\sin(x^2))\cdot2x=x\sin(x^2)\cos^2(x^2)\) ✓.

Aufgabe 13.5 · parts with \(g'=1\)Skript 13.5

\(\displaystyle\int\arctan x\,dx\).

Solution

There is only one factor, so write the second one as 1 and integrate by parts with \(f=\arctan x\), \(g'=1\), \(f'=\frac1{1+x^2}\), \(g=x\): \[\int\arctan x\,dx=x\arctan x-\int\frac x{1+x^2}\,dx=x\arctan x-\frac12\ln(1+x^2)+C,\] the last integral being of the form \(\frac12\int\frac{g'}g\) with \(g=1+x^2>0\).

Same trick as for \(\int\ln x\,dx=x\ln x-x\) (Skript 8.17): for a lone \(\ln\), \(\arctan\), \(\arcsin\), take \(g'=1\).

!What the exam asks from Chapter 8

Every exam has at least one integral, often a proof about integrals as well:

TypeExam tasksWhat earns the points
By partsWiSe19 4a, WiSe22 2a, SoSe23 2b, WiSe23 A4, SoSe21 4a, WiSe20 3ctable \(f,g',f',g\); for \(e^x\sin x\) twice + solve; state the technique
SubstitutionSoSe23 2a, WiSe24 6aspot \(g\) and \(g'\); change the limits
SymmetryWiSe18 A2, WiSe20 5a, SoSe21 1bsubstitution \(y=-x\) on \([-a,0]\) plus additivity
Fundamental theoremSoSe19 A6, WiSe22 4b, WiSe20 A4\(F'=f\) without computing the integral; then monotonicity/extrema (Ch. 7)
Antiderivative with a conditionWiSe22 2a (\(F(0)=0\))find \(F+c\), then fix \(c\)
Properties of the integral, no computationSoSe23 A6positivity (8.2b) to locate a sign, then the IVT; resist the urge to integrate

Drill: which technique?

Deciding the technique is half the task. For each integral, pick the method (and check the answer in the explanation).

What you must be able to do after Chapter 8
  • For the exam: the five task types above, and the antiderivative table by heart.
  • For Chapter 9: improper integrals \(\int_1^\infty\frac{dx}{x^\alpha}\) are limits of ordinary ones; the integral test compares sums with integrals; parameter integrals differentiate under the integral sign.
  • For Chapter 10: Taylor's remainder in integral form is proved by repeated integration by parts.
  • For Chapter 12: separation of variables is substitution; variation of constants needs \(\int f(t)e^{A(t)}dt\), usually by parts.

ΣSummary sheet

  1. \(\int_a^bf=\lim\) of Riemann sums \(\sum f(\xi_j)\Delta x_j\); continuous ⇒ integrable; signed area.
  2. Linear, monotone, additive; \(|\int f|\le\int|f|\); \(\int_a^bf=f(\xi)(b-a)\) for continuous \(f\).
  3. FTC: \(\frac{d}{dx}\int_a^xf(t)\,dt=f(x)\), and \(\int_a^bf=F(b)-F(a)\). Use it to differentiate integral-defined functions without computing them.
  4. By parts: \(\int fg'=[fg]-\int f'g\); choose \(f\) to simplify; twice + solve for cyclic cases.
  5. Substitution: \(x=g(t)\), \(dx=g'(t)dt\), change the limits. Look for "function and its derivative".
  6. Partial fractions for rational functions; odd functions on symmetric intervals integrate to 0.
  7. Always check antiderivatives by differentiating.

Analysis für Informatik · MA0902 · Chapter 9

More on IntegralsMehr über Integrale

Four extensions of the integral. Improper integrals over unbounded intervals or up to singularities; integrals that depend on a parameter; when you may swap sum and integral; and, most useful for computer science, estimating sums by integrals: \(H_n\approx\ln n\), \(\ln(n!)\approx n\ln n\) (the comparison count of sorting), and the integral test for series.

Source labels: Skript 9.6 = stated in your lecture script · Blatt 12 · 12.4 = exercise sheet · Klausur WiSe24 = old exam · Extra = my own addition (intuition, a proof the script leaves out, or an extra example). A § marks a whole section of the script (§9.1 = Section 9.1) rather than a numbered result (9.1 = Definition 9.1).

0The big picture

  1. 9.1 \(\int_a^\infty f=\lim_{M\to\infty}\int_a^Mf\): an improper integral is a limit of ordinary integrals, just like a series is a limit of partial sums. Benchmark: \(\int_1^\infty x^{-\alpha}\) converges iff \(\alpha>1\); \(\int_0^1x^{-\alpha}\) iff \(\alpha<1\).
  2. 9.2 For continuous integrands you may swap integration order (Fubini) and differentiate under the integral sign.
  3. 9.3 \(\int\sum=\sum\int\) if there is a convergent majorant. Gives \(\ln(1+x)=\sum(-1)^k\frac{x^{k+1}}{k+1}\) and \(\ln2=1-\frac12+\frac13-\dots\)
  4. 9.4 For monotone \(f\), \(\sum f(k)\) is squeezed between integrals. Integral test: \(\sum f(k)\) and \(\int f\) converge together.
Before you start
  • The fundamental theorem, antiderivatives and substitution (§8.3, §8.4) — every improper integral is computed with them first.
  • Series: majorant/minorant and "non-negative terms: convergent ⇔ partial sums bounded" (§3.2, Satz 3.6). §9.1 and §9.4 copy these ideas for integrals.
  • Monotone + bounded ⇒ convergent (Satz 2.17), one-sided limits (§4.1), and the growth ladder (§5.2).

9.1Improper integrals [Uneigentliche Integrale]

DefinitionSkript 9.1

If \(f:[a,b)\to\mathbb R\) is integrable on every \([a,\beta]\), \(\beta<b\), the improper integral is \[\int_a^bf(x)\,dx:=\lim_{\beta\to b}\int_a^\beta f(x)\,dx,\] if the limit exists (\(b=\infty\) allowed). Same at the lower limit. If both ends are critical, split at some \(c\in(a,b)\) and require both limits to exist separately: \[\int_a^bf=\lim_{\alpha\to a}\int_\alpha^cf+\lim_{\beta\to b}\int_c^\beta f.\]

One-sided limits (Skript 9.3): \(\lim_{x\downarrow x_0}f(x)=c\) means \(f(x_n)\to c\) for all \(x_n\to x_0\) with \(x_n>x_0\); \(\lim_{x\uparrow x_0}\) analogously from the left.

Recognise improper integralsSkript 9.2, 9.4
  • An integration limit is \(\pm\infty\), or
  • the integrand blows up at a point of \([a,b]\) (a singularity), possibly inside the interval.

\(\int_0^1\frac1x\,dx=\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}[\ln x]_\varepsilon^1=\lim(-\ln\varepsilon)=\infty\). And \(\int_{-1}^1\frac1x\,dx\) is not defined: it splits into \(-\infty+\infty\). Writing \([\ln|x|]_{-1}^1=0\) is wrong because the FTC needs continuity on the whole interval.

But (Skript 13.3): \(\int_0^{\pi/2}\frac{\sin2x}{\cos x+\cos^2x}\) only looks improper. After cancelling \(\cos x\), the integrand \(\frac{2\sin x}{1+\cos x}\) is continuous on the whole interval.

Lemma · the benchmark integralsSkript 9.5, 9.6, 9.7
\[\int_1^\infty\frac{dx}{x^\alpha}=\begin{cases}\frac1{\alpha-1}&\alpha>1\\\infty&\alpha\le1\end{cases}\] \[\int_0^1\frac{dx}{x^\alpha}=\begin{cases}\frac1{1-\alpha}&\alpha<1\\\infty&\alpha\ge1\end{cases}\]
Proof

For \(\alpha\neq1\): \(\int_1^M x^{-\alpha}=\left[\frac{x^{1-\alpha}}{1-\alpha}\right]_1^M=\frac{M^{1-\alpha}-1}{1-\alpha}\). As \(M\to\infty\), \(M^{1-\alpha}\to0\) iff \(\alpha>1\) (limit \(\frac1{\alpha-1}\)), else → ∞. For \(\alpha=1\): \(\ln M\to\infty\). Near 0 it is the other way round: \(\varepsilon^{1-\alpha}\to0\) iff \(\alpha<1\).

Memory hook: at ∞ the function must decay faster than \(\frac1x\); at 0 it may blow up only slower than \(\frac1x\). \(\frac1x\) itself fails at both ends.

Try it · does the area stay finite?
Method · improper integral tasksExtra
  1. Write it as a limit: \(\lim_{M\to\infty}\int_a^M\) or \(\lim_{\varepsilon\downarrow0}\int_\varepsilon^b\) (both, if needed, at a split point).
  2. Compute the proper integral with an antiderivative (substitution/by parts).
  3. Take the limit. Finite ⇒ converges, value = limit. Otherwise diverges.
  4. If no antiderivative is available: compare with a benchmark \(\frac1{x^\alpha}\) (smaller than convergent ⇒ convergent; larger than divergent ⇒ divergent, for nonnegative integrands). Same logic as majorant/minorant for series.
Comparison test for integralsExtra — used on Blatt 13.2a and in the exams, not stated in the script

Let \(0\le f(x)\le g(x)\) on \([a,\infty)\), both integrable on every \([a,M]\). If \(\int_a^\infty g\) converges, so does \(\int_a^\infty f\) (and \(\int f\le\int g\)). If \(\int_a^\infty f\) diverges, so does \(\int_a^\infty g\). The same holds at a singular endpoint.

Proof (the Satz 3.6 argument for integrals)

\(I(M)=\int_a^Mf\) is increasing in \(M\) (since \(f\ge0\), additivity) and bounded by \(\int_a^Mg\le\int_a^\infty g\) (monotonicity). An increasing bounded function has a limit as \(M\to\infty\) (the sup — proof as in Satz 2.17). The second statement is the first read backwards. ∎

Also as in Chapter 3: if \(f,g>0\) and \(\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\to c\in(0,\infty)\), the two integrals converge or diverge together (asymptotic comparison).

From the exercise sheetBlatt 12 · Abgabeaufgabe 12.4 · Blatt 13 · 13.2a

Blatt 12.4 — "check whether the improper integral exists and compute its value if it does". Parts (a)–(b) were the ones handed in; (c) is marked voluntary on the sheet.

  • (a) \(\int_{-\infty}^\infty\frac{dx}{1+x^2}\): split at 0, as the sheet's hint says. \(\int_0^\beta=\arctan\beta\to\frac\pi2\), and \(\int_\alpha^0\to\frac\pi2\) as \(\alpha\to-\infty\). Total \(\pi\).
  • (b) \(\int_3^\infty\frac{4x}{x^2-4}dx\): substitution \(y=x^2-4\): \(2\ln(\beta^2-4)-2\ln5\to\infty\). Diverges (integrand \(\simeq\frac4x\)).
  • (c) \(\int_0^e\ln x\,dx\) (singular at 0): \([x\ln x-x]_\varepsilon^e=0-(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon-\varepsilon)\to0\), using \(\varepsilon\ln\varepsilon\to0\). Value 0.

Blatt 13.2 (a) — a convergence question with no value asked for:

  • \(\int_1^\infty\frac{dx}{x^4(1+2\ln x)}\): for \(x\ge1\), \(1+2\ln x\ge1\), so the integrand is \(\le\frac1{x^4}\). Comparison with \(\int_1^\infty x^{-4}=\frac13\) ⇒ converges. (Part (b) of the same task is a series estimate and sits in §9.4.)
From the exercise sheetBlatt 13 · Tutoriumsaufgabe 13.1 · the Gamma function

\(\Gamma(x)=\int_0^\infty t^{x-1}e^{-t}\,dt\) extends the factorial (\(\Gamma(n)=(n-1)!\)). For \(x\in(0,1)\) it is improper at both ends, and the task is to show the integral is well defined [wohldefiniert] — in four steps, one per part of the sheet.

Solution, part by part

(a) At 0. The sheet writes \(A(s)=\int_s^1t^{x-1}e^{-t}dt\) and asks for: bounded above, and decreasing in \(s\). On \((0,1)\), \(e^{-t}<1\), so \(A(s)\le\int_s^1t^{x-1}dt=\frac{1-s^x}x\le\frac1x\) — bounded. Shrinking \(s\) adds a piece of a non-negative integrand, so \(A\) decreases as \(s\) grows, i.e. increases as \(s\downarrow0\).

(b) At ∞. \(B(s)=\int_1^st^{x-1}e^{-t}dt\) is increasing in \(s\) (again non-negative integrand) and bounded: on \((1,\infty)\) we have \(t^{x-1}<1\) for \(x<1\), so \(B(s)\le\int_1^se^{-t}dt\le e^{-1}\).

(c) Conclusion. This is the part that answers the question. Both one-sided limits exist by "monotone + bounded ⇒ convergent" (Satz 2.17 transported to functions), so \(\Gamma(x)=\lim_{s\downarrow0}A(s)+\lim_{s\to\infty}B(s)\) is a finite number for every \(x\in(0,1)\) — the Gamma function is well defined there. Note the split at 1 is what makes each piece monotone; one single limit would not be monotone in anything.

(d) \(\Gamma(\frac12)\): substitute \(t=u^2\), \(dt=2u\,du\): \(\int_0^\infty\frac1ue^{-u^2}2u\,du=2\int_0^\infty e^{-u^2}du=2\cdot\frac{\sqrt\pi}2=\sqrt\pi\), using \(\int_0^\infty e^{-u^2}du=\frac{\sqrt\pi}2\), which the sheet lets you quote without proof.

Exam questionsWiSe19 4b · WiSe20 5b · WiSe22 2b · WiSe24 6c
WiSe19 4b: \(\int_0^\infty\min\left(x^2,\frac1{x^2}\right)dx\)

\(\min=x^2\) on \([0,1]\), \(=\frac1{x^2}\) on \((1,\infty)\) (continuous). \(\int_0^1x^2=\frac13\), \(\int_1^\infty x^{-2}=\lim_\alpha\left(1-\frac1\alpha\right)=1\). Total \(\frac43\).

WiSe20 5b: for which \(a\) does \(\int_0^\infty\frac x{(1+x^2)^a}dx\) converge?

Substitute \(y=1+x^2\), \(dy=2x\,dx\): \(\frac12\int_1^\infty y^{-a}\,dy\). By the benchmark: converges iff \(a>1\), value \(\frac1{2(a-1)}\). (\(a=1\): \(\frac12\ln M\to\infty\).)

WiSe22 2b: \(\int_0^\infty x^4e^{-x^5}dx\)

\(y=x^5\), \(dy=5x^4dx\): \(\lim_M\frac15\int_0^{M^5}e^{-y}dy=\lim\frac15(1-e^{-M^5})=\frac15\). (Variant with \(x^3e^{-x^4}\): \(\frac14\).)

WiSe24 6c: does \(\int_0^\infty|\sin x|^{\cos x}\,xe^{-x}\,dx\) exist? (hard)

No. Look near \(x=\pi\): there \(\cos x\approx-1\), so \(|\sin x|^{\cos x}\approx\frac1{|\sin x|}\approx\frac1{|x-\pi|}\), and the integrand behaves like \(\frac{\pi e^{-\pi}}{|x-\pi|}\), which is not integrable (\(\int\frac{dh}h\) diverges at 0).

The common wrong solution (partial credit only): "\(|\sin x|^{\cos x}\le1\), so the integral is \(\le\int xe^{-x}=1\)". The bound is false where \(\cos x<0\): a number in \((0,1)\) raised to a negative power is \(>1\). Always check where the integrand can blow up.

What you must know for 9.1: recognising improper integrals; for which α the integrals \(\int_0^1x^{-\alpha}\) and \(\int_1^\infty x^{-\alpha}\) converge or diverge.

9.2Parameter-dependent integrals [Parameterabhängige Integrale]

For \(f(x,y)\) and fixed \(x\), the derivative of \(y\mapsto f(x,y)\) is the partial derivative \(\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}=\partial_yf=\partial_2f\) (differentiate in \(y\), treat \(x\) as a constant; Chapter 11 in detail).

SatzSkript 9.8

Let \(f:[a,b]\times[c,d]\to\mathbb R\) be continuous.

  1. \(F(y)=\int_a^bf(x,y)\,dx\) is continuous.
  2. Fubini: \(\int_c^d\int_a^bf(x,y)\,dx\,dy=\int_a^b\int_c^df(x,y)\,dy\,dx\). The order does not matter.
  3. If \(\partial_yf\) exists and is continuous, then \(F\) is \(C^1\) and \(\displaystyle\frac{d}{dy}\int_a^bf(x,y)\,dx=\int_a^b\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}(x,y)\,dx\).
Example · Laplace transformSkript 9.9

\(\varphi(t)=\int_a^be^{tx}g(x)\,dx\) with continuous \(g\). Differentiating under the integral: \(\varphi^{(k)}(t)=\int_a^bx^ke^{tx}g(x)\,dx\). If \(g\) is a probability density of \(X\), then \(\varphi(t)=E[e^{tX}]\) and \(E[X^k]=\varphi^{(k)}(0)\): all moments come from one function (used in probability theory).

What you must know for 9.2: computing partial derivatives; Satz 9.8.

9.3Swapping summation and integration

SatzSkript 9.10

Let \(f_k\) be integrable on \([a,b]\) with \(|f_k(x)|\le a_k\) for all \(x\) and \(\sum a_k<\infty\) (a convergent majorant independent of \(x\)). Then \(f=\sum f_k\) converges and \[\int_a^b\sum_{k=1}^\infty f_k(x)\,dx=\sum_{k=1}^\infty\int_a^bf_k(x)\,dx.\]

Why the majorant makes it work Extra — the script gives no proof

For each \(x\), \(\sum f_k(x)\) converges absolutely by the majorant test (Satz 3.7). Split off the first \(n\) terms: \(f=\sum_{k\le n}f_k+R_n\) with the tail \(|R_n(x)|\le\sum_{k>n}a_k=:\varepsilon_n\) — the same bound for every \(x\), and \(\varepsilon_n\to0\). For finitely many terms, integral and sum may be swapped (linearity), so \[\Big|\int_a^bf-\sum_{k\le n}\int_a^bf_k\Big|=\Big|\int_a^bR_n\Big|\le(b-a)\,\varepsilon_n\to0.\] (That \(f\) itself is integrable is part of the theorem; you may take it as given.) The key is that the tail bound does not depend on \(x\) — exactly what fails in 9.14. ∎

Example · the log seriesSkript 9.11 – 9.13

For \(|t|<1\): \(\frac1{1+t}=\sum_{k\ge0}(-t)^k\) (geometric). For \(|t|\le|x|<1\), \(|(-t)^k|\le|x|^k\), a convergent majorant. So integrate term by term: \[\ln(1+x)=\int_0^x\frac{dt}{1+t}=\sum_{k=0}^\infty(-1)^k\frac{x^{k+1}}{k+1}=x-\frac{x^2}2+\frac{x^3}3-\dots\qquad(|x|<1).\] This is a power series [Potenzreihe] with radius of convergence [Konvergenzradius] \(r=1\) (Def. 9.12; at \(x=-1\) the series is \(-\sum\frac1{k+1}=-\infty\)).

Lemma 9.13: the formula even holds at \(x=1\): \[\ln2=1-\frac12+\frac13-\frac14+\dots\] Proof idea: for \(0<x<1\) the Leibniz error bound gives \(\left|\ln(1+x)-s_n(x)\right|\le\frac{x^{n+2}}{n+2}\); let \(x\uparrow1\) (ln is continuous), then \(n\to\infty\).

The script's second power series (stated right after Lemma 9.13, and named in the Minimale Inhalte) is the geometric series itself: \[\sum_{k=0}^\infty x^k=\frac1{1-x}\qquad(|x|<1),\quad\text{radius of convergence }r=1.\] It is the same series that started this box, with \(t\) replaced by \(-x\); both are the templates Chapter 10 builds on. You are expected to be able to write both down from memory.

Without a majorant it can failSkript 9.14 · Klausur WiSe24 6b

The script builds functions \(f_k\) whose partial sums are spikes of height \(n\) on \((0,\frac1n)\) (the \(f_k\) themselves are steps taking the values \(1\) and \(-(k-1)\); see the details below) with \(\sum_k\int_0^1f_k=1\) but \(\int_0^1\sum_kf_k=0\). The same phenomenon appears in WiSe24 6b: \(f_n(x)=\frac n{(1+x)^n}\) on \([0,1]\). For each \(x\in(0,1]\), \(f_n(x)\to0\) (exponential beats linear), yet \[\int_0^1f_n=\frac n{n-1}\left(1-2^{1-n}\right)\to1\neq0.\] The mass concentrates in an ever thinner spike near 0. Moral: "limit of integrals = integral of the limit" needs a justification (such as a majorant).

The script's counterexample in detail (Bemerkung 9.14)

Let \(f_1=\mathbb 1_{(0,1)}\) and \(f_k=k\,\mathbb 1_{(0,\frac1k)}-(k-1)\,\mathbb 1_{(0,\frac1{k-1})}\) for \(k\ge2\) (\(\mathbb 1_A\) = 1 on \(A\), 0 elsewhere). Then \(\int_0^1f_k=k\cdot\frac1k-(k-1)\frac1{k-1}=0\) for \(k\ge2\) and \(\int f_1=1\), so \(\sum_k\int f_k=1\). But the partial sums telescope: \(\sum_{k\le n}f_k=n\,\mathbb 1_{(0,\frac1n)}\), a spike of height \(n\) and width \(\frac1n\). For every fixed \(x\) it is 0 as soon as \(\frac1n\le x\), so \(\sum_kf_k(x)=0\) and \(\int\sum=0\). No majorant exists: \(\sup_x|f_k|=k-1\), and \(\sum(k-1)=\infty\).

Try it · pointwise to 0, but the area does not go to 0

Slide \(n\). At every fixed \(x>0\) the value goes to 0 — yet the graph keeps a spike near 0 that carries (almost) all the area. That is why the limit and the integral cannot simply be swapped.

Pointwise vs uniform convergenceExtra — not in the script, but asked in WiSe24 2b and SoSe19 A3

A sequence of functions \(f_n:D\to\mathbb R\) converges pointwise [punktweise] to \(f\) if \(f_n(x)\to f(x)\) for every single \(x\in D\). It converges uniformly [gleichmäßig] if the worst-case distance goes to 0: \[\sup_{x\in D}|f_n(x)-f(x)|\xrightarrow{n\to\infty}0.\] Uniform means one \(n_0\) works for all \(x\) at once; pointwise allows \(n_0\) to depend on \(x\). Uniform ⇒ pointwise, not conversely. To disprove uniform convergence, find points \(x_n\) (depending on \(n\)) with \(|f_n(x_n)-f(x_n)|\not\to0\).

Link to Satz 9.10: a convergent majorant \(|f_k(x)|\le a_k\) makes the partial sums converge uniformly (the tail bound \(\varepsilon_n\) above does not depend on \(x\)). That is the "Weierstrass test" behind SoSe19 A3 (Chapter 3).

Exam questionKlausur WiSe24 · 2b

\(f_n:[0,1]\to\mathbb R\), \(f_n(x)=\frac{nx}{(1+x)^n}\). Pointwise and/or uniform convergence?

Solution: pointwise to 0, not uniformly

Pointwise: \(f_n(0)=0\). For \(x\in(0,1]\), \((1+x)^n\) grows exponentially and \(nx\) only linearly, so \(f_n(x)\to0\) (e.g. \((1+x)^n\ge\binom n2x^2\) by the binomial theorem, so \(0\le f_n(x)\le\frac{2}{(n-1)x}\to0\)).

Not uniform: take \(x_n=\frac1n\). Then \(f_n(x_n)=\frac1{(1+\frac1n)^n}\to\frac1e\neq0\) (Satz 2.21), so \(\sup_x|f_n(x)-0|\ge f_n(\frac1n)\not\to0\).

Flagged: the uploaded Musterlösung writes "\(f_n(x_n)\to\infty\)". With \(x_n=\frac1n\) the value tends to \(\frac1e\), not ∞; the conclusion "not uniform" is still right, because \(\frac1e\neq0\).

What you must know for 9.3: the sufficient criterion for swapping sum and integral; the power series of \(\ln(1+x)\) and \(\frac1{1-x}\).

9.4Estimating sums and series [Abschätzungen von Summen und Reihen]

SatzSkript 9.15

Let \(a<b\) be integers and \(f:[a,b]\to\mathbb R\) continuous and monotone.

  • \(f\) increasing: \(\displaystyle\sum_{k=a}^{b-1}f(k)\le\int_a^bf(x)\,dx\le\sum_{k=a+1}^bf(k)\).
  • \(f\) decreasing: \(\displaystyle\sum_{k=a+1}^bf(k)\le\int_a^bf(x)\,dx\le\sum_{k=a}^{b-1}f(k)\).

Proof: on each \([k,k+1]\), \(f(k)\le f(x)\le f(k+1)\) (increasing case); integrate over the unit interval and sum up. Picture: the sum is a staircase of unit-width rectangles, lying below or above the graph (see the widget).

Note on numbering Blatt 12.1 tells you to "Betrachten Sie Satz 9.18 (b) aus dem Skript" — that is this result under an older numbering of the script. In the version you have it is Satz 9.15, and 9.18 is the lemma \(\ln(n!)\simeq n\ln n\) below. Quote the number from your script.

Try it · sums as staircases around the integral
ExamplesSkript 9.16 – 9.19 · Blatt 12.1
  • Power sums (\(\alpha>0\), \(f=x^\alpha\) increasing): \(\frac{n^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha+1}\le\sum_{k=1}^nk^\alpha\le\frac{(n+1)^{\alpha+1}-1}{\alpha+1}\), hence \(\sum_{k=1}^nk^\alpha\simeq\frac{n^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha+1}\) (e.g. \(\sum k\simeq\frac{n^2}2\)).
  • Harmonic numbers (Blatt 12.1): (a) with \(f=\frac1x\) decreasing, \(\ln(n+1)\le H_n\le1+\ln n\) (the sheet's hint is \(\int_1^y\frac{dx}x=\ln y\)); (b) divide by \(\ln n\) and squeeze to get \(\frac{H_n}{\ln n}\to1\), written \(H_n\simeq\ln n\). Feeding this into the formula for the average number of comparisons of Quicksort, \(C_n=2(n+1)H_n-2n\) (stated in the script's Chapter 2 motivation, not here), gives \(C_n\simeq2n\ln n\).
  • How many comparisons does sorting need? Skript 9.17 Sorting \(n\) elements takes up to \(\log_2(n!)\) comparisons — that is the script's wording — and \(\log_2(n!)=\ln(n!)\log_2e\), so the growth is decided by \(\ln(n!)=\sum_{k=2}^n\ln k\). With \(f=\ln x\) increasing: \[n\ln n-n+1\le\ln(n!)\le(n+1)\ln(n+1)-n.\] Hence \(\ln(n!)\simeq n\ln n\) (Lemma 9.18) and even \(\ln(n!)=n\ln n-n+O(\ln n)\) (Lemma 9.19). Either way \(\log_2(n!)\) grows like \(n\log_2n\).

    Extra — which direction is the famous one? In computer science \(\log_2(n!)\) is normally quoted as a lower bound: a comparison-based sorting algorithm is a decision tree that must distinguish \(n!\) orderings, so some branch has depth \(\ge\log_2(n!)\), and no such algorithm can beat \(\approx n\log_2n\) comparisons in the worst case. Mergesort attains that order, so \(n\log n\) is both a lower and an upper bound (\(\Theta(n\log n)\)). Your script writes "bis zu" (up to) and proves nothing about the direction — so take the estimate \(\log_2(n!)\simeq n\log_2n\) from Beispiel 9.17 and leave the word "lower bound" out of an exam answer unless you prove it.

    Proofs of 9.18 and 9.19

    9.18: divide the bounds by \(n\ln n\): the lower bound gives \(1-\frac1{\ln n}+\frac1{n\ln n}\to1\); the upper gives \(\frac{n+1}n\cdot\frac{\ln(n+1)}{\ln n}-\frac1{\ln n}\to1\), because \(\frac{\ln(n+1)}{\ln n}=1+\frac{\ln(1+\frac1n)}{\ln n}\to1\). Squeeze.

    9.19: subtract \(n\ln n-n\): \(1\le\ln(n!)-n\ln n+n\le(n+1)\ln(n+1)-n\ln n=\ln n+r_n\) with \(r_n=\frac{\ln(1+\frac1n)}{1/n}+\ln(1+\frac1n)\to1\) (using \(\frac{\ln(1+u)}u\to1\), Chapter 5). So the difference is at most \(\ln n+\) a bounded amount, i.e. \(O(\ln n)\).

Satz · integral testSkript 9.20 · Integralkriterium

Let \(f:[1,\infty)\to[0,\infty)\) be decreasing. Then \[0\le\sum_{k=1}^nf(k)-\int_1^{n+1}f(x)\,dx\le f(1),\] and \(\sum_{k\ge1}f(k)\) converges iff \(\int_1^\infty f(x)\,dx\) converges. In that case \(0\le\sum_{k=1}^\infty f(k)-\int_1^\infty f\le f(1)\).

Starting at another index \(m\) works the same (Blatt 13.2 remark): \(0\le\sum_{k=m}^nf(k)-\int_m^{n+1}f\le f(m)\). Corollary: \(\sum\frac1{k^s}\) converges iff \(s>1\) (compare \(\int_1^\infty x^{-s}\)), which proves the rule of thumb from Chapter 3.

Proof (it is Satz 9.15 with \(a=1\), \(b=n+1\))

\(f\) is decreasing, so part (b) of Satz 9.15 with \(a=1\), \(b=n+1\) gives \[\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}f(k)\le\int_1^{n+1}f(x)\,dx\le\sum_{k=1}^nf(k).\] The right inequality is \(\sum_{k=1}^nf(k)-\int_1^{n+1}f\ge0\). For the other bound, add \(f(1)-f(n+1)\) to the left sum: \(\sum_{k=2}^{n+1}f(k)=\sum_{k=1}^nf(k)-f(1)+f(n+1)\), so \[\sum_{k=1}^nf(k)-\int_1^{n+1}f\le f(1)-f(n+1)\le f(1),\] using \(f\ge0\) in the last step. That is the displayed double inequality.

Convergence now follows in both directions, because both sides are increasing in \(n\) (terms and integrand are \(\ge0\)): if \(\int_1^\infty f<\infty\), the partial sums are bounded by \(\int_1^\infty f+f(1)\), hence the series converges (Satz 3.6). If the series converges, the integrals \(\int_1^{n+1}f\) are bounded by \(\sum_{k\ge1}f(k)\), and being increasing in \(n\) they converge as well. Letting \(n\to\infty\) in the double inequality gives the final estimate. ∎

Picture (same as in the widget above): \(\sum_{k=1}^nf(k)\) is a staircase of unit-width rectangles whose left ends sit on the graph. Since \(f\) falls, each rectangle covers its piece of the area under the graph, and the total overshoot is at most the first rectangle, \(f(1)\).

Try it · Σ 1/kˢ and ∫ dx/xˢ move together

Blue: partial sums \(\sum_{k=1}^n k^{-s}\). Green: \(\int_1^{n+1}x^{-s}dx\). By the integral test the blue curve is always above the green one, but never by more than \(f(1)=1\) (shaded band). So both level off (\(s>1\)) or both climb to ∞ (\(s\le1\)). The horizontal axis is logarithmic, up to \(n=10^6\).

The zeta functionSkript 9.21, 9.22

\(\zeta(s)=\sum_{k\ge1}k^{-s}\) converges for \(s>1\), and the integral test gives \(\frac1{s-1}\le\zeta(s)\le\frac1{s-1}+1\). For \(s\to\infty\), \(\zeta(s)=1+O(2^{-s})\): the first term dominates.

From the exercise sheetBlatt 13 · Abgabeaufgabe 13.2b
\(\sum\frac{\ln k}{k^2}\) converges, with bounds; \(\sum\frac{\ln k}k\) diverges

\(f(x)=\frac{\ln x}{x^2}\): \(f'=\frac{1-2\ln x}{x^3}<0\) for \(x>\sqrt e\), so decreasing on \([2,\infty)\) (the \(k=1\) term is 0). With \(y=\ln x\), \(x=e^y\): \(\int_2^{n+1}\frac{\ln x}{x^2}dx=\int_{\ln2}^{\ln(n+1)}ye^{-y}dy\to\frac{1+\ln2}2\). Integral test from \(m=2\): \[\frac{1+\ln2}2\le\sum_{k\ge2}\frac{\ln k}{k^2}\le\frac{1+\ln2}2+\frac{\ln2}4.\] \(\frac{\ln x}x\) (decreasing for \(x>e\)): \(\int_3^{n+1}\frac{\ln x}x=\frac12\left((\ln(n+1))^2-(\ln3)^2\right)\to\infty\): diverges. (Faster: \(\frac{\ln k}k\ge\frac1k\) for \(k\ge3\), harmonic minorant.)

Exam questionKlausur WiSe24 · 6d

Does \(\displaystyle\sum_{n\ge3}\frac1{n\ln n\,(\ln\ln n)^2}\) converge?

Solution: yes, integral test

The function \(\frac1{x\ln x(\ln\ln x)^2}\) is positive and decreasing for \(x\ge3\). Substitute \(y=\ln\ln x\), \(dy=\frac{dx}{x\ln x}\): \[\int_3^\infty\frac{dx}{x\ln x(\ln\ln x)^2}=\int_{\ln\ln3}^\infty\frac{dy}{y^2}<\infty\] (since \(\ln\ln3>0\)). So the series converges. Compare: the neighbouring series \(\sum_{n\ge2}\frac1{n\ln n}\) diverges, because the same substitution leaves \(\int\frac{dy}y\). That divergence is its own exam question — SoSe23 A4e, where you are asked to derive it from the mean value theorem instead of the integral test. One extra power of \(\ln\ln\) is the whole difference between the two.

Exam question · the same series without the integral testKlausur SoSe23 · Aufgabe 4 (d), (e)

\(\displaystyle\sum_{n\ge2}\frac1{n\ln n}\) diverges — and this exam does not let you use the integral test. Parts (a)–(c) set up \(f(x)=\ln\ln x\) and \(g(x)=x\ln x\), (d) says "apply the mean value theorem to \(f\) on \([n,n+1]\)", and (e) asks for the conclusion.

The whole argument is worked in §7.2, where the MVT lives. In one line: the MVT turns \(\ln\ln(n+1)-\ln\ln n\) into \(\frac1{\xi\ln\xi}\) with \(\xi\in(n,n+1)\); monotonicity of \(x\ln x\) replaces \(\xi\) by \(n\); and the resulting sum telescopes to \(\ln\ln(N+1)-\ln\ln2\to\infty\).

Extra — read the wording Here the integral test would finish in one line (\(y=\ln x\) turns \(\int\frac{dx}{x\ln x}\) into \(\int\frac{dy}y=\infty\)), but "Wenden Sie den Mittelwertsatz an" removes the choice. When a task prescribes a tool, the prescribed route is the answer; a different correct proof earns little. Conversely, WiSe24 6d prescribes nothing, so use the integral test there.

What you must know for 9.4: estimating sums with integrals; the integral test for series.

Mixed exercises from the script [Kapitel 13]

Aufgabe 13.6 · an improper integralSkript 13.6

\(\displaystyle I=\int_0^\infty xe^{-x^2/2}\,dx\).

Solution

Write it as a limit, then substitute \(u=\frac{x^2}2\), \(du=x\,dx\) (the factor \(x\) is exactly what the substitution needs); the limits \(0\) and \(M\) become \(0\) and \(\frac{M^2}2\): \[I=\lim_{M\to\infty}\int_0^Mxe^{-x^2/2}\,dx=\lim_{M\to\infty}\int_0^{M^2/2}e^{-u}\,du=\lim_{M\to\infty}\left(1-e^{-M^2/2}\right)=1.\]

Aufgabe 13.7 · use the symmetrySkript 13.7

\(\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}xe^{-x^2/2}\,dx\).

Solution

The integrand \(f(x)=xe^{-x^2/2}\) is odd: \(f(-x)=-f(x)\). Splitting at 0 and substituting \(u=-x\) in the left half turns it into the right half with a minus sign: \[\int_{-\infty}^0f(x)\,dx=-\int_0^\infty f(u)\,du=-1,\] using the previous exercise. Hence \(\int_{-\infty}^\infty f=-1+1=0\).

Important: this argument is only allowed because both halves converge separately (each equals \(\pm1\)). For \(\int_{-1}^1\frac{dx}x\) the same symmetry is useless, because each half is infinite and "\(-\infty+\infty\)" is undefined (Bemerkung 9.4). Up to the factor \(\frac1{\sqrt{2\pi}}\) this integral is the expected value of a standard normal random variable — 0, as symmetry demands.

!What the exam asks from Chapter 9

Chapter 9 appears in six of the nine exams, most heavily in WiSe24 (four subtasks):

TypeExam tasksWhat earns the points
Compute an improper integralWiSe19 4b, WiSe22 2b, Skript 13.6write the limit explicitly; substitution; evaluate the limit
Not improper at allWiSe24 6a\(\int_0^1x^{2024}e^{-x^{2025}}dx=\frac{1-e^{-1}}{2025}\) — an ordinary Chapter-8 substitution sitting in the middle of the improper-integral block. Check the interval and the integrand before reaching for a limit
For which parameter?WiSe20 5breduce to the benchmark \(\int y^{-a}\)
Does it exist? (trap)WiSe24 6cfind every point where the integrand can blow up
Integral testWiSe24 6d; companion: SoSe23 A4echeck positive + decreasing; substitute \(y=\ln x\) or \(\ln\ln x\). SoSe23 A4e is the neighbouring series \(\sum\frac1{n\ln n}\), which diverges, and the exam has you show it with the mean value theorem instead
Limits and integralsWiSe24 6b, WiSe24 2bpointwise limit first, then a moving point \(x_n\) to kill uniformity or to show \(\int f_n\not\to\int f\)
Prove uniform convergenceSoSe19 A3the opposite direction: produce a constant majorant \(b_k\) with \(\sum b_k<\infty\) (here \(b_k=\frac1{k^2}\)) — the Weierstrass test, no moving point involved

Drill: does it converge?

What you must be able to do after Chapter 9
  • For the exam: the five task types above; the benchmarks \(\int_1^\infty x^{-\alpha}\), \(\int_0^1x^{-\alpha}\) and \(\sum k^{-s}\) by heart.
  • For Chapter 10: \(\ln(1+x)=\sum(-1)^k\frac{x^{k+1}}{k+1}\) and \(\frac1{1-x}=\sum x^k\) are the model power series; term-by-term integration (Satz 10.10c) is Satz 9.10 again; "radius of convergence" was introduced here (Def. 9.12).
  • For Chapter 11: partial derivatives \(\partial_yf\) (§9.2) become the main tool.

ΣSummary sheet

  1. Improper integral = limit of proper integrals; split at an interior point if both ends are critical; each part must converge separately.
  2. \(\int_1^\infty x^{-\alpha}<\infty\iff\alpha>1\); \(\int_0^1x^{-\alpha}<\infty\iff\alpha<1\). Compare other integrands with these.
  3. Continuous \(f(x,y)\): Fubini; \(\frac d{dy}\int f\,dx=\int\partial_yf\,dx\).
  4. \(\int\sum=\sum\int\) with a convergent majorant; otherwise it can fail (spikes). \(\ln(1+x)=\sum(-1)^k\frac{x^{k+1}}{k+1}\) on \((-1,1]\).
  5. Monotone \(f\): \(\sum f(k)\) is sandwiched by integrals. \(H_n\simeq\ln n\), \(\sum k^\alpha\simeq\frac{n^{\alpha+1}}{\alpha+1}\), \(\ln n!\simeq n\ln n\).
  6. Integral test: for decreasing \(f\ge0\), \(\sum f(k)\) and \(\int_1^\infty f\) converge together.

?Self-test

Analysis für Informatik · MA0902 · Chapter 10

Power SeriesPotenzreihen

The tangent line (Chapter 6) approximates \(f\) to first order. Taylor polynomials go further: they approximate \(f\) near a point by a polynomial of degree \(n\), with an explicit error term. Letting \(n\to\infty\) gives power series, "infinite polynomials" that converge inside a radius of convergence and can be differentiated and integrated term by term.

Source labels: Skript 10.1 = stated in your lecture script · Blatt 14 · 14.2 = exercise sheet · Klausur SoSe23 = old exam · Extra = my own addition (intuition, a proof the script leaves out, or an extra example). A § marks a whole section of the script (§10.1 = Section 10.1) rather than a numbered result (10.1 = Satz 10.1).

0The big picture

  1. Known power series so far: \(e^x=\sum\frac{x^k}{k!}\) (all \(x\)) and \(\ln(1+x)=\sum(-1)^k\frac{x^{k+1}}{k+1}\) — these two are the script's own list at the head of the chapter — plus the geometric series \(\frac1{1-x}=\sum x^k\) (\(|x|<1\)) from Chapter 3.
  2. Why they matter: truncating gives polynomial approximations (numerics); conversely, a closed formula for \(\sum a_kx^k\) (a generating function) tells you things about the \(a_k\) (combinatorics, probability).
  3. 10.1 Taylor's formula: \(f(a+h)=\sum_{k\le n}\frac{f^{(k)}(a)}{k!}h^k+\text{remainder}\). Taylor series = limit, when the remainder → 0.
  4. 10.2 Every power series has a radius \(r\): absolutely convergent for \(|x|<r\), divergent for \(|x|>r\). Inside, differentiate/integrate term by term.
Before you start
  • Higher derivatives and Lemma 7.22 (\(a_l=\frac{f^{(l)}(0)}{l!}\) for polynomials) (§7.5); l’Hospital (§7.4); \(o(h^n)\) notation (§6.1).
  • Integration by parts and the mean value theorem of integration (§8.4, §8.2) — they produce the two remainder formulas.
  • Series: geometric series, ratio test, Cauchy product (§3.1, §3.2, §3.3); \(\limsup\) = largest accumulation point (§4.3).
  • Power series are not new here. The script already defined them, together with the words Potenzreihe and Konvergenzradius, in Definition 9.12, and proved \(\ln(1+x)=\sum(-1)^k\frac{x^{k+1}}{k+1}\) on \((-1,1]\) in Lemma 9.13. What Chapter 10 adds is (i) where the coefficients come from — Taylor — and (ii) that the radius always exists, with a formula for it (Satz 10.7).

10.1Taylor's formula [Taylorsche Formel]

Where the coefficients come from. Suppose \(f(a+h)=f(a)+f'(a)h+a_2h^2+\dots+a_nh^n+o(h^n)\). Then each \(a_k\) is a limit of the form \(\frac{f(a+h)-(\dots)}{h^k}\), which is "\(\frac00\)". Applying l’Hospital \(k\) times gives \(a_k=\frac{f^{(k)}(a)}{k!}\). This matches Lemma 7.22 for polynomials: the coefficients are the derivatives at the centre, divided by factorials.

Notation: centre and distanceExtra

A Taylor polynomial is built around a centre [Entwicklungspunkt] \(a\). The script writes the input either as \(a+h\) (\(h\) = distance from the centre) or as \(x\) (then \(h=x-a\)); both mean the same. Degree by degree: \(n=0\) gives the constant \(f(a)\), \(n=1\) the tangent \(f(a)+f'(a)h\) (Chapter 6), \(n=2\) the parabola that also matches the curvature \(f''(a)\), and so on — each new term matches one more derivative at \(a\).

Satz · Taylor's formulaSkript 10.1

If \(f\) is \(n\) times continuously differentiable near \(a\), then \[f(a+h)=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{f^{(k)}(a)}{k!}h^k+o(h^n)\qquad(h\to0).\] \(T_{n,a}f(h)=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{f^{(k)}(a)}{k!}h^k\) is the \(n\)-th Taylor polynomial [Taylorpolynom] of \(f\) at \(a\). (For \(n=1\): the tangent.)

Where the coefficients come from, and why the error is \(o(h^n)\)

Finding the coefficients. Suppose such an approximation \(f(a+h)=f(a)+f'(a)h+a_2h^2+\dots+a_nh^n+o(h^n)\) exists. Solve for \(a_2\) and let \(h\to0\): \[a_2=\lim_{h\to0}\frac{f(a+h)-f(a)-f'(a)h}{h^2}\overset{\text{l'Hospital}}{=}\lim_{h\to0}\frac{f'(a+h)-f'(a)}{2h}=\frac{f''(a)}2,\] where l'Hospital (Satz 7.15) applies because numerator and denominator both tend to 0, and the last step is the difference quotient of \(f'\) at \(a\). Repeating this \(k\) times gives \(a_k=\frac{f^{(k)}(a)}{k!}\) — the factor \(k!\) appears because differentiating \(h^k\) that often produces \(k!\). Sanity check: for a polynomial this is exactly Lemma 7.22, \(a_k=\frac1{k!}f^{(k)}(0)\), and there the "approximation" is exact.

Why the error really is \(o(h^n)\). This follows from the version with remainder below: the Lagrange form gives \(R_{n+1}=\frac{f^{(n+1)}(\xi)}{(n+1)!}h^{n+1}\), and \(h^{n+1}=o(h^n)\). (Satz 10.1 assumes only \(n\) continuous derivatives, one less than Satz 10.2; in that case one applies the same argument to \(T_{n-1}\) and uses continuity of \(f^{(n)}\) at \(a\).)

Satz · with remainderSkript 10.2 · Restglied

If \(f\) is \((n+1)\) times continuously differentiable on an open interval \(I\), then for all \(x,a\in I\): \[f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{f^{(k)}(a)}{k!}(x-a)^k+R_{n+1}f(a,x),\] \[R_{n+1}f(a,x)=\frac1{n!}\int_a^x(x-t)^nf^{(n+1)}(t)\,dt\ \ \text{(Cauchy)}\ =\ \frac{f^{(n+1)}(\xi)}{(n+1)!}(x-a)^{n+1}\ \ \text{(Lagrange)}\] for some \(\xi\) between \(a\) and \(x\).

Proof

Cauchy form, induction on \(n\). \(n=0\): \(f(x)=f(a)+\int_a^xf'(t)\,dt\) (FTC). Step: integrate the remainder by parts with \(u=f^{(n)}(t)\), \(v'=(x-t)^{n-1}\), \(v=-\frac1n(x-t)^n\): \[\frac1{(n-1)!}\int_a^x(x-t)^{n-1}f^{(n)}(t)\,dt=\frac{f^{(n)}(a)}{n!}(x-a)^n+\frac1{n!}\int_a^x(x-t)^nf^{(n+1)}(t)\,dt.\] The first term is the next Taylor term; the second is the new remainder.

Lagrange form. \((x-t)^n\) has a fixed sign for \(t\) between \(a\) and \(x\), so the MVT of integration (Satz 8.4) with weight \(p(t)=(x-t)^n\) pulls out \(f^{(n+1)}(\xi)\); and \(\int_a^x(x-t)^n\,dt=\frac{(x-a)^{n+1}}{n+1}\) (substitute \(u=x-t\)). ∎

Extra — the sign, carefully Satz 8.4 is stated for a weight \(p\ge0\). For \(a<x\) that is fine, since \(x-t\ge0\). For \(x<a\) and odd \(n\) the weight is \(\le0\), so apply the theorem to \(-p\) instead: the same \(\xi\) comes out, because multiplying both sides by \(-1\) changes nothing about which \(\xi\) exists. This is why the statement only needs "\(\xi\) between \(a\) and \(x\)" and never says which of the two is larger.

How to use Lagrange: the unknown \(\xi\) is harmless if you bound \(|f^{(n+1)}|\) on the whole interval: \(|R_{n+1}|\le\frac{\max|f^{(n+1)}|}{(n+1)!}|x-a|^{n+1}\).

Try it · true error vs. Lagrange bound

For each degree \(n\) the bars show the actual error \(|f(x)-T_n(x)|\) (blue) and the Lagrange bound \(\frac{M_{n+1}}{(n+1)!}|x|^{n+1}\) with \(M_{n+1}=\max|f^{(n+1)}|\) between 0 and \(x\) (amber), on a logarithmic scale. The bound is always above the truth — that is all an exam estimate needs — and the factorial eventually crushes \(|x|^{n+1}\).

Try it · Taylor polynomials hugging the function

Example · exp and a good bound for eSkript 10.3

All derivatives of \(e^x\) are \(e^x\), all equal to 1 at 0. Lagrange: \(e^x=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{x^k}{k!}+\frac{e^\xi}{(n+1)!}x^{n+1}\). At \(x=1\), \(\xi\in[0,1]\) and \(1\le e^\xi\le3\): \[\sum_{k=0}^n\frac1{k!}+\frac1{(n+1)!}\le e\le\sum_{k=0}^n\frac1{k!}+\frac3{(n+1)!}.\] With \(n=10\) the uncertainty is below \(10^{-7}\).

From the exercise sheetBlatt 13 · Abgabeaufgabe 13.3

\(f(x)=e^{x^2}\). (a) \(T_{5,0}f\). (b) A constant \(C\) with \(|f(x)-T_{5,0}f(x)|\le C\) on \([-1,1]\).

Solution (two ways)

(a) Fast way: substitute \(x^2\) into the exp series: \(e^{x^2}=\sum\frac{x^{2k}}{k!}=1+x^2+\frac{x^4}2+\frac{x^6}6+\dots\) Keep the terms of degree ≤ 5: \(T_{5,0}f=1+x^2+\frac12x^4\). (By uniqueness of power series coefficients, Satz 10.10b, this is the Taylor polynomial.) The direct way computes \(f'(0)=0,f''(0)=2,f'''(0)=0,f^{(4)}(0)=12,f^{(5)}(0)=0\) and gets the same.

(b) With \(y=x^2\in[0,1]\) and the Lagrange remainder of \(e^y\) of order 3: \(e^y=1+y+\frac{y^2}2+\frac{e^\xi}{6}y^3\), so \(|f(x)-T_5(x)|=\frac{e^\xi}6x^6\le\frac36=\frac12\). (The direct route via \(f^{(6)}\) gives the weaker \(C=\frac{173}{30}\). Both are valid answers.)

Korollar · Taylor seriesSkript 10.4 (Korollar), 10.5 (Beispiel)

If \(f\in C^\infty\) and \(R_nf(a,x)\to0\), then \(f(x)=\sum_{k=0}^\infty\frac{f^{(k)}(a)}{k!}(x-a)^k\), the Taylor series [Taylorreihe].

The condition \(R_n\to0\) cannot be dropped (Skript 10.5): \(f(x)=e^{-1/x^2}\) (\(f(0)=0\)) is \(C^\infty\) with \(f^{(n)}(0)=0\) for all \(n\). Its Taylor series at 0 is the zero series, but \(f(x)>0\) for \(x\neq0\). The function is too flat at 0 for any polynomial to "see" it. (The script computes \(f'(0)=f''(0)=0\) by the difference quotient: e.g. \(f'(0)=\lim_{h\to0}\frac1he^{-1/h^2}=\lim_{y\to\infty}\frac{\pm y}{e^{y^2}}=0\) with \(y=\frac1h\); the higher derivatives work the same way, because every derivative is a polynomial in \(\frac1x\) times \(e^{-1/x^2}\), and the exponential wins.)

Binomial seriesSkript 10.6

For \(a\in\mathbb R\), \(f(x)=(1+x)^a\) has \(\frac{f^{(k)}(0)}{k!}=\frac{a(a-1)\cdots(a-k+1)}{k!}=:\binom ak\), and for \(|x|<1\): \[(1+x)^a=\sum_{k=0}^\infty\binom akx^k.\] For \(a=n\in\mathbb N\), \(\binom nk=0\) for \(k>n\): the ordinary binomial theorem. Example: \(\sqrt{1+x}=1+\frac12x-\frac18x^2+\dots\) (\(\binom{1/2}2=\frac{\frac12\cdot(-\frac12)}2=-\frac18\)).

That the remainder tends to 0 for \(|x|<1\) is stated in the script without proof ("man kann zeigen"). You only need the formula and its range.

Exam questionsWiSe19 2a · SoSe23 1e, 1f · WiSe23 6b

WiSe19 2a: Taylor series of \(e^x\) at 0: \(e^x=\sum_{k=0}^\infty\frac{x^k}{k!}\), \(x\in\mathbb R\).

SoSe23 1e: \(T_{20,0}f\) for \(f(x)=\ln(1+x^2)\)

Substitute \(x^2\) into \(\ln(1+y)=\sum_{k\ge1}(-1)^{k+1}\frac{y^k}k\): \(\ln(1+x^2)=\sum_{k\ge1}(-1)^{k+1}\frac{x^{2k}}k\). Degree ≤ 20 means \(k\le10\): \[T_{20,0}f(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{10}(-1)^{k+1}\frac{x^{2k}}k=x^2-\frac{x^4}2+\frac{x^6}3-\dots-\frac{x^{20}}{10}.\] Never differentiate 20 times; substitute into a known series.

SoSe23 1f: value of \(\sum_{k\ge0}2\binom{1/3}k(-8)^{-k}x^k\)

Binomial series with \(a=\frac13\) and \(-\frac x8\) in place of \(x\): \(2\left(1-\frac x8\right)^{1/3}=2\cdot\frac{\sqrt[3]{8-x}}{\sqrt[3]8}=\sqrt[3]{8-x}\), valid for \(|x|<8\).

WiSe23 6b: Taylor series of \(f(x)=\frac13x^3+x+\frac23\) at \(x_0=1\), written out

\(f'=x^2+1\), \(f''=2x\), \(f'''=2\), higher = 0. At 1: \(f=2,f'=2,f''=2,f'''=2\). So \[T_{x_0}f(x)=2+2(x-1)+\frac22(x-1)^2+\frac26(x-1)^3.\] Expanding gives back \(\frac13x^3+x+\frac23\): the Taylor series of a polynomial is the polynomial, just re-centred.

Watch the notation The exam writes \(T_{x_0}f\) for the whole Taylor series about \(x_0\), with only the centre in the subscript. This guide (and the script, Satz 10.1) writes \(T_{n,a}f\) with the degree first, so the same object here is \(T_{3,1}f\) — everything beyond degree 3 vanishes. When a task uses a subscript, check from its own definition whether it means a degree or a centre before you start.

What you must know for 10.1: Taylor's formula including the Cauchy and Lagrange remainders; when a function can be expanded into its Taylor series; the binomial series.

10.2Radius of convergence and analytic functions

SatzSkript 10.7 · Cauchy–Hadamard

For every power series \(\sum a_kz^k\) there is \(r\in[0,\infty]\), the radius of convergence, such that the series converges absolutely for \(|z|<r\) and diverges for \(|z|>r\). It is \[r=\frac1{\limsup_{k\to\infty}\sqrt[k]{|a_k|}}\qquad\left(\tfrac10=\infty,\ \tfrac1\infty=0\right).\] Nothing is said about \(|z|=r\): the boundary points must be checked separately.

\(\limsup_k\sqrt[k]{|a_k|}\) is the largest accumulation point of the sequence \(\sqrt[k]{|a_k|}\) (Chapter 4); if the sequence converges, it is simply the limit.

What is actually new The name "radius of convergence" was already given in Definition 9.12, as the largest bound up to which a power-series expansion is valid. Satz 10.7 is what makes that definition safe: it says such a radius exists for every power series — the set of \(x\) where it converges is always an interval around 0, never something stranger — and it hands you a formula to compute it.

Why a radius exists — the idea Extra — the script states 10.7 without proof

Suppose \(\sqrt[k]{|a_k|}\to L\) (the common case). Then \(\sqrt[k]{|a_kz^k|}=\sqrt[k]{|a_k|}\,|z|\to L|z|\). If \(L|z|<1\), pick \(q\) with \(L|z|<q<1\); eventually \(|a_kz^k|\le q^k\), a convergent geometric majorant (Satz 3.7) — absolute convergence. If \(L|z|>1\), eventually \(|a_kz^k|\ge1\), so the terms do not tend to 0 (Lemma 3.4) — divergence. The border is \(|z|=\frac1L=r\). With lim sup instead of lim the same argument works for the largest accumulation point.

Ratio shortcut: if \(\left|\frac{a_{k+1}}{a_k}\right|\to q\), the ratio test (Satz 3.13) applied to \(\sum a_kz^k\) gives convergence for \(q|z|<1\) and divergence for \(q|z|>1\), so \(r=\frac1q\) (with \(\frac10=\infty\)).

Method · finding r and the convergence set DExtra (as in Blatt 14.2)
  1. Ratio test on the terms (often easiest): \(\left|\frac{a_{k+1}x^{k+1}}{a_kx^k}\right|\to q|x|\). Converges for \(q|x|<1\), so \(r=\frac1q\). (Or root test / Cauchy–Hadamard with \(\sqrt[k]{|a_k|}\); useful fact: \(\sqrt[k]k\to1\).)
  2. Substitution if the series is geometric in disguise: \(\sum(3x)^k\) is geometric in \(y=3x\).
  3. Check both endpoints \(x=\pm r\) by hand (Leibniz, harmonic, terms ↛ 0, …).
From the exercise sheetBlatt 14 · Abgabeaufgabe 14.2 (a)

(a) For each series, give the radius of convergence \(r\) and the full convergence set \(D\) — so the endpoints have to be decided separately. (Part (b) of the same task asks for the value of \(\sum_{k\ge1}\frac k{5^k}\) and is done below, with the differentiated geometric series.)

seriesrDboundary
\(\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{(-x)^k}k\)1\((-1,1]\)\(x=1\): \(\sum\frac{(-1)^k}k\) Leibniz ✓; \(x=-1\): harmonic ✗
\(\sum_{k\ge0}(3x)^k\)\(\frac13\)\((-\frac13,\frac13)\)terms \((\pm1)^k\not\to0\) ✗
\(\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{x^{2k}}{3^k}\)\(\sqrt3\)\((-\sqrt3,\sqrt3)\)terms \(=1\) ✗ (ratio \(\frac{x^2}3<1\))
\(\sum_{k\ge0}k!\,x^k\)0\(\{0\}\)ratio \((k+1)|x|\to\infty\) for \(x\neq0\)
Try it · inside, outside and on the boundary

The first 60 partial sums \(s_n(x)\) at the chosen \(x\) (starting at whichever index the series itself starts at). The number line shows the convergence set \(D\).

ExampleSkript 10.8

The geometric series \(\sum x^k\) has \(a_k=1\), so \(\sqrt[k]{|a_k|}=1\) and \(r=1\).

DefinitionSkript 10.9

\(f\) is analytic at 0 [analytisch] if \(f(x)=\sum a_kx^k\) for \(|x|<r\) with some \(r>0\). (\(e^{-1/x^2}\) is \(C^\infty\) but not analytic at 0.)

Satz · calculus with power seriesSkript 10.10

Let \(f(x)=\sum a_kx^k\) (\(|x|<r_f\)), \(g(x)=\sum b_kx^k\) (\(|x|<r_g\)).

  1. Differentiate term by term: \(f'(x)=\sum_{k\ge1}ka_kx^{k-1}\) for \(|x|<r_f\).
  2. Coefficients are unique: \(a_k=\frac{f^{(k)}(0)}{k!}\). (So any way of finding a power series, e.g. substitution, gives the Taylor series.)
  3. Integrate term by term: \(\int_0^xf=\sum\frac{a_k}{k+1}x^{k+1}\).
  4. \(f+g\) and \(fg\) are analytic, with \(\sum(a_k+b_k)x^k\) and the Cauchy product \(\sum_k\left(\sum_{j\le k}a_jb_{k-j}\right)x^k\), for \(|x|<\min(r_f,r_g)\).

Stated without proof in the script. Useful facts behind it: the differentiated and integrated series have the same radius (since \(\sqrt[k]k\to1\)); (b) follows from (a) by differentiating \(k\) times and setting \(x=0\) — exactly as in Lemma 7.22; (c) is Satz 9.10 with the majorant \(|a_k|\rho^k\) for \(|x|\le\rho<r\); (d) is the Cauchy product, Satz 3.23.

Example · derivatives of the geometric seriesSkript 10.11 · Blatt 14.2b · Klausur WiSe19 1e

For \(|x|<1\): \(\frac1{(1-x)^2}=\left(\frac1{1-x}\right)'=\sum_{k\ge1}kx^{k-1}\), \(\ \frac2{(1-x)^3}=\sum_{k\ge2}k(k-1)x^{k-2}\).

  • Blatt 14.2b: multiply by \(x\): \(\sum_{k\ge1}kx^k=\frac x{(1-x)^2}\). At \(x=\frac15\): \(\sum\frac k{5^k}=\frac{1/5}{(4/5)^2}=\frac5{16}\).
  • WiSe19 1e: \(\sum_{n\ge1}n3^nx^n=\sum n(3x)^n=\frac{3x}{(1-3x)^2}\), converging iff \(|3x|<1\): \(M=(-\frac13,\frac13)\).
  • Geometric distribution Skript, Anwendung after 10.11 — marked ausgelassen: \(P(X=k)=p(1-p)^{k-1}\) (first success at trial \(k\)). \(E[X]=\sum kp(1-p)^{k-1}=\frac p{(1-(1-p))^2}=\frac1p\). On average \(\frac1p\) trials until the first success.

Note That last bullet is the one place in this chapter where the script writes "ausgelassen" in the margin — the lecture skipped it, and it is not in the Minimale Inhalte. Keep it as a sanity check that you can differentiate a power series and as motivation (it is the same computation you will meet in Diskrete Wahrscheinlichkeitstheorie), but do not expect it in the exam. The two bullets above it are examinable.

Exam questionsWiSe24 1b · WiSe22 1c, 1d
WiSe24 1b: radius of convergence of \(\sum_{k\ge1}k!\,e^{-k^2}x^k\)

Ratio: \(\frac{(k+1)!e^{-(k+1)^2}}{k!e^{-k^2}}|x|=(k+1)e^{-2k-1}|x|\to0\) for every \(x\) (exp beats polynomial). So \(r=\infty\).

WiSe22 1c/1d: determine \(M_1,M_2\) and the value of the series on them

Both parts read "Bestimmen Sie die Menge \(M\)" of all \(x\) for which the series converges, and its value there. Recognising the series is the whole task — no criterion is needed.

1c: \(S_1(x)=\sum_{k\ge0}4^{2k}x^k=\sum_{k\ge0}(16x)^k\), since \(4^{2k}=16^k\). Geometric, so it converges iff \(|16x|<1\): \[M_1=\left(-\tfrac1{16},\tfrac1{16}\right),\qquad S_1(x)=\frac1{1-16x}.\]

1d: \(S_2(x)=\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{x^{2k}}{5^kk!}=\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{\left(\frac{x^2}5\right)^k}{k!}\) — the exponential series in the variable \(y=\frac{x^2}5\), with the \(k=0\) term (which is 1) missing: \[M_2=\mathbb R,\qquad S_2(x)=e^{x^2/5}-1.\] The exponential series converges for every real argument, and \(\frac{x^2}5\) is a real number for every \(x\), so there is no restriction at all.

Extra — the trick both parts share Each series is a known series in a substituted variable: \(16x\) in 1c, \(\frac{x^2}5\) in 1d. So the method is: force the series into the shape \(\sum u^k\) or \(\sum\frac{u^k}{k!}\), read off the value from the known formula, and translate the condition on \(u\) back into a condition on \(x\). Watch the starting index — here \(k\ge1\), which costs exactly the \(k=0\) term.

What you must know for 10.2: what the radius of convergence is and how to determine it; the derivative, an antiderivative and the coefficients of a power series.

!What the exam asks from Chapter 10

Chapter 10 appears in five of the nine exams, almost always as short answer-only items:

TypeExam tasksWhat earns the points
Taylor polynomial of a compositeSoSe23 1e, Blatt 13.3substitute into a known series; cut at the right degree
Taylor series written out / re-centredWiSe19 2a, WiSe23 6bderivatives at the centre, divide by \(k!\)
Value of a power seriesSoSe23 1f, WiSe19 1e, WiSe22 1c/1drecognise a known series in a substituted variable — \(16x\), \(\frac{x^2}5\), \(3x\), \(-\frac x8\) — possibly differentiated; then translate the condition back to \(x\), and mind the starting index
Radius of convergenceWiSe24 1b, Blatt 14.2ratio of consecutive coefficients; boundary by hand

Drill: Taylor polynomials, values, radii

What you must be able to do after Chapter 10
  • For the exam: the five task types above, and the table of standard series in the summary by heart.
  • For Chapter 11: the second-order Taylor expansion in several variables, \(f(x+h)\approx f(x)+\nabla f(x)^th+\frac12h^tH_f(x)h\), is why the Hessian decides extrema — the same idea as \(f''(x_0)\) in one variable.
  • For Chapter 12: \(e^{\lambda x}\) with complex \(\lambda\) (Euler's formula) produces the oscillating solutions of second-order ODEs.

ΣSummary sheet

\(\sin x\), \(\cos x\)\(\sum(-1)^k\frac{x^{2k+1}}{(2k+1)!}\), \(\sum(-1)^k\frac{x^{2k}}{(2k)!}\)ℝ
\(\frac1{1-x}\)\(\sum x^k\)\(|x|<1\)

\((1+x)^a\)\(\sum\binom akx^k\)\(|x|<1\)
  1. \(T_{n,a}f(x)=\sum_{k\le n}\frac{f^{(k)}(a)}{k!}(x-a)^k\); error \(o((x-a)^n)\), or exactly \(\frac{f^{(n+1)}(\xi)}{(n+1)!}(x-a)^{n+1}\) (Lagrange).
  2. To get Taylor polynomials of composite functions, substitute into known series (uniqueness makes it legitimate).
  3. Radius via ratio or root test; boundary points by hand.
  4. Inside the radius: differentiate/integrate term by term.
