Analysis für Informatik · MA0902 · Chapter 11

Several VariablesDifferentialrechnung mehrerer Veränderlicher

Functions of several inputs, like \(f(x,y)\). Differentiate in one variable at a time (partial derivatives), collect them in the gradient, the second derivatives in the Hessian matrix, and use both to find and classify extrema. This extrema task appears in most recent exams.

Source labels: Skript 11.6 = stated in your lecture script · Blatt 14 · 14.3 = exercise sheet · Klausur SoSe23 = old exam · Extra = my own addition (intuition, a proof the script leaves out, or an extra example). A § marks a whole section of the script (§11.3 = Section 11.3) rather than a numbered result (11.3 = Definition 11.3).

0The big picture

Everything is the one-variable theory with vectors and matrices in place of numbers:

one variableseveral variables
\(f'(x)\)gradient \(\nabla f(x)\) (vector of partial derivatives)
tangent linetangent plane \(f(x_0)+Df(x_0)(x-x_0)\)
\(f''(x)\)Hessian \(Hf(x)\) (matrix of second partials)
\(f'(x_0)=0\)\(\nabla f(x_0)=0\) (critical point)
\(f''(x_0)>0\) ⇒ min\(Hf(x_0)\) positive definite (all eigenvalues \(>0\)) ⇒ min
—saddle point: eigenvalues of both signs

Roadmap. §11.1 treats functions out of one variable into \(\mathbb R^n\) (curves): differentiate each component. §11.2 treats functions from \(\mathbb R^n\) into \(\mathbb R\): differentiate in one variable at a time, and Lemma 11.7 says these partial derivatives together give the best linear approximation. §11.3 goes to second order (Hessian) — which is exactly what decides extrema, just as \(f''\) did in Chapter 7 — and ends with maps \(\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^m\) (Jacobian), where everything is combined.

Before you start
  • All one-variable derivative rules — product, chain, exp/ln rewriting (§6.3) — and \(o(\cdot)\) notation (§6.1). Partial derivatives are nothing but these rules applied with the other variables frozen.
  • Local extrema in one variable: \(f'(x_0)=0\) is necessary, \(f''(x_0)\gtrless0\) sufficient (§7.1, §7.5); the mean value theorem (§7.2), used in the proof of Lemma 11.7.
  • The second-order Taylor formula (§10.1): its several-variable version is why the Hessian decides.
  • Length of a vector \(\|x\|=\sqrt{x_1^2+\dots+x_n^2}\) and the scalar product (norms, Chapter 1).
  • Matrices, eigenvalues and definiteness — not in the script's earlier chapters, so they are explained from scratch right below.

The linear algebra you need here Extra

The script assumes matrices, eigenvalues and definiteness are known. Here is everything this chapter actually uses, from scratch. If it is familiar, skip ahead.

Vectors, matrices, transposeExtra

A vector \(x\in\mathbb R^n\) is a list of \(n\) numbers; we write it as a column. The transpose [Transponierte] \(x^t\) is the same list written as a row. A matrix \(A\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}\) is a square table of numbers \(A_{ij}\) (row \(i\), column \(j\)); \(A^t\) is the table mirrored along its diagonal, and \(A\) is symmetric [symmetrisch] if \(A=A^t\), i.e. \(A_{ij}=A_{ji}\).

Matrix times vector: \((Ax)_i=\sum_jA_{ij}x_j\) — row \(i\) of \(A\), multiplied entrywise with \(x\) and summed. The number \(x^tAx=\sum_{i,j}x_iA_{ij}x_j\) is a quadratic form [quadratische Form]. For \(n=2\), with \(A=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&c\end{pmatrix}\): \[\begin{pmatrix}x&y\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&c\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}x\\y\end{pmatrix}=ax^2+2bxy+cy^2.\] So a quadratic form is just a homogeneous quadratic polynomial, and \(x^tAx\) is the several-variable version of \(ax^2\). That is why the Hessian plays the role that \(f''\) plays in one variable.

EigenvaluesExtra

\(\lambda\in\mathbb R\) is an eigenvalue [Eigenwert] of \(A\) if there is a vector \(v\neq0\) (an eigenvector) with \[Av=\lambda v.\] In words: \(v\) is a direction that \(A\) only stretches (by the factor \(\lambda\)) instead of turning. Eigenvalues are the roots of the characteristic polynomial \(\det(A-\lambda I)=0\). For \(2\times2\) this is \[\lambda^2-\underbrace{(a+c)}_{\text{trace}}\lambda+\underbrace{(ac-b^2)}_{\det}=0,\qquad \lambda_{1,2}=\frac{a+c}2\pm\sqrt{\left(\frac{a+c}2\right)^2-(ac-b^2)}.\] A symmetric matrix always has real eigenvalues, and one can choose eigenvectors that are perpendicular to each other. Note \(\lambda_1\lambda_2=\det A\) and \(\lambda_1+\lambda_2=a+c\) — this is what makes the shortcut below work.

Example: \(A=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&4\end{pmatrix}\) has eigenvalues 2 and 4 (the coordinate directions are stretched by 2 and 4). \(A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}\): \(\lambda^2-4\lambda+3=0\), so \(\lambda=1,3\).

Definiteness — and why eigenvalues decideExtra

A symmetric \(A\) is called

  • positive definite if \(x^tAx>0\) for every \(x\neq0\) (a bowl opening upwards) ⇔ all eigenvalues \(>0\);
  • negative definite if \(x^tAx<0\) for every \(x\neq0\) ⇔ all eigenvalues \(<0\);
  • indefinite if \(x^tAx\) takes both signs ⇔ eigenvalues of both signs (a saddle);
  • semidefinite if \(\ge0\) (resp. \(\le0\)) everywhere, with 0 occurring ⇔ all eigenvalues \(\ge0\) (resp. \(\le0\)) and some eigenvalue is 0.

Why the eigenvalues decide: write \(x\) in a perpendicular eigenvector basis, \(x=t_1v_1+t_2v_2\) with \(\|v_i\|=1\). Then \(Ax=\lambda_1t_1v_1+\lambda_2t_2v_2\) and \[x^tAx=\lambda_1t_1^2+\lambda_2t_2^2.\] The signs of \(\lambda_1,\lambda_2\) are exactly the signs this expression can take. With \(\lambda_1,\lambda_2>0\) it is a sum of positive terms; with opposite signs you get \(+\) along \(v_1\) and \(-\) along \(v_2\) — a saddle.

Method · classifying a \(2\times2\) Hessian in 10 secondsExtra

For \(H=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&c\end{pmatrix}\) compute \(\det H=ac-b^2\) and look at \(a\):

\(\det H\)\(a\)conclusion at a critical point
\(>0\)\(>0\)positive definite ⇒ strict local minimum
\(>0\)\(<0\)negative definite ⇒ strict local maximum
\(<0\)anyindefinite ⇒ saddle point
\(=0\)anyan eigenvalue is 0 ⇒ test says nothing, look at \(f\) directly

Why: \(\det H=\lambda_1\lambda_2\), so \(\det H>0\) means equal signs and \(\det H<0\) opposite signs; with equal signs, the sign of \(a=e_1^tHe_1\) tells you which. You may always compute the eigenvalues instead — the exam solutions usually do — but the determinant test is faster and harder to get wrong.

Three or more variables (e.g. WiSe24 5a has \(x_1,x_2,x_3\)): there is no one-line determinant test in this course; compute the eigenvalues. When the Hessian is diagonal — common in exams, whenever \(f\) is a sum of one-variable functions like \(x^3+y^4\) — the eigenvalues are simply the diagonal entries.

Try it · the shape of \(ax^2+2bxy+cy^2\)

Choose the entries of \(H=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&c\end{pmatrix}\). Green = \(q(x,y)=ax^2+2bxy+cy^2>0\), red = \(q<0\), with level lines. The arrows are the eigenvector directions, labelled with their eigenvalues. Near a critical point \(f(x_0+h)\approx f(x_0)+\tfrac12q(h)\), so this picture is what \(f\) looks like there.

11.1Curves [Kurven]

DefinitionSkript 11.1, 11.3

A parametrised curve is a map \(\gamma:[a,b]\to\mathbb R^n,\ t\mapsto(\gamma_1(t),\dots,\gamma_n(t))\) with continuous components; think of \(\gamma(t)\) as the position of a moving point at time \(t\). The set \(\gamma([a,b])\) is its trace [Spur].

If differentiable: \(\gamma'(t)=(\gamma_1'(t),\dots,\gamma_n'(t))\) is the tangent / velocity vector, \(\|\gamma'(t)\|\) the speed, and \(T_\gamma(t)=\frac{\gamma'(t)}{\|\gamma'(t)\|}\) (if \(\gamma'(t)\neq0\)) the unit tangent vector.

ExamplesSkript 11.2, 11.4, 11.5
  • \(\alpha(t)=(\cos t,\sin t)\) and \(\beta(t)=(\cos t,-\sin t)\), \(t\in[0,2\pi]\): same trace (unit circle), different curves (opposite direction). \(\alpha'(t)=(-\sin t,\cos t)\) has length 1 and is perpendicular to \(\alpha(t)\) (\(\langle\alpha,\alpha'\rangle=0\)).
  • Graph of \(f\) as a curve: \(\gamma(t)=(t,f(t))\), \(T_\gamma(t)=\frac{(1,f'(t))}{\sqrt{1+f'(t)^2}}\).
  • Extra A computed example: the helix \(\gamma(t)=(\cos t,\sin t,t)\) has \(\gamma'(t)=(-\sin t,\cos t,1)\), speed \(\|\gamma'(t)\|=\sqrt{\sin^2t+\cos^2t+1}=\sqrt2\), and \(T_\gamma(t)=\frac1{\sqrt2}(-\sin t,\cos t,1)\).
  • Caution: \(\gamma(t_1)=\gamma(t_2)\) does not imply \(\gamma'(t_1)=\gamma'(t_2)\) (a curve can pass through a point twice in different directions).

What you must know for 11.1: what a curve is; determining its tangent vector.

11.2Partial derivatives [Partielle Ableitungen]

DefinitionSkript 11.6

For \(f:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R\), the partial derivative \(\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_k}(x)=\partial_kf(x)\) is the ordinary derivative of \(\xi\mapsto f(x_1,\dots,x_{k-1},\xi,x_{k+1},\dots,x_n)\) at \(\xi=x_k\): differentiate in \(x_k\), treat all other variables as constants. The gradient is the column vector \(\nabla f(x)=\operatorname{grad}f(x)=(\partial_1f(x),\dots,\partial_nf(x))^t\); \(Df(x)=\nabla f(x)^t\) is the same as a row.

Extra Tiny example: \(f(x,y)=x^2y\). For \(\partial_xf\) pretend \(y\) is a number like 5: \((5x^2)'=10x\), so \(\partial_xf=2xy\). For \(\partial_yf\) pretend \(x\) is a number: \(\partial_yf=x^2\). So \(\nabla f(x,y)=(2xy,\ x^2)^t\), and at \((1,3)\): \(\nabla f=(6,1)^t\).

Try it · a partial derivative is the slope of one slice

Freeze \(y=y_0\): you get a function of \(x\) alone (green), whose slope at \(x_0\) is \(\partial_xf(x_0,y_0)\). Freeze \(x=x_0\) instead (amber): its slope at \(y_0\) is \(\partial_yf(x_0,y_0)\). The map on the left shows where the two slices run.

Lemma · total differentiabilitySkript 11.7

If \(f\) is continuously partially differentiable, then \[f(x+h)=f(x)+Df(x)\,h+o(\|h\|)\qquad(h\to0\text{ in }\mathbb R^n),\] exactly like the one-dimensional linearisation. The tangent plane to the graph at \((x_0,f(x_0))\) is \(x\mapsto f(x_0)+Df(x_0)(x-x_0)\).

What it says. The partial derivatives only look along the coordinate axes. The lemma says that, if they are continuous, they already determine the behaviour in every direction: the error of the linear approximation is small compared with \(\|h\|\), however \(h\) approaches 0. A function with this property is called (totally) differentiable [(total) differenzierbar] (Definition 11.18 below).

Proof for two variables (the script states the lemma without proof) Extra

Let \(x=(x_1,x_2)\), \(h=(h_1,h_2)\). Go from \(x\) to \(x+h\) in two axis-parallel steps: \[f(x+h)-f(x)=\underbrace{f(x_1+h_1,x_2+h_2)-f(x_1,x_2+h_2)}_{\text{only }x_1\text{ changes}}+\underbrace{f(x_1,x_2+h_2)-f(x_1,x_2)}_{\text{only }x_2\text{ changes}}.\] Each bracket is a difference of a one-variable function, so the mean value theorem (Satz 7.4) gives points \(\xi_1\) between \(x_1\) and \(x_1+h_1\), \(\xi_2\) between \(x_2\) and \(x_2+h_2\) with \[f(x+h)-f(x)=\partial_1f(\xi_1,x_2+h_2)\,h_1+\partial_2f(x_1,\xi_2)\,h_2.\] Subtract \(Df(x)h=\partial_1f(x)h_1+\partial_2f(x)h_2\): \[r(h):=f(x+h)-f(x)-Df(x)h=\big(\partial_1f(\xi_1,x_2+h_2)-\partial_1f(x)\big)h_1+\big(\partial_2f(x_1,\xi_2)-\partial_2f(x)\big)h_2.\] Since \(|h_i|\le\|h\|\), \(\frac{|r(h)|}{\|h\|}\le\big|\partial_1f(\xi_1,x_2+h_2)-\partial_1f(x)\big|+\big|\partial_2f(x_1,\xi_2)-\partial_2f(x)\big|\). As \(h\to0\) the points \((\xi_1,x_2+h_2)\) and \((x_1,\xi_2)\) tend to \(x\), so by continuity of the partial derivatives both terms tend to 0. Hence \(r(h)=o(\|h\|)\). For \(n\) variables, change one coordinate at a time in \(n\) steps; the argument is the same.

Why "continuous" matters — a counterexample Extra

\(f(x,y)=\frac{xy}{x^2+y^2}\) for \((x,y)\neq0\), \(f(0,0)=0\). On both axes \(f=0\), so \(\partial_xf(0,0)=\partial_yf(0,0)=0\) exist. But on the diagonal \(f(t,t)=\frac12\) for every \(t\neq0\): \(f\) is not even continuous at 0, so certainly \(f(h)\neq f(0)+0\cdot h+o(\|h\|)\). The partial derivatives exist but are not continuous at 0, and the lemma does not apply. Existence of partial derivatives alone says little about the other directions.

ExamplesSkript 11.8, 11.9
  • Linear \(f(x)=\langle a,x\rangle=\sum a_kx_k\): \(\partial_kf=a_k\), \(\nabla f=a\) (like \((ax)'=a\)); the linearisation is exact.
  • Quadratic \(f(x)=x^tAx\) with symmetric \(A\): \(\nabla f(x)=2Ax\) (like \((ax^2)'=2ax\)), and \(f(x+h)=f(x)+2x^tAh+h^tAh\) with \(h^tAh=o(\|h\|)\).
Where \(2x^tAh\) and \(o(\|h\|)\) come from Extra

Multiply out: \((x+h)^tA(x+h)=x^tAx+x^tAh+h^tAx+h^tAh\). The number \(h^tAx\) equals its own transpose \(x^tA^th=x^tAh\) because \(A\) is symmetric, so the middle terms give \(2x^tAh=(2Ax)^th\). This is the linear part, so \(Df(x)=2x^tA\) and \(\nabla f(x)=2Ax\).

The rest is small: \(|h^tAh|=\big|\sum_{i,j}h_iA_{ij}h_j\big|\le\sum_{i,j}|A_{ij}|\,|h_i|\,|h_j|\le C\|h\|^2\) with \(C=\sum_{i,j}|A_{ij}|\), using \(|h_i|\le\|h\|\). Hence \(\frac{|h^tAh|}{\|h\|}\le C\|h\|\to0\): it is \(o(\|h\|)\), even \(O(\|h\|^2)\). Check with \(n=1\): \((x+h)^2a=ax^2+2axh+ah^2\).

From the exercise sheetBlatt 14 · 14.3 (a), (b)
  • \(f(x,y)=3x^4+xy^2-2\sin(e^y)\): \(\partial_xf=12x^3+y^2\), \(\partial_yf=2xy-2\cos(e^y)e^y\), \(\partial_x\partial_yf=2y\).
  • \(f(x,y)=(x^2+5)^y=e^{y\ln(x^2+5)}\): \(\nabla f=\left(\frac{2xy}{x^2+5}(x^2+5)^y,\ \ln(x^2+5)(x^2+5)^y\right)^t\). Variable exponent → rewrite with exp/ln first (Chapter 6).
Exam questionsWiSe19 1g · WiSe24 5a

WiSe19 1g: \(f(x,y)=\sin(3x^2y)\): \(\partial_xf=6xy\cos(3x^2y)\); \(\partial_yf=3x^2\cos(3x^2y)\), so \(\partial_y\partial_yf=-9x^4\sin(3x^2y)\).

WiSe24 5a: \(f=\sin(x_1+x_2^2)e^{x_1+x_3}\): \(\partial_1f=\cos(x_1+x_2^2)e^{x_1+x_3}+\sin(x_1+x_2^2)e^{x_1+x_3}\) (product rule), \(\partial_2f=2x_2\cos(x_1+x_2^2)e^{x_1+x_3}\) (chain rule), \(\partial_3f=\sin(x_1+x_2^2)e^{x_1+x_3}\).

What you must know for 11.2: for \(f:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R\), the partial derivatives, grad f, Df and tangent planes; the notion of total differentiability.

11.3Higher derivatives and extrema

DefinitionSkript 11.10, 11.12

Second partial derivatives: \(\partial_j\partial_if=\frac{\partial^2f}{\partial x_j\partial x_i}\). The Hessian matrix [Hesse-Matrix] is \(Hf(x)=\left(\partial_j\partial_if(x)\right)_{i,j}\).

Satz · SchwarzSkript 11.11, 11.13

If all \(\partial_i\partial_jf\) are continuous, then \(\partial_i\partial_jf=\partial_j\partial_if\): the order of differentiation does not matter, and the Hessian is symmetric. Example 11.11: \(f=\cos(xy)+\ln x-\ln y\) has \(\partial_x\partial_yf=\partial_y\partial_xf=-\sin(xy)-xy\cos(xy)\).

Exam question · 11 pointsKlausur WiSe23 · A5

For a vector field \(v=(v_1,v_2,v_3):\mathbb R^3\to\mathbb R^3\) the rotation [Rotation] is \(\operatorname{rot}(v)=(\partial_yv_3-\partial_zv_2,\ \partial_zv_1-\partial_xv_3,\ \partial_xv_2-\partial_yv_1)^t\). The exam defines it on the spot; you only need to apply the definition.

(a) For \(f\in C^\infty(\mathbb R^3)\) show \(\operatorname{rot}(\nabla f)=0\)

With \(v=\nabla f=(\partial_xf,\partial_yf,\partial_zf)\): \[\operatorname{rot}(\nabla f)=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_y\partial_zf-\partial_z\partial_yf\\\partial_z\partial_xf-\partial_x\partial_zf\\\partial_x\partial_yf-\partial_y\partial_xf\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}0\\0\\0\end{pmatrix},\] because \(f\) is at least twice continuously differentiable, so by Schwarz the order of the two partial derivatives may be swapped. Name the theorem and its hypothesis — that is where the points are.

(b) Give \(v_1,v_2,v_3\), polynomials of degree \(\ge2\) in each of \(x,y,z\), with \(\operatorname{rot}(v)=0\)

Use (a) backwards: any gradient field works. Take \(f=x^3y^3z^3\) and \(v=\nabla f=(3x^2y^3z^3,\ 3x^3y^2z^3,\ 3x^3y^3z^2)\). Every component has degree \(\ge2\) in each variable, and \(\operatorname{rot}(v)=0\) by (a) — no need to verify by hand (though e.g. \(\partial_yv_3=9x^3y^2z^2=\partial_zv_2\)).

(c) \(v=\big(\tfrac13z^3+\sin z-2y,\ \tfrac13x^3+\sin x-2z,\ \tfrac13y^3+\cos y-2x\big)\): find \(f\) with \(\nabla f=v\), or show none exists

Compute \(\operatorname{rot}(v)\): \(\partial_yv_3-\partial_zv_2=(y^2-\sin y)-(-2)=y^2-\sin y+2\), and similarly the others give \(z^2+\cos z+2\) and \(x^2+\cos x+2\). Since \(y^2\ge0\) and \(|\sin y|\le1\), the first component is \(\ge1>0\) everywhere. So \(\operatorname{rot}(v)\neq0\). If \(v=\nabla f\) with \(f\in C^\infty\), (a) would give \(\operatorname{rot}(v)=0\) — contradiction. No such \(f\) exists.

Extra The pattern: "rot \(\neq0\) ⇒ not a gradient" is always valid. The converse ("rot \(=0\) ⇒ gradient") is true on all of \(\mathbb R^3\) but is not part of this course.

Open setsSkript 11.14

\(B(x,\varepsilon)=\{y:\|x-y\|<\varepsilon\}\) is the ball of radius ε. \(U\subseteq\mathbb R^n\) is open if around every point of \(U\) some ball lies entirely in \(U\) (no boundary points belong to \(U\)). \((0,1)^2\) is open; \([0,1)^2\) and \([0,1]^2\) are not.

Satz · extrema in several variablesSkript 11.15

Let \(U\) be open and \(f:U\to\mathbb R\) twice continuously differentiable.

  • Necessary: local extremum at \(x\) ⇒ \(\nabla f(x)=0\). At a local min, \(Hf(x)\) is positive semidefinite (all eigenvalues \(\ge0\)); at a local max, negative semidefinite.
  • Sufficient: \(\nabla f(x)=0\) and \(Hf(x)\) positive definite (all eigenvalues \(>0\)) ⇒ strict local min. \(\nabla f(x)=0\) and negative definite (all \(<0\)) ⇒ strict local max.
Why this is true (the script omits the proof) Extra

Necessary condition. Fix a direction \(v\neq0\) and walk along the line through \(x\): \(g(t)=f(x+tv)\), a function of one variable, defined for small \(|t|\) because \(U\) is open. If \(f\) has a local extremum at \(x\), then \(g\) has one at \(t=0\), so \(g'(0)=0\) by Chapter 7 (Lemma 7.2). By the chain rule \(g'(0)=\nabla f(x)^tv\). This vanishes for every \(v\), and a vector perpendicular to everything is 0, so \(\nabla f(x)=0\). Differentiating once more, \(g''(0)=v^tHf(x)\,v\); at a local minimum \(g''(0)\ge0\) for every \(v\), which is exactly "positive semidefinite".

Sufficient condition. Taylor in several variables (Lemma 11.7 applied twice) gives, with \(\nabla f(x)=0\), \[f(x+h)=f(x)+\tfrac12h^tHf(x)h+o(\|h\|^2).\] Let \(\lambda_{\min}>0\) be the smallest eigenvalue of \(Hf(x)\). Writing \(h\) in the eigenvector basis as in the primer above, \(h^tHf(x)h\ge\lambda_{\min}\|h\|^2\). Hence \[f(x+h)-f(x)\ge\tfrac12\lambda_{\min}\|h\|^2+o(\|h\|^2)=\|h\|^2\left(\tfrac12\lambda_{\min}+\tfrac{o(\|h\|^2)}{\|h\|^2}\right).\] The bracket tends to \(\tfrac12\lambda_{\min}>0\) as \(h\to0\), so it is positive for all small \(h\neq0\): \(f(x+h)>f(x)\), a strict local minimum. For the maximum apply this to \(-f\).

Why "definite" and not just "semidefinite" is needed. With a zero eigenvalue the quadratic term vanishes in that direction and the \(o(\|h\|^2)\) decides — and it can go either way: \(f=x^4+y^2\) has a minimum at 0, \(f=-x^4+y^2\) a saddle, and both have \(Hf(0)=\operatorname{diag}(0,2)\).

ProcedureSkript §11.3 · Vorgehensweise
  1. Find all critical points: solve \(\nabla f(x)=0\) (a system of equations).
  2. Compute \(Hf\) at each critical point.
  3. Look at the eigenvalues \(\lambda_i\):
    • all \(>0\) ⇒ strict local min; all \(<0\) ⇒ strict local max;
    • positive and negative ⇒ neither: saddle point [Sattelpunkt];
    • some \(>0\) and some \(=0\) ⇒ no local max (but could be a min); some \(<0\) and some \(=0\) ⇒ no local min.
  4. Global extrema: check the behaviour as \(\|x\|\to\infty\) or at the boundary (e.g. \(f(x,y)\to\pm\infty\) along a line ⇒ no global max/min).

Extra For a symmetric \(2\times2\) matrix \(\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\b&c\end{pmatrix}\) you don't need to compute eigenvalues: \(\det=\lambda_1\lambda_2\), \(\operatorname{tr}=a+c=\lambda_1+\lambda_2\). \(\det>0,\ a>0\) ⇒ positive definite; \(\det>0,\ a<0\) ⇒ negative definite; \(\det<0\) ⇒ indefinite (saddle). For diagonal matrices the eigenvalues are the diagonal entries.

Try it · critical points, gradient and Hessian

Colour = height of \(f\) (dark = low, light = high), with contour lines. Critical points are marked. Click or tap anywhere to see the gradient arrow and the Hessian there.

ExamplesSkript 11.16, 11.17
  • \(f=x^2+2y^2\): \(\nabla f=(2x,4y)=0\iff(0,0)\); \(Hf=\operatorname{diag}(2,4)\), positive definite ⇒ strict local and global min.
  • \(f=x^2-2y^2\): same critical point; \(Hf=\operatorname{diag}(2,-4)\) indefinite ⇒ saddle point.
Exam questionsSoSe23 A3 · WiSe24 5b · Blatt 14.3c · WiSe22 A5
SoSe23 A3: all local and global extrema of \(f=x^3+y^4-12x+4y\)

\(\nabla f=(3x^2-12,\ 4y^3+4)=0\iff x=\pm2,\ y=-1\). Two critical points. \(Hf=\operatorname{diag}(6x,12y^2)\).

  • \((2,-1)\): \(Hf=\operatorname{diag}(12,12)\), both eigenvalues \(>0\) ⇒ strict local min.
  • \((-2,-1)\): \(\operatorname{diag}(-12,12)\) ⇒ saddle point.

No global extrema: for fixed \(y\), \(f(x,y)\to\pm\infty\) as \(x\to\pm\infty\) (the \(x^3\) term).

WiSe24 5b: local extrema of \(f=x_1^2+x_1+x_2+e^{-x_1-x_2}\)

\(\nabla f=(2x_1+1-e^{-x_1-x_2},\ 1-e^{-x_1-x_2})\). Second component 0 ⇒ \(x_1+x_2=0\) ⇒ \(e^{0}=1\), then the first gives \(2x_1=0\). Only critical point \((0,0)\).

\(Hf=\begin{pmatrix}2+e^{-x_1-x_2}&e^{-x_1-x_2}\\e^{-x_1-x_2}&e^{-x_1-x_2}\end{pmatrix}\), at \((0,0)\): \(\begin{pmatrix}3&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}\), \(\det=2>0\), top-left \(>0\) ⇒ positive definite ⇒ local min. (The official solution writes the top-left entry as 2, which is a slip; its determinant 1 leads to the same conclusion.)

Blatt 14.3c: extrema of \(f=y^2\,3^x\)

\(\nabla f=(\ln3\,y^23^x,\ 2y3^x)=0\iff y=0\) (since \(3^x>0\)): a whole line of critical points. Here the Hessian test is inconclusive, but directly: \(f\ge0\) everywhere and \(f(x,0)=0\), so every \((x,0)\) is a local and global minimum. Sometimes looking at the function beats the Hessian.

WiSe22 A5: sufficient condition; critical points of \(f=x\ln(x^2y^2)-y\); a Hessian from a given gradient

(a) Strict local max at \(x\): \(\nabla g(x)=0\) and \(Hg(x)\) negative definite. (The printed solution says "positive definite", which would be a minimum. Watch out for this in your own answers.)

(b) Rewrite \(f=2x(\ln x+\ln y)-y\). \(\nabla f=(2\ln(xy)+2,\ \frac{2x}y-1)=0\iff xy=e^{-1},\ y=2x\iff x=\frac1{\sqrt{2e}},\ y=\frac2{\sqrt{2e}}=\sqrt{\tfrac2e}\) (only the positive root, since \(x,y>0\)). Exactly one critical point.

(c) \(\nabla g(x,y)=\left(\frac{3y}x-1,\ \ln(x^3y^3)+3\right)^t\); find \(Hg\). You never need \(g\) itself: row \(i\) of the Hessian is the gradient of the \(i\)-th component of \(\nabla g\). Rewrite \(\ln(x^3y^3)=3\ln x+3\ln y\) (valid for \(x,y>0\)). Then \[Hg(x,y)=\begin{pmatrix}\partial_x(\frac{3y}x-1)&\partial_y(\frac{3y}x-1)\\\partial_x(3\ln x+3\ln y+3)&\partial_y(3\ln x+3\ln y+3)\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-\frac{3y}{x^2}&\frac3x\\\frac3x&\frac3y\end{pmatrix}.\] The two off-diagonal entries agree, as Schwarz predicts — a free check on your computation.

Vector-valued functions: the Jacobian matrix

DefinitionSkript 11.18, 11.19

\(f:U\subseteq\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R^m\) is (totally) differentiable at \(x\) if \(f(x+h)=f(x)+Ah+o(\|h\|)\) for a linear map / \(m\times n\) matrix \(A\). Then \(a_{ij}=\frac{\partial f_i}{\partial x_j}(x)\): row \(i\) is the gradient (as a row) of component \(f_i\). \(A=Df(x)=J_f(x)\) is the Jacobian matrix [Jacobi-Matrix]. \(f\) is differentiable iff all components are.

Example: \(f(x,y)=(x^2y,\ \sin x+y)\): \(J_f=\begin{pmatrix}2xy&x^2\\\cos x&1\end{pmatrix}\).

Special cases you already know: for \(m=1\) the Jacobian is the row \(Df=\nabla f^t\); for \(n=1\) (a curve) it is the column \(\gamma'(t)\); and the Hessian of \(f\) is the Jacobian of \(\nabla f\).

Why \(a_{ij}=\partial f_i/\partial x_j\) (Lemma 11.19, the script's proof spelled out) Skript 11.19

Put \(h=te_j\) (move only in coordinate \(j\), by \(t\)). Then \(Ah=t\cdot(\text{column }j\text{ of }A)\), and \(\|h\|=|t|\). Row \(i\) of \(f(x+h)=f(x)+Ah+o(\|h\|)\) reads \[f_i(x+te_j)=f_i(x)+t\,a_{ij}+o(|t|).\] Divide by \(t\) and let \(t\to0\): \(\frac{f_i(x+te_j)-f_i(x)}t\to a_{ij}\). The left side is by definition \(\partial_jf_i(x)\). So the matrix \(A\) is forced: if a linear approximation exists at all, it must be the matrix of partial derivatives. The same argument, read row by row, shows that \(f\) is differentiable iff each component \(f_i\) is.

What you must know for 11.3: higher partial derivatives and in particular the Hessian; \(\partial_i\partial_jf=\partial_j\partial_if\) when continuous; necessary and sufficient conditions for local extrema; total differentiability; the Jacobian matrix.

!What the exam asks from Chapter 11

Chapter 11 appears in five of the nine exams provided (WiSe19, WiSe22, SoSe23, WiSe23, WiSe24; the others end before this chapter), usually as a full task of 4–11 points:

TypeExam tasksWhat earns the points
Compute partial derivatives / gradientWiSe19 1g, WiSe24 5a, Blatt 14.3a/bfreeze the other variables; product and chain rule; variable exponents via \(e^{\ln}\)
Critical pointsSoSe23 A3, WiSe22 A5b, WiSe24 5bsolve \(\nabla f=0\) completely; respect the domain (e.g. \(x,y>0\))
Classify with the HessianSoSe23 A3, WiSe24 5b, Blatt 14.3c\(Hf\) at each point, eigenvalues or det test, the right conclusion (min/max/saddle/inconclusive)
Global extremaSoSe23 A3, Blatt 14.3ca line along which \(f\to\pm\infty\), or a direct bound like \(f\ge0\)
State the sufficient conditionWiSe22 A5a\(\nabla g=0\) and \(Hg\) negative definite (max) / positive definite (min)
Hessian from a given gradientWiSe22 A5cdifferentiate each gradient component again; check symmetry
Schwarz argumentsWiSe23 A5quote Schwarz and its hypothesis; "rot \(\neq0\) ⇒ not a gradient"
Slips in the official solutions

WiSe22 A5a says "positive definite" for a strict local maximum (it must be negative definite). WiSe24 5b writes the top-left Hessian entry as 2 instead of 3. Neither changes what you should write, but do not copy them.

Drill: partial derivatives, critical points, classification

What you must be able to do after Chapter 11
  • For the exam: the seven task types above; the procedure of §11.3 written out with every step justified; the \(2\times2\) determinant test and the eigenvalue criterion; the statement of Schwarz with its hypothesis.
  • Common point losses: forgetting critical points (solve the system fully, e.g. both \(x=2\) and \(x=-2\)); calling a point with a zero eigenvalue a minimum; claiming a global extremum without checking behaviour at infinity.
  • For Chapter 12: the condition "\(\partial f/\partial y\) continuous" in Picard–Lindelöf is a partial derivative, and a curve \(\gamma(t)\) with \(\gamma'(t)=F(\gamma(t))\) is exactly what a system of ODEs describes.

ΣSummary sheet

  1. Partial derivative = ordinary derivative in one variable, others frozen. \(\nabla f\) = column of partials.
  2. \(f(x+h)=f(x)+\nabla f(x)^th+o(\|h\|)\); tangent plane.
  3. Hessian \(Hf=(\partial_i\partial_jf)\), symmetric (Schwarz).
  4. Extrema: \(\nabla f=0\) (critical points) → eigenvalues of \(Hf\): all \(>0\) min, all \(<0\) max, mixed saddle, zero eigenvalues ⇒ look closer.
  5. Global: check behaviour at infinity / along lines.
  6. Jacobian: row \(i\) = gradient of component \(i\).

Analysis für Informatik · MA0902 · Chapter 12

Differential EquationsDifferentialgleichungen

An equation where the unknown is a function \(y\), and the equation links \(y\) with its derivatives. The course teaches four solution recipes: integrate directly, separation of variables, variation of constants for linear 1st order equations, and the characteristic polynomial + ansatz for linear 2nd order equations with constant coefficients. Each recipe has appeared in the exams, usually as a task worth 3–8 points.

Source labels: Skript 12.5 = stated in your lecture script · Blatt 14 · 14.1 = exercise sheet · Klausur WiSe19 = old exam · Extra = my own addition (intuition, a proof the script leaves out, or an extra example). A § marks a whole section of the script (§12.4 = Section 12.4) rather than a numbered result (12.4 = Beispiel 12.4). "Aufgabe 13.x" refers to the solved problems in Chapter 13 of the script.

0The big picture

With an ordinary equation like \(x^2=4\) you look for a number. With a differential equation like \(y'=y\) you look for a function: which function equals its own derivative? (Answer: \(y=ce^x\).) Differential equations describe change: "the population grows proportionally to its size", "the spring pulls back proportionally to the stretch".

Before computing anything, recognise the type. The type tells you which recipe to use:

the equation looks like …namerecipesection
\(y'=g(x)\)pure integration\(y=y_0+\int_{x_0}^xg\)12.1
\(y'=f(x)\,g(y)\)separable [getrennte Variablen]\(\int\frac{dy}{g(y)}=\int f(x)\,dx\), solve for \(y\)12.2
\(y'+a(x)y=f(x)\)linear, 1st orderhomogeneous \(ce^{-A}\), then variation of constants12.3
\(y''+ay'+by=f(x)\), \(a,b\) constantslinear, 2nd order, constant coefficients\(\lambda^2+a\lambda+b=0\), then ansatz for \(f\)12.4
\(y'=f(x,y)\) in general—no recipe; Picard–Lindelöf says a unique solution exists12.5

And one habit for every task: check your answer ("Probe"). Differentiate your \(y\), plug it in, check the initial value. The script does this after every example, and in an exam it catches almost every sign error.

Before you start
  • Integration is the engine of this chapter: antiderivatives and the fundamental theorem (§8.3), substitution and integration by parts (§8.4). Separation of variables is a substitution; variation of constants ends with an integral.
  • Derivative rules, especially the chain rule (§6.3); "\(f'=0\) on an interval ⇒ \(f\) constant" (§7.3, Satz 7.13) — every uniqueness proof below uses it.
  • \(\exp\), \(\ln\), \(\arctan\), \(\tan\) and their domains (§5.2, §5.3): solving \(G(y)=F(x)\) for \(y\) means applying an inverse function, and its domain gives the maximal domain of the solution.
  • Complex numbers and Euler's formula \(e^{i\omega x}=\cos\omega x+i\sin\omega x\) (Chapter 1, §5.3) for the oscillating case of 12.4; a \(2\times2\) determinant for the Wronskian.

12.1Initial value problems [Anfangswertprobleme]

Motivation Skript 12.1. A population of size \(N(t)\) with birth rate \(\beta\) and death rate \(\delta\) changes by \(N'(t)=(\beta-\delta)N(t)\). All solutions are \(N(t)=ce^{(\beta-\delta)t}\) (check: \(N'=c(\beta-\delta)e^{(\beta-\delta)t}=(\beta-\delta)N\)). There is one solution for every \(c\). Fixing the starting size \(N(0)=N_0\) picks one: \(c=N_0\).

DefinitionSkript 12.1

An equation \(y'(x)=f(x,y(x))\) for an unknown function \(y\) of one real variable \(x\) is an ordinary differential equation [gewöhnliche Differentialgleichung] of 1st order [1. Ordnung] (only \(y'\) occurs). "Ordinary" means there is only one variable \(x\), so there are no partial derivatives. The order is the highest derivative that occurs.

An initial value problem (IVP) additionally prescribes \(y(x_0)=y_0\): for an interval \(I\ni x_0\) and a set \(\Omega_0\ni y_0\) of allowed values, find \(y:I\to\Omega_0\) with \[y'(x)=f(x,y(x))\ \ \forall x\in I,\qquad y(x_0)=y_0.\]

Why "initial value"? Think of \(x\) as time: the equation says how the state changes, the initial value says where you start. Without it you get a whole family of solutions, and with it (usually) exactly one.

ExamplesSkript 12.2 – 12.4
  • \(y'=g(x)\), \(y(x_0)=y_0\): \(y\) is an antiderivative of \(g\). By the fundamental theorem (Chapter 8), \(y(x)-y_0=\int_{x_0}^xg(t)\,dt\), so \(y(x)=y_0+\int_{x_0}^xg(t)\,dt\), and this solution is unique. E.g. \(y'=a\), \(y(0)=y_0\) gives \(y=y_0+ax\).
  • \(y'=y\), \(y(0)=1\) (characterisation of \(\exp\)): since \(y(0)=1>0\) and \(y\) is continuous, \(y>0\) near 0, so we may divide: \(\frac{y'}{y}=1\). Integrate from 0 to \(x\): \([\ln y(t)]_0^x=x\), i.e. \(\ln y(x)=x\) and \(y(x)=e^x\). With \(y(x_0)=y_0>0\) the same computation gives \(y=y_0e^{x-x_0}\).
Uniqueness trickBlatt 10 · 10.4Klausur SoSe19 A4

If \(f'=cf\) on an interval \(I\), then \(f(x)=be^{cx}\) for some \(b\in\mathbb R\). The computation above divided by \(y\), which only works where \(y\neq0\). This proof avoids that problem:

Proof (Blatt 10.4)

Put \(g(x)=f(x)e^{-cx}\). By the product rule, \(g'(x)=f'(x)e^{-cx}-cf(x)e^{-cx}=(cf(x)-cf(x))e^{-cx}=0\). A function with derivative 0 on an interval is constant (mean value theorem, Blatt 10.4a): \(g\equiv b\). Hence \(f(x)=be^{cx}\).

SoSe19 A4 uses the same trick. From \(f(x+y)=f(x)f(y)\), \(f(0)=1\), \(f'(0)=1\), part a) shows \(f'=f\) via the difference quotient \(\frac{f(x)f(h)-f(x)}h=f(x)\frac{f(h)-f(0)}h\to f(x)\). Then b) \(g=fe^{-x}\) has \(g'=0\), c) \(g(0)=1\), so d) \(f=e^x\).

Try it · slope field and solution curves

Each short line has slope \(f(x,y)\): a solution curve must be tangent to the lines everywhere. Click or tap to choose a new initial point \((x_0,y_0)\). The curve is then computed numerically (Runge–Kutta) forwards and backwards. Watch for curves that shoot off to infinity (blow-up) or hit the edge of the allowed region (collapse). Both are explained in 12.5.

What you must know for 12.1: what a differential equation and an initial value problem are; solving \(y'=g(x)\).

12.2Separation of variables [Trennung der Variablen]

The right-hand side splits into a factor depending only on \(x\) times a factor depending only on \(y\). Then you can put all \(y\)-terms on one side and all \(x\)-terms on the other, and integrate each side.

DerivationSkript §12.2, (12.1)

IVP: \(y'(x)=f(x)\,g(y(x))\), \(y(x_0)=y_0\), with \(f,g\) continuous and \(g(y_0)\neq0\).

  1. \(g\) and \(y\) are continuous and \(g(y_0)\ne0\), so \(g(y(x))\neq0\) for \(x\) near \(x_0\). Only there may we divide: \(\frac{y'(x)}{g(y(x))}=f(x)\).
  2. Integrate from \(x_0\) to \(x\): \(\int_{x_0}^x\frac{y'(t)}{g(y(t))}dt=\int_{x_0}^xf(t)\,dt\).
  3. Substitute \(s=y(t)\), \(ds=y'(t)\,dt\) on the left (Chapter 8): \(\displaystyle\int_{y_0}^{y(x)}\frac{ds}{g(s)}=\int_{x_0}^xf(t)\,dt.\)
  4. With \(G(y)=\int_{y_0}^y\frac{ds}{g(s)}\), \(F(x)=\int_{x_0}^xf\): \(G(y(x))=F(x)\), so \(y(x)=G^{-1}(F(x))\) if \(G\) is invertible.
Method · the short form used in solutionsSkript §12.2

Write \(y'=\frac{dy}{dx}\) and "multiply" by \(dx\) (a shorthand for steps 1–3): \[\frac{dy}{dx}=f(x)g(y)\ \Longrightarrow\ \frac{dy}{g(y)}=f(x)\,dx\ \Longrightarrow\ \int\frac{dy}{g(y)}=\int f(x)\,dx+c.\] Then: (1) solve for \(y\); (2) determine \(c\) from \(y(x_0)=y_0\); (3) state where the solution is defined (the exams often ask for the maximal domain [maximaler Definitionsbereich]); (4) check.

Extra If \(g(y_0)=0\), the constant function \(y\equiv y_0\) is a solution (both sides are 0), so there is nothing to separate. Example: \(y'=y^2\), \(y(0)=0\) has the solution \(y\equiv0\).

Example · a solution that only lives on an intervalSkript 12.5

\(y'=1+y^2\), \(y(0)=0\). Since \(1+y^2>0\), divide: \(\frac{y'}{1+y^2}=1\). Integrate and substitute: \(x=\int_0^{y(x)}\frac{ds}{1+s^2}=\arctan y(x)\). So \(y(x)=\tan x\), but only on \(\left(-\frac\pi2,\frac\pi2\right)\), because \(\arctan\) takes values only there. Check: \(y'=1+\tan^2x=1+y^2\). The right-hand side \(1+y^2\) is harmless, yet the solution reaches \(\pm\infty\) at \(x=\pm\frac\pi2\).

From the exercise sheetBlatt 14 · 14.1 (a), (b)

(a) \(y'=e^{2x-y}\), \(y(0)=1\). The key observation is \(e^{2x-y}=e^{2x}e^{-y}\), so the equation is separable: \[\int e^y\,dy=\int e^{2x}dx\ \Rightarrow\ e^y=\tfrac12e^{2x}+c\ \Rightarrow\ y=\ln\!\left(\tfrac12e^{2x}+c\right).\] \(1=\ln(\tfrac12+c)\) gives \(c=e-\tfrac12\), so \(y(x)=\ln\!\left(\tfrac12e^{2x}+e-\tfrac12\right)\). The argument is \(>0\) because \(e-\frac12>0\), so \(y\) is defined on all of \(\mathbb R\).

(b) \(y'=(x+y)^2\) is not separable. Substitute \(z=x+y\): \(z'=1+y'=1+z^2\), which is separable (it is Skript 12.5 again). \(\arctan z-\arctan z_0=x-x_0\), so \(z=\tan(\arctan z_0+x-x_0)\) and \[y(x)=\tan\big(\arctan(x_0+y_0)+x-x_0\big)-x.\] With \(y(0)=0\): \(y=\tan x-x\) on \(\left(-\frac\pi2,\frac\pi2\right)\).

Exam questionsWiSe24 4b · SoSe23 A7

WiSe24 4b (3 P): \(y'=\frac{2x}{3y^2}\), \(y(0)=1\). Separate: \(\int_1^y3s^2\,ds=\int_0^x2t\,dt\), so \(y^3-1=x^2\) and \(y(x)=\sqrt[3]{x^2+1}\). Check: \(y'=\frac{2x}{3(x^2+1)^{2/3}}=\frac{2x}{3y^2}\) ✓.

SoSe23 A7 (5 P): \(y'=\frac{4xe^{-y}}{(1+x^2)^3}\), \(y(0)=2\), maximal domain?

Solution
  1. Separable: \(\int e^y\,dy=\int\frac{4x}{(1+x^2)^3}dx\).
  2. Right side: substitute \(u=1+x^2\), \(du=2x\,dx\): \(\int\frac{2}{u^3}du=-\frac1{u^2}=-\frac1{(1+x^2)^2}\). So \(e^y=-\frac1{(1+x^2)^2}+c\).
  3. Initial value: \(e^2=-1+c\), so \(c=e^2+1\) and \(y(x)=\ln\!\left(e^2+1-\frac1{(1+x^2)^2}\right)\).
  4. Domain: \(\frac1{(1+x^2)^2}\le1\), so the argument is \(\ge e^2>0\) for every \(x\). The maximal domain is \(\mathbb R\).
Method · the maximal domainExtra

A solution of an IVP is a differentiable function on an interval containing \(x_0\). The maximal domain is the largest such interval on which your formula makes sense and satisfies the equation. Find it by listing where the formula breaks — a zero denominator, \(\ln\) of something \(\le0\), a root of something negative, \(\tan\) at \(\pm\frac\pi2\), or leaving the allowed region of \(y\) — and taking the piece around \(x_0\).

Example: \(y'=2xy^2\), \(y(0)=1\). Separate: \(-\frac1y=x^2+c\), \(c=-1\), so \(y=\frac1{1-x^2}\). The formula is defined on \(\mathbb R\setminus\{\pm1\}\), but the solution is only the piece through \(x_0=0\): maximal domain \((-1,1)\). The branches on \((1,\infty)\) and \((-\infty,-1)\) are separate solutions of the same equation with different initial values; they are not part of this solution, because a solution cannot jump across a pole.

Notice that the maximal domain depends on the initial value: with \(y(0)=y_0>0\) you get \(y=\frac1{1/y_0-x^2}\) on \(|x|<1/\sqrt{y_0}\), and with \(y_0\le0\) on all of \(\mathbb R\). Try it below.

Try it · how the maximal domain depends on y(0)

Choose an equation and move the initial value \(y_0=y(0)\). The solution is drawn only on its maximal domain (green band on the axis); a dashed line marks where it ends.

What you must know for 12.2: recognise when separation of variables applies, and use it to solve the equation.

12.3Linear equations of 1st order [Lineare DGL 1. Ordnung]

DefinitionSkript (12.2), (12.3)

With \(a,f:\mathbb R\to\mathbb R\) continuous, \[y'(x)+a(x)y(x)=f(x)\] is an inhomogeneous linear equation of 1st order. If \(f\equiv0\), it is homogeneous [homogen]. "Linear" means that \(y\) and \(y'\) appear only to the first power and are never multiplied together: no \(y^2\), no \(e^y\), no \(yy'\).

Lemma 12.6 · structure of the solution setSkript 12.6

Let \(H\) = solutions of the homogeneous equation and \(I\) = solutions of the inhomogeneous one. (a) \(y_i\in I,\ y_h\in H\Rightarrow y_i+y_h\in I\). (b) \(y_{i1},y_{i2}\in I\Rightarrow y_{i1}-y_{i2}\in H\). (c) For any single \(y_i\in I\): \(I=y_i+H\).

In words: general solution = one particular solution + all homogeneous solutions. This is the same structure as for linear systems of equations \(Ax=b\) in linear algebra.

Proof

(a) \((y_i+y_h)'+a(y_i+y_h)=(y_i'+ay_i)+(y_h'+ay_h)=f+0=f\). (b) \((y_{i1}-y_{i2})'+a(y_{i1}-y_{i2})=f-f=0\). (c) "⊇" is (a). "⊆": for \(y\in I\), \(y-y_i\in H\) by (b), and \(y=y_i+(y-y_i)\).

Step 1 · homogeneous equationSkript 12.7, 12.8

All solutions of \(y'+a(x)y=0\) are \(y(x)=ce^{-A(x)}\), \(c\in\mathbb R\), where \(A\) is an antiderivative of \(a\) (\(A'=a\)).

Proof

They are solutions: \((ce^{-A})'=-A'ce^{-A}=-a\,ce^{-A}\). They are all solutions (the same trick as in Blatt 10.4): \(\big(y\,e^{A}\big)'=(y'+ay)e^{A}=0\), so \(ye^A=c\) is constant.

Example 12.8: \(y'=y\) means \(a=-1\), \(A=-x\), so \(y=ce^x\).

Step 2 · variation of constantsSkript 12.9, 12.10

Replace the constant \(c\) by a function: ansatz \(y(x)=c(x)e^{-A(x)}\). Insert it: \[c'e^{-A}\underbrace{-c\,a\,e^{-A}+a\,c\,e^{-A}}_{=0}=f\ \Longrightarrow\ c'(x)=f(x)e^{A(x)}\ \Longrightarrow\ c(x)=\int_{x_0}^xf(t)e^{A(t)}dt.\] The two middle terms cancel because \(A'=a\). This cancellation happens every time, so it is a good check on your computation.

Satz 12.10: all solutions are \(\displaystyle y(x)=e^{-A(x)}\Big(c+\int_{x_0}^xf(t)e^{A(t)}dt\Big)\), \(c\in\mathbb R\). If \(A(x_0)=0\), the IVP solution has \(c=y_0\).

ExampleSkript 12.11

\(y'+y=\sin x\), \(y(0)=1\). Here \(a=1\), \(A=x\), \(A(0)=0\), so \(y=e^{-x}\big(1+\int_0^xe^t\sin t\,dt\big)\). Integrating by parts twice (the "loop" trick from Chapter 8) gives \(I(x)=\int_0^xe^t\sin t\,dt=\tfrac12e^x(\sin x-\cos x)+\tfrac12\). Hence \[y(x)=\tfrac32e^{-x}+\tfrac12(\sin x-\cos x).\] Check: \(y'=-\frac32e^{-x}+\frac12(\cos x+\sin x)\), so \(y'+y=\sin x\) ✓, and \(y(0)=\frac32-\frac12=1\) ✓. For large \(x\) the \(e^{-x}\) part dies out and \(y\) follows the oscillation. Choose this equation in the slope field above to see it.

Exam questionWiSe19 A5b

\(y'+y=\frac1{1+e^x}\). The homogeneous solutions \(ce^{-x}\) are given. Find all solutions by variation of constants.

Solution

Ansatz \(y=c(x)e^{-x}\): \(c'e^{-x}-ce^{-x}+ce^{-x}=\frac1{1+e^x}\), so \(c'(x)=\frac{e^x}{1+e^x}\). This is of the form \(\frac{g'}{g}\) with \(g=1+e^x\), so \(c(x)=\ln(1+e^x)+c_1\). All solutions: \[y(x)=e^{-x}\big(\ln(1+e^x)+c_1\big),\quad c_1\in\mathbb R.\]

What you must know for 12.3: recognise homogeneous and inhomogeneous linear equations of 1st order; get the homogeneous solutions by separation of variables; find a particular solution by variation of constants; general solution = particular + homogeneous.

12.4Linear equations of 2nd order [Lineare DGL 2. Ordnung]

\(y''+a(x)y'+b(x)y=f(x)\). The structure from 12.3 carries over unchanged: all solutions = \(y_i+H\). There is no general recipe, but for constant coefficients \(a,b\in\mathbb R\) there is one, and that is the case the exams ask about.

How many homogeneous solutions? Linear independence

DefinitionsSkript 12.12, 12.13

\(y_1,y_2:I\to\mathbb R\) are linearly independent [linear unabhängig] if \(\alpha_1y_1(x)+\alpha_2y_2(x)=0\) for all \(x\in I\) forces \(\alpha_1=\alpha_2=0\). In other words, neither function is a constant multiple of the other. The Wronskian [Wronski-Determinante] is \[W(x)=\det\begin{pmatrix}y_1(x)&y_2(x)\\y_1'(x)&y_2'(x)\end{pmatrix}.\]

Where it comes from: if \(\alpha_1y_1+\alpha_2y_2\equiv0\), differentiating gives \(\alpha_1y_1'+\alpha_2y_2'\equiv0\). Together this is a \(2\times2\) linear system for \((\alpha_1,\alpha_2)\). If \(W(x)\neq0\) at some point, its only solution is \(\alpha_1=\alpha_2=0\).

Satz 12.14, 12.15Skript 12.14, 12.15

The script marks Definition 12.13 and Satz 12.14 as ausgelassen (skipped in the lecture). You need the idea "two independent solutions give all solutions", and the Wronskian is the tool that checks independence.

(a) Linearly dependent ⇒ \(W\equiv0\). (b) For two solutions of the homogeneous equation: independent ⇔ \(W(x)\ne0\) for some \(x\).

All solutions of the homogeneous equation are \(y=c_1y_1+c_2y_2\) with two linearly independent solutions \(y_1,y_2\), called fundamental solutions [Fundamentallösungen / Fundamentalsystem]. The solution space is 2-dimensional: one free constant per order.

Example: \(e^{2x}\) and \(e^{3x}\) give \(W=\det\begin{pmatrix}e^{2x}&e^{3x}\\2e^{2x}&3e^{3x}\end{pmatrix}=e^{5x}\neq0\), so they are independent.

Proof of Satz 12.14 (the script's short argument, with the missing step for (b) "⇒" filled in) Extra

Recall from linear algebra that the \(2\times2\) system \(M\alpha=0\) has only the solution \(\alpha=0\) exactly when \(\det M\neq0\). Apply this to \(M=\begin{pmatrix}y_1(x)&y_2(x)\\y_1'(x)&y_2'(x)\end{pmatrix}\), whose determinant is \(W(x)\).

(a) If \(y_1,y_2\) are linearly dependent, there is \((\alpha_1,\alpha_2)\neq(0,0)\) with \(\alpha_1y_1+\alpha_2y_2\equiv0\); differentiating, \(\alpha_1y_1'+\alpha_2y_2'\equiv0\). So for every \(x\) the system \(M\alpha=0\) has a non-zero solution, which forces \(W(x)=0\) for all \(x\).

(b) "⇐" If \(W(x_1)\ne0\) at some \(x_1\), then \(M(x_1)\alpha=0\) forces \(\alpha=0\). So any relation \(\alpha_1y_1+\alpha_2y_2\equiv0\) has \(\alpha_1=\alpha_2=0\): independent. (This direction works for any differentiable functions.)

(b) "⇒", shown in the contrapositive form "\(W(x_1)=0\) at some \(x_1\) ⇒ dependent". Here we need that \(y_1,y_2\) solve the same equation. Since \(\det M(x_1)=0\), there is \(\alpha\ne(0,0)\) with \(M(x_1)\alpha=0\). The function \(y=\alpha_1y_1+\alpha_2y_2\) solves the homogeneous equation (a sum of solutions) and has \(y(x_1)=0\), \(y'(x_1)=0\). The zero function solves the same problem. A second-order linear equation has only one solution with given \(y(x_1)\), \(y'(x_1)\) (for constant coefficients this follows from the proof of Satz 12.15 below; in general from Picard–Lindelöf applied to the pair \((y,y')\)). So \(y\equiv0\), a non-trivial relation: dependent. ∎

Why the script's short argument is not enough on its own: for arbitrary functions, "\(W\ne0\) somewhere" is sufficient but not necessary for independence. \(y_1=x^3\), \(y_2=|x|^3\) are independent on \(\mathbb R\), yet \(W\equiv0\). They are not solutions of a common equation of the form (12.4), which is exactly the hypothesis (b) needs.

Consequence worth remembering: for solutions of the same equation, \(W\) is either identically 0 or nowhere 0 — so a single test point decides.

Homogeneous case: the characteristic polynomial

Method · \(y''+ay'+by=0\)Skript §12.4, 12.15

Idea: \(y\), \(y'\), \(y''\) must cancel, so they should be "the same kind of function" up to constant factors. Exponentials have this property. Ansatz \(y=e^{\lambda x}\) gives \(e^{\lambda x}(\lambda^2+a\lambda+b)=0\), and since \(e^{\lambda x}\ne0\): \[\lambda^2+a\lambda+b=0,\qquad \lambda_{1,2}=-\frac a2\pm\sqrt D,\quad D=\frac{a^2}4-b.\]

caserootsgeneral solutionbehaviour
\(D>0\)\(\lambda_1\ne\lambda_2\) real\(c_1e^{\lambda_1x}+c_2e^{\lambda_2x}\)no oscillation
\(D=0\)\(\lambda=-\frac a2\) (double)\(c_1e^{-\frac a2x}+c_2\,x\,e^{-\frac a2x}\)critical case
\(D<0\)\(-\frac a2\pm i\omega\), \(\omega=\sqrt{-D}\)\(e^{-\frac a2x}\big(c_1\cos\omega x+c_2\sin\omega x\big)\)oscillation; damped if \(a>0\)
Why the extra \(x\) when \(D=0\), and why cos/sin when \(D<0\)

\(D=0\): the ansatz only produces one function \(e^{-\frac a2x}\), but we need two. The script checks directly that \(y=xe^{-\frac a2x}\) also works: \(y'=(1-\frac a2x)e^{-\frac a2x}\), \(y''=(\frac{a^2}4x-a)e^{-\frac a2x}\), and inserting gives \((b-\frac{a^2}4)xe^{-\frac a2x}=0\) because \(D=0\).

\(D<0\): the roots are complex, \(e^{\lambda_1x}=e^{-\frac a2x}(\cos\omega x+i\sin\omega x)\) (Euler, Chapter 5). The real and imaginary parts of a complex solution are real solutions (the equation has real coefficients), which gives \(e^{-\frac a2x}\cos\omega x\) and \(e^{-\frac a2x}\sin\omega x\).

Why these are all solutions (Satz 12.15; the script gives no proof) Extra

Factor \(\lambda^2+a\lambda+b=(\lambda-\lambda_1)(\lambda-\lambda_2)\), so \(a=-(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)\), \(b=\lambda_1\lambda_2\). Let \(y\) be any solution and put \(z=y'-\lambda_1y\). Then \[z'-\lambda_2z=y''-(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)y'+\lambda_1\lambda_2y=y''+ay'+by=0.\] So \(z\) solves a first-order homogeneous equation, and by Lemma 12.7 \(z=c\,e^{\lambda_2x}\). Now \(y'-\lambda_1y=c\,e^{\lambda_2x}\) is a first-order linear equation, and Satz 12.10 (\(A=-\lambda_1x\)) gives all its solutions: \[y=e^{\lambda_1x}\Big(c_1+c\int_0^xe^{(\lambda_2-\lambda_1)t}\,dt\Big).\] If \(\lambda_1\ne\lambda_2\), the integral is \(\frac{e^{(\lambda_2-\lambda_1)x}-1}{\lambda_2-\lambda_1}\), and \(y\) is a combination of \(e^{\lambda_1x}\) and \(e^{\lambda_2x}\). If \(\lambda_1=\lambda_2\), the integrand is 1, the integral is \(x\), and \(y=(c_1+cx)e^{\lambda_1x}\) — this is where the extra factor \(x\) comes from. (For complex roots the same computation works with complex numbers; taking real parts gives the cos/sin form.)

The proof also shows uniqueness for the IVP: \(y(x_0)\) and \(y'(x_0)\) fix \(z(x_0)\) and hence both constants. So there are exactly two free constants — the solution space is 2-dimensional.

Try it · the three cases of y″ + a y′ + b y = 0
Example · fitting the two constants to initial valuesExtra

\(y''-2y'-3y=0\) (WiSe19 A5a) with \(y(0)=2\), \(y'(0)=2\). General solution \(y=c_1e^{3x}+c_2e^{-x}\), so \(y'=3c_1e^{3x}-c_2e^{-x}\). At \(x=0\): \(c_1+c_2=2\) and \(3c_1-c_2=2\). Adding gives \(4c_1=4\), so \(c_1=1\), \(c_2=1\): \(y=e^{3x}+e^{-x}\). A second-order IVP needs two initial conditions, one per constant. For an inhomogeneous equation, fit the constants after adding the particular solution.

Inhomogeneous case: guess a particular solution

Method 1 · ansatz of the same type as \(f\)Skript §12.4, 12.16
right-hand side \(f(x)\)ansatz \(y_i(x)\)
polynomial of degree \(n\)general polynomial of degree \(n\): \(\sum_{k=0}^nb_kx^k\)
\(e^{\alpha x}(a_1\cos\omega x+a_2\sin\omega x)\)\(e^{\alpha x}(b_1\cos\omega x+b_2\sin\omega x)\), with both cos and sin even if \(f\) has only one
\(e^{\alpha x}\) (special case \(\omega=0\))\(b\,e^{\alpha x}\)

Insert, compare coefficients [Koeffizientenvergleich] of \(x^k\) (or of cos and sin), solve the linear system. Then general solution = \(y_i\) + homogeneous.

Why comparing coefficients is legitimate: if two polynomials agree for all \(x\), their coefficients agree, because \(a_k=\frac{p^{(k)}(0)}{k!}\) is determined by the function (Lemma 7.22). The same holds for \(b_1\cos\omega x+b_2\sin\omega x\), since \(\cos\) and \(\sin\) are linearly independent — set \(x=0\) to read off \(b_1\), then \(x=\frac\pi{2\omega}\) for \(b_2\).

Extra Resonance: the ansatz fails when it is already a homogeneous solution. In Skript 12.16 this is the case \(|\omega_0|=|\omega|\): the coefficient equations become \(0=1\). The fix (not needed in this course's exams) is to multiply the ansatz by \(x\). Method 2, variation of constants with \(c_1(x)y_1+c_2(x)y_2\), is mentioned in the script but not worked out.

ExamplesSkript 12.16 · Aufgaben 13.1, 13.2
  • 12.16: \(y''+\omega^2y=\sin(\omega_0x)\), \(|\omega_0|\ne|\omega|\). Ansatz \(b_1\cos\omega_0x+b_2\sin\omega_0x\) has \(y''=-\omega_0^2y\), so \((\omega^2-\omega_0^2)(b_1\cos+b_2\sin)=\sin\) gives \(b_1=0\), \(b_2=\frac1{\omega^2-\omega_0^2}\). Homogeneous: \(\lambda=\pm i\omega\). All solutions: \(\frac{\sin\omega_0x}{\omega^2-\omega_0^2}+c_1\cos\omega x+c_2\sin\omega x\).
  • 13.1: \(y''-3y'+2y=x^2+1\). \(\lambda^2-3\lambda+2=(\lambda-1)(\lambda-2)\). Ansatz \(ax^2+bx+c\): \(2a-3(2ax+b)+2(ax^2+bx+c)=x^2+1\) gives \(2a=1\), \(-6a+2b=0\), \(2a-3b+2c=1\), so \(a=\frac12,b=\frac32,c=\frac94\). All solutions: \(\frac12x^2+\frac32x+\frac94+c_1e^x+c_2e^{2x}\).
  • 13.2: \(y''-6y'+8y=e^{3x}\). \(\lambda=2,4\). Ansatz \(be^{3x}\): \((9-18+8)b=1\), so \(b=-1\). All solutions: \(-e^{3x}+c_1e^{2x}+c_2e^{4x}\).
Try it · the full recipe for y″ + a y′ + b y = f(x), step by step

Pick a task or type your own coefficients. The widget runs the three steps of Aufgabe 13.1 — homogeneous solution, ansatz and comparison of coefficients, general solution — and checks the particular solution by inserting it. Use it to check your own homework, not to replace it.

From the exercise sheetBlatt 14 · 14.1 (c)

\(y''+9y=5\cos2x\). Homogeneous: \(\lambda^2+9=0\), \(\lambda=\pm3i\), fundamental system \(\cos3x,\sin3x\). Ansatz \(b_1\cos2x+b_2\sin2x\): \(y_i''=-4y_i\), so \(5b_1\cos2x+5b_2\sin2x=5\cos2x\) and \(b_1=1,b_2=0\). \[y(x)=\cos2x+c_1\cos3x+c_2\sin3x.\]

Exam questionsWiSe19 A5a · WiSe22 A6

WiSe19 A5a (4 P): \(y''-2y'-3y=0\). \(\lambda^2-2\lambda-3=(\lambda-3)(\lambda+1)\), so \(y=c_1e^{3x}+c_2e^{-x}\).

WiSe22 A6a: \(y''-7y'+12y=0\). \((\lambda-3)(\lambda-4)=0\), so \(y=c_1e^{3x}+c_2e^{4x}\). (Variant: \(y''-5y'+6y=0\) gives \(c_1e^{2x}+c_2e^{3x}\).)

WiSe22 A6b: one solution of \(y''-7y'+12y=12x^2+10x\).

Solution

Quadratic right side, so the ansatz is \(y=ax^2+bx+c\), \(y'=2ax+b\), \(y''=2a\): \[2a-7(2ax+b)+12(ax^2+bx+c)=12x^2+10x.\] Compare coefficients: \(x^2\): \(12a=12\); \(x\): \(-14a+12b=10\); \(1\): \(2a-7b+12c=0\). So \(a=1\), \(b=2\), \(c=1\): \(y=x^2+2x+1=(x+1)^2\). (Variant \(y''-5y'+6y=12x^2+4x+2\) gives \(2x^2+4x+3\).) The task asks for a solution; adding \(c_1e^{3x}+c_2e^{4x}\) gives all of them.

What you must know for 12.4: recognise homogeneous and inhomogeneous linear equations of 2nd order; find the solution space of the homogeneous equation; find a particular solution of the inhomogeneous equation when the right-hand side is a polynomial.

12.5General theory: existence and uniqueness

Satz 12.17 · Picard–LindelöfSkript 12.17

Let \(\Omega=(\underline x,\overline x)\times\Omega_0\), \(f:\Omega\to\mathbb R\) continuous with \(\frac{\partial f}{\partial y}\) continuous, \(x_0\in(\underline x,\overline x)\), \(y_0\in\Omega_0\). Then the IVP \(y'=f(x,y)\), \(y(x_0)=y_0\) has a solution continued in both directions (\(x<x_0\) and \(x>x_0\)), and it is unique.

Why the assumption on \(\partial f/\partial y\) matters Extra: \(y'=2\sqrt{|y|}\), \(y(0)=0\) has the two solutions \(y\equiv0\) and \(y=x^2\) (for \(x\ge0\)). Here \(f=2\sqrt{|y|}\) is continuous, but \(\partial f/\partial y\) does not exist at \(y=0\), so uniqueness fails. Geometrically, solution curves in the slope field cannot cross under the assumptions of the theorem, but here they can.

The theorem does not say that the solution lives on the whole interval. There are three possibilities:

behaviourmeaningexample
existence to the end [Existenz bis zum Schluss]solution exists on all of \((\underline x,\overline x)\)every linear equation \(y'=a(x)y+b(x)\): global solution on \(\mathbb R\)
blow-up\(y(x)\to\pm\infty\) as \(x\to x_+<\overline x\)12.18: \(y'=y^2\), \(y(0)=1\): \(y=\frac1{1-x}\), \(y\to\infty\) as \(x\uparrow1\)
premature collapse [vorzeitiger Kollaps]\(y(x)\) reaches the boundary of \(\Omega_0\) at \(x_+<\overline x\)12.19: \(y'=-\frac1{\sqrt y}\), \(y>0\), \(y(0)=1\): \(y=(1-\frac32x)^{2/3}\to0\) as \(x\to\frac23\)
The two computationsSkript 12.18, 12.19
  • 12.18: \(f(x,y)=y^2\) and \(\partial_yf=2y\) are continuous, so Picard–Lindelöf applies near 0. Separate: \(x=\int_0^x\frac{y'}{y^2}=\big[-\frac1y\big]_0^x=1-\frac1{y(x)}\), so \(y=\frac1{1-x}\) on \((-\infty,1)\). The right-hand side \(y^2\) is defined everywhere, but it grows so fast that the solution runs off to infinity in finite time.
  • 12.19: \(\sqrt y\,y'=-1\), so \(\int_1^{y(x)}\sqrt s\,ds=-x\), \(\frac23(y^{3/2}-1)=-x\), \(y=(1-\frac32x)^{2/3}\). At \(x=\frac23\) the solution hits \(y=0\), where the equation is no longer defined.

Both are among the equations in the slope-field widget in 12.1.

This section is marked as partly omitted [ausgelassen] in the script. Know the statement of Picard–Lindelöf and the three possible behaviours with their examples.

ΣSummary sheet

  1. Identify the type (table in section 0). Try in this order: \(y'=g(x)\)? separable \(f(x)g(y)\)? linear \(y'+ay=f\)? 2nd order with constant coefficients?
  2. Separable: \(\int\frac{dy}{g(y)}=\int f\,dx+c\) → solve for \(y\) → constant from the IVP → domain. Look for hidden products such as \(e^{2x-y}=e^{2x}e^{-y}\), or substitute \(z=x+y\).
  3. Linear 1st order: \(y_h=ce^{-A}\), ansatz \(c(x)e^{-A}\) → \(c'=fe^{A}\) → \(y=e^{-A}(c+\int fe^A)\).
  4. Linear 2nd order, constant coefficients: \(\lambda^2+a\lambda+b=0\); \(D>0\): \(e^{\lambda_1x},e^{\lambda_2x}\); \(D=0\): \(e^{\lambda x},xe^{\lambda x}\); \(D<0\): \(e^{-\frac a2x}\cos\omega x,\ e^{-\frac a2x}\sin\omega x\).
  5. Inhomogeneous: ansatz of the same type as \(f\) (polynomial of the same degree; \(b_1\cos+b_2\sin\) with the same frequency), compare coefficients. Result: \(y_i+y_h\).
  6. Theory: Picard–Lindelöf (\(f\), \(\partial_yf\) continuous ⇒ unique local solution); blow-up (\(y'=y^2\)); collapse (\(y'=-1/\sqrt y\)).
  7. Always check the solution by inserting it.

!What the exam asks from Chapter 12

Differential equations appear in four of the nine exams (WiSe19, WiSe22, SoSe23, WiSe24), always as a computation of 3–8 points; the \(f'=cf\) uniqueness argument appears once more as SoSe19 A4. Nothing from §12.5 (Picard–Lindelöf, blow-up) has been asked so far, and the script gives no "minimal contents" for it; knowing the statement and the examples is enough.

TypeExam tasksWhat earns the points
Separable IVPWiSe24 4b, SoSe23 A7, Blatt 14.1aseparate correctly, integrate (often a substitution), constant from \(y(x_0)\), solve for \(y\)
Maximal domainSoSe23 A7where the formula is defined, the interval around \(x_0\), with a reason
Linear 1st order, variation of constantsWiSe19 A5b, Skript 12.11ansatz \(c(x)e^{-A}\), the cancellation, \(c'=fe^{A}\), integrate, "+ homogeneous"
2nd order homogeneousWiSe19 A5a, WiSe22 A6aansatz \(e^{\lambda x}\), characteristic polynomial, correct case, both constants
Particular solution, polynomial right sideWiSe22 A6b, Aufgabe 13.1ansatz of the same degree, comparison of coefficients, clean linear system
\(f'=cf\Rightarrow f=be^{cx}\)SoSe19 A4, Blatt 10.4differentiate \(fe^{-cx}\), derivative 0 ⇒ constant (mean value theorem)
Where points are lost
  • Forgetting "+ all homogeneous solutions" when the task asks for all solutions (WiSe19 A5b), or adding them when it asks for one (WiSe22 A6b — harmless, but read the question).
  • Sign errors in \(e^{-A}\) (the homogeneous solution of \(y'+ay=0\) is \(ce^{-A}\), with a minus).
  • Dropping the constant of integration and then fitting the initial value to nothing.
  • Giving a domain that is not an interval around \(x_0\), or not giving one when asked.
  • Skipping the check. Inserting the solution takes a minute and finds almost every mistake.

Drill: recognise, solve, check

What you must be able to do after Chapter 12
  • Recognise the type of a given equation in seconds (table in section 0) — this decides the method.
  • Solve separable IVPs including the maximal domain; linear first-order equations by variation of constants; second-order equations with constant coefficients in all three cases, with a polynomial, exponential or cos/sin right-hand side.
  • Explain why general solution = particular + homogeneous (Lemma 12.6), and state Picard–Lindelöf with the blow-up and collapse examples.
  • Across the course: this chapter uses almost everything before it — integration (Ch. 8), the chain rule (Ch. 6), exp/ln/trig (Ch. 5), complex numbers (Ch. 1). Weak spots there show up here first; the drill above is a good final check.
